Vaṁso visālova yathā visatto, Puttesu dāresu ca yā apekkhā; Vaṁsakkaḷīrova asajjamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.

\(5\) As a deer unbound in the forest, goes off to graze wherever it wants; so a wise person, looking out for freedom, should live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Migo araññamhi yathā abaddho, Yenicchakaṁ gacchati gocarāya; Viññū naro seritaṁ pekkhamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(6\) There is constant interaction living with companions, whether in resting, standing, going, or traveling. Seeing \[the value of\] not-craved (un-coveted) independence \[or freedom\], Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Āmantanā hoti sahāyamajjhe, Vāse ṭhāne gamane cārikāya; Anabhijjhitaṁ seritaṁ pekkhamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(7\) There is amusement and pleasure living with companions, and abundant loving affection for one's sons (children). Being disenchanted with the separation from the beloved, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Khiḍḍā ratī hoti sahāyamajjhe, Puttesu ca vipulaṁ hoti pemaṁ; Piyavippayogaṁ vijigucchamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(8\) Not bound to a single home or territory, and is without resentment with the environment, being content with whatever there is; enduring hardships, unwavering, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Cātuddiso appaṭigho ca hoti, Santussamāno itarītarena; Parissayānaṁ sahitā achambhī, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(9\) Even some renunciants are hard to please; so, too, are laypeople living at home. Uninterested for others' sons (other men, renunciants or laypeople), Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Dussaṅgahā pabbajitāpi eke, Atho gahaṭṭhā gharamāvasantā; Appossukko paraputtesu hutvā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(10\) Having renounced layperson's life, like an Ebony tree with its leaves fallen off; The hero, having severed householder-bonds, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Oropayitvā gihibyañjanāni, Sañchinnapatto yathā koviḷāro; Chetvāna vīro gihibandhanāni, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(11\) If one can find a wise companion, can live together, behaves well, strong and brave; (then ) overcome all troubles (in order) to live with him, joyful and mindful.

*Sace labhetha nipakaṁ sahāyaṁ, Saddhiṁ caraṁ sadhu-vihāri-dhīraṁ; Abhibhuyya sabbāni parissayāni, Careyya tenattamano satīmā.*

\(12\) But if one cannot find a wise companion, who can live together, behaves well, strong and brave; like a king abandons his conquered realm, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*No ce labhetha nipakaṁ sahāyaṁ, Saddhiṁ caraṁ sadhu-vihāri-dhīraṁ; Rājāva raṭṭhaṁ vijitaṁ pahāya, Eko care mātaṅgaraññeva nāgo. *

\(13\) Certainly, we praise the success of companions: one should associate with companions who are superior or equal \[to oneself\]; if cannot find such, \[living\] as one who eats blamelessly, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Addhā pasaṁsāma sahāyasampadaṁ, Seṭṭhā samā sevitabbā sahāyā; Ete aladdhā anavajjabhojī, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(14\) Having seen the shining of gold \[bangles\], well-finished by the goldsmith; Striking against each other, in pairs on the arm, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Disvā suvaṇṇassa pabhassarāni, Kammāraputtena suniṭṭhitāni; Saṅghaṭṭamānāni duve bhujasmiṁ, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(15\) In this way, with a second \[companion\] for me, \[there would be\] talking or entanglement; Seeing this danger for the future, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Evaṁ dutīyena sahā mamassa, Vācābhilāpo abhisajjanā vā; Etaṁ bhayaṁ āyatiṁ pekkhamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(16\) Sensual pleasures are colorful, sweet, delightful, but in diverse forms, they agitate the mental volition. Having seen danger in the strands of sensual pleasure, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Kāmā hi citrā madhurā manoramā, Virūparūpena mathenti cittaṁ; Ādīnavaṁ kāmaguṇesu disvā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(17\) "This is plague, a swelling, misfortune, a disease, a dart, and danger for me": having seen this danger in the strands of sensual pleasure, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Ītī ca gaṇḍo ca upaddavo ca, Rogo ca sallañca bhayañca metaṁ; Etaṁ bhayaṁ kāmaguṇesu disvā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(18\) And cold and heat, hunger and thirst, wind and sun heat, stinging insects and snakes: having endured all these, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Sītañca uṇhañca khudaṁ pipāsaṁ, Vātātape ḍaṁsasarīsape ca; Sabbānipetāni abhisambhavitvā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(19\) Like a Nāga that has left the herds behind, with fully developed body (becomes mature and independent), lotus-marks and magnificent; Living in the forest as he pleases, one should live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Nāgova yūthāni vivajjayitvā, Sañjātakhandho padumī uḷāro; Yathābhirantaṁ viharaṁ araññe, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(20\) It is impossible for one who delights in company, might attain even temporary liberation; Having understood the word of the Kinsman of the Sun (Gautama the Supreme Arahant), Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Aṭṭhānataṁ saṅgaṇikāratassa, Yaṁ phassaye sāmayikaṁ vimuttiṁ; Ādiccabandhussa vaco nisamma, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo*

\(21\) Having transcended the distortions of views, having arrived at the Right Path, having found the Way; I am one in whom direct knowledge has arisen, not to be led by another, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Diṭṭhīvisūkāni upātivatto, Patto niyāmaṁ paṭiladdha-maggo; Uppanna-ñāṇomhi anaññaneyyo, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(22\) Without greed, without deceit, without thirst, without malice, having blown away the stain of **moha** \[ignorance and delusion\]; Having become \[one\] without dependency in the whole world, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Nillolupo nikkuho nippipāso, Nimmakkho niddhantakasāvamoho; Nirāsayo sabbaloke bhavitvā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(23\) One should avoid an unwholesome companion, who sees a different purpose for life (who mistakes worldly "play" (**khiḍḍā**) for true welfare -- the ultimate aim of liberation), one settled in the wrong path. One should not personally associate with \[one\] pursuing zeallessness (a state of spiritual slackness or "letting go" of the meditative pressure); Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Pāpaṁ sahāyaṁ parivajjayetha, Anatthadassiṁ visame niviṭṭhaṁ; Sayaṁ na seve pasutaṁ pamattaṁ, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

Comment: **Appamāda as Foundation of the Deathless (Appamādo amatapada) and the Danger of Pamāda in Spiritual Association**

This verse draws a sharp distinction between two orientations of life: one aligned with disciplined striving toward liberation, and one absorbed in worldly play (khiḍḍā) and misdirected purpose.

The "unwholesome companion" is defined not merely by moral fault, but by **a fundamentally different existential aim**---one who mistakes worldly engagement for true welfare and therefore remains established in a wrong trajectory (visama).

The critical opposition introduced here is between **appamāda (zealousness)** and **pamāda (slackened engagement)**.

- **Pasutaṁ pamattaṁ** refers to one who is *absorbed in a negligent condition of engagement*---a mind no longer carrying sustained intensity toward liberation, but instead drifting in worldly preoccupations and reduced meditative pressure.

- This is not simply "bad conduct," but a **collapse of zealous continuity**, where the force of striving is no longer held.

In contrast, the implied practitioner of the path is defined by **appamāda (zealousness)**---a continuous, intense, and non-negligent engagement with the goal of liberation. This is the inner condition that keeps the path alive: sustained energy, seriousness of orientation, and refusal to lapse into worldly absorption.

The instruction "do not associate with such a one" therefore functions as a warning about **contagion of slackened orientation**. Association with pamāda weakens the intensity of practice, dissolving the continuity required for liberation.

Hence the verse concludes with the image:

"Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn."

This solitude is not social rejection, but symbolic of **protecting the integrity of zealous continuity**. The solitary practitioner preserves appamāda---uninterrupted, intense, self-sustained engagement---without dilution from environments rooted in pamāda.

\(24\) One should associate with the very learned, the Dhamma-bearer, a great friend who has the **Paññā** (wisdom) to explain the path clearly and cut through confusion. Having understood the goals and dispelled doubt, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Bahussutaṁ dhammadharaṁ bhajetha, Mittaṁ uḷāraṁ paṭibhānavantaṁ; Aññāya atthāni vineyya kaṅkhaṁ, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(25\) Having found no satisfaction in the world with amusement, delight, and the happiness of sensory pleasure, not taking any interest in them; Abstaining from the habit of ornamentation and beautifying, a speaker of truth, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Khiḍḍaṁ ratiṁ kāmasukhañca loke, Analaṅkaritvā anapekkhamāno; Vibhūsanaṭṭhānā virato saccavādī, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(26\) Having given up children and spouse, father and mother, wealth, grain ("security" of the household), and relatives; Having abandoned sensory pleasures to their full extent, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Puttañca dāraṁ pitarañca mātaraṁ, Dhanāni dhaññāni ca bandhavāni; Hitvāna kāmāni yathodhikāni, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(27\) This is a Saṅga (group); in it is protection and ease. But more in it is stress (dukkha), offering little satisfaction. This is a hook (fish-hook): the wise ones have known thus; should live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Saṅgo eso parittamettha sokhyaṁ, Appassādo dukkhamettha bhiyyo; Gaḷo eso iti ñatvā matīmā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

Commentary: **The Tension of the Collective**

This verse highlights a fundamental mechanical conflict between the individual and a collective body.

**1). The Illusion of Protection (Paritta)**

A **Saṅgha** (the group) functions as a unit of protection (**Paritta**). The word Paritta comes from the prefix Pari (around/all-over) + the root √tā (to protect/save) or √dā (to cut). Originally, it meant to "cut around" or "limit." If you cut a small piece of cloth out of a large bolt, that piece is *paritta. Paritta *evolved to mean a **shield, a ward, or a protection**. To "protect" something in the ancient world, you "fenced it around." You drew a boundary (**Sīmā**) to keep the outside out. **Paritta chanting** is literally "fencing the mind/area with sound" to create a safe zone. For the practitioner seeking the **Nibbāna-Dhātu**, the social safety of the herd (**Saṅgha/ Saṅga**) is a low-value asset. It is the "bait" on the **Gaḷo** (the hook).

**2). The Asymmetry of Stress (Dukkha)**

"Politics" arise the moment a group forms. In the **Vinaya**, **4 bhikkhus** is the legal threshold. Once you reach 4, you are a corporate **Saṅgha**. You must have consensus, attend meetings, and manage communal karma. This is the **bhiyyo dukkha** (greater stress). The "gratification" of having companions is **Appassādo** (small) compared to the mental "churning" required to manage the politics of the collective.

According to the **Mahāvagga**, a group of **4 bhikkhus** is the minimum quorum required to be legally defined as a **Saṅgha**. 1 Bhikkhu (Ekaka): no collective acts. He performs a "determination" (adhiṭṭhāna) for the Uposatha alone. **2-3 Bhikkhus (Gaṇa):** A "group." They perform the Uposatha by way of mutual "purity" (**pārisuddhi**), where they declare their purity to each other. They cannot perform formal Sangha-kamma (legal acts). **4 Bhikkhus:** The **Threshold**. At 4, they are a **Saṅgha**. They can perform most formal acts, including the full recitation of the **Pātimokkha**. **5 Bhikkhus:** This is the quorum for certain specific high-level acts, such as a full ordination (**Upasampadā**) in "outlying regions" where monks are scarce. **10 Bhikkhus:** The standard quorum for ordination in central regions. **20 Bhikkhus:** The quorum for the **Abbhāna** (rehabilitation) of a monk who has committed a *Saṅghādisesa* offense.

**3). The "Sub-4" Strategy**

From the Sword-Horn (Rhinoceros Horn) life point of view, a group of **2 or 3** (a **Gaṇa**) or remaining solitary (**Ekaka**) is the superior option for **Concentration (Samadhi)**. It avoids the administrative "viscosity" and organizational "hooks" that characterize the formal **Saṅgha**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

\(28\) Having shattered the fetters, like a fish breaking through a net in the water; Like a fire that does not return to what it has already burnt; live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Sandālayitvāna saṁyojanāni, Jālaṁva bhetvā salilambucārī; Aggīva daḍḍhaṁ anivattamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(29\) With eyes downcast, not restless of foot, with senses guarded and the mind well-protected; Not leaking \[desire\], not burning \[consumed by the fever of passions\]; Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Okkhittacakkhu na ca pādalolo, Guttindriyo rakkhitamānasāno; Anavassuto apariḍayhamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(30\) Having discarded the outward signs of a layman, just like a coral tree that has shed its leaves; Wearing the saffron-dyed robes, having renounced the world, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Ohārayitvā gihibyañjanāni, Sañchannapatto yathā pārichatto; Kāsāyavattho abhinikkhamitvā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(31\) Not arousing attachment for sensual delights, not wanting for more, independent (not supported by others); walking from house to house in order for alms (without skipping houses); A mind that is not bound, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Rasesu gedhaṁ akaraṁ alolo, Anaññaposī sapadānacārī; Kule kule appaṭibaddha-citto, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(32\) Having abandoned the five hindrances of the mind, having dispelled all mental defilements; independent from the world, having cut off affection and anger, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Pahāya pañcāvaraṇāni cetaso, Upakkilese byapanujja sabbe; **Anissito** chetva sineha-dosaṁ, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(33\) Having left behind pleasure and stress of former life; having gained equanimity over joy and grief, Pure and concentrated, one live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Vipiṭṭhikatvāna sukhaṁ dukhañca, Pubbeva ca; somanassa-domanassaṁ Laddhān-upekkhaṁ, **samathaṁ visuddhaṁ**, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(34\) Having aroused energy for the goal to reach the other shore (Para, Nibbana-Dhatu), A mind not shrinking and not lazy in conduct; firmly to leave (the world), endowed with stamina and strength, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Āraddha-viriyo param-attha-pattiyā, Alīnacitto akusītavutti; Daḷha-nikkamo thāma-balūpapanno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(35\) Not neglecting seclusion and jhāna, always in the Dhammas, Living in accordance with the Dhamma; Contemplating the danger (drawback) in all realms of existence, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Paṭisallānaṁ jhānam-ariñcamāno, Dhammesu niccaṁ anudhammacārī; Ādīnavaṁ sammasitā bhavesu, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(36\)  Zealous for seeking the destruction of worldly craving, mentally sharp and clear, having deep knowledge, mindful; One who has thoroughly discerned the Dhamma, resolute for the Destined \[Nibbāna\], Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Taṇhakkhayaṁ patthayam-appamatto, Aneḷamūgo sutavā satīmā; Saṅkhāta-dhammo niyato padhānavā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(37\) Like a lion not trembling in \[midst of\] sounds, like the wind not caught in a net; like a lotus not stained by water, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Sīhova saddesu asantasanto, Vātova jālamhi asajjamāno; Padumaṁva toyena alippamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(38\) Just like a lion, having mastered the strength of its fangs, is king of animals; Having surpassed all modes of existence (cārī), one should dwell in remote lodgings, one should live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Sīho yathā dāṭhabalī pasayha, Rājā migānaṁ; abhibhuyya cārī, Sevetha pantāni senāsanāni, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(39\) Cultivating kindness, transcendental equanimity, compassion, and liberation, and brahmanic joy at the proper time; make peace with the entire world, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Mettaṁ upekkhaṁ karuṇaṁ vimuttiṁ Āsevamāno, muditañca kāle; Sabbena lokena avirujjhamāno, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(40\) Having abandoned raga (worldly cravings), dosa (survival instincts of fear and anger), and moha (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the liberation path), having shattered the fetters; not troubled at the end of the worldly life, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Rāgañca dosañca pahāya mohaṁ, Sandālayitvāna saṁyojanāni; Asantasaṁ jīvitasaṅkhayamhi, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

\(41\) They follow you and serve you for a motive; friends without motive are rare nowadays; humans are impure, only wise about their own benefit, Let him live alone like a rhinoceros horn.

*Bhajanti sevanti ca kāraṇatthā, Nikkāraṇā dullabhā ajja mittā; Attaṭṭha-paññā asucī manussā, Eko care khaggavisāṇakappo.*

### Tissa Metteyya Sutta (Snp 4.7): A Dialogue on Sexual Conduct

This sutta is a tightly structured diagnostic discourse on **methuna** (sexual intercourse) as a decisive obstruction to liberation. The inquiry begins with a sincere question from Tissa Metteyya, and the Blessed One (Gautama the Supreme Arahant) responds with a **systematic exposure of causal degeneration**---how indulgence in sexual activity dismantles the architecture of the liberation path from within.

The central movement of the sutta is clear:\
**from solitude (viveka) → to entanglement (gedha) → to distortion (micchā-paṭipatti) → to downfall (hīna state).**

**1. The Question: From Hearing to Training**

*"Sutvāna tava sāsanaṁ, Viveke sikkhissāmase"*\
"Having heard your teaching, we shall train in solitude."

The initial orientation is correct:

- **Sāsana (teaching)** → properly received

- **Viveka (seclusion)** → correctly identified as the training ground

The request is for **mechanism**. What exactly goes wrong when one engages in sexual intercourse?

**2. The First Breakdown: Loss of the Teaching**

The Blessed One answers directly: *"Mussate vāpi sāsanaṁ"* --- "The teaching is forgotten."

This is **functional loss of alignment**. Sexual indulgence produces:

- Cognitive distraction

- Emotional agitation

- Reprioritization of desire

Thus:

- The Dhamma is no longer central

- The citta reorients toward gratification

Immediately follows: *"Micchā ca paṭipajjati"* --- "He practices wrongly." This means wrong practice is not ignorance alone, it is **misaligned execution due to desire-pressure**

This is why it is called: *"Anāriyaṁ"* --- ignoble, not aligned with the Noble Ones.

**3. Structural Regression: From Solitary to Worldly**

*"Eko pubbe caritvāna..."* --- "Having formerly lived alone..."

This indicates regression: the practitioner had already established **ekacariya (solitary conduct)**, yet returns to sensual engagement.

The simile: *"Yānaṁ bhantaṁva"* --- "like a vehicle gone off track"

This is precise:

- The path is a **trajectory**

- Sexual indulgence causes **derailment**, not just delay

The result: *"Hīnamāhu puthujjanaṁ"* --- "He is called a low, common-born," **structural fall back into saṃsāric conditioning**.

**4. Dissolution of Attainment: Loss of Yasa and Kitti**

*"Yaso kitti ca... hāyate"* --- "Fame and reputation decline"

This refers to both **Spiritual dignity (yasa)** and **Recognized integrity (kitti)** .

These qualities arise from **internal coherence**. Sexual indulgence fractures that coherence. Thus, what was stable begins to erode.

**5. Psychological Consequence: Agitation and Shame**

*"Saṅkappehi pareto"* --- "Overwhelmed by (sexual) thoughts"

The citta becomes:

- Proliferative (saṅkappa-driven)

- Obsessed

- Internally noisy

Then: *"Kapaṇo viya jhāyati"* --- "He broods like a miserable man"

This is **after-effect suffering**:

- Restlessness

- Regret

- Dependency

Further: *"Sutvā paresaṁ nigghosaṁ, maṅku hoti"* "Hearing criticism, he becomes dejected"

Why? Because the citta **knows its own deviation**.

**6. Escalation: From Desire to Conflict and Falsehood**

*"Atha satthāni kurute"* --- "Then he prepares weapons"

This is a strong statement. It shows escalation:

1.  Desire →

2.  Defensiveness →

3.  Aggression

Then: *"Paravādehi codito"* --- "provoked by disputes"

Sexual entanglement generates:

- Jealousy

- Competition

- Argumentation

Finally: *"Mosavajjaṁ pagāhati"* --- "He plunges into false speech"

This is the ethical collapse:

- To protect desire → one distorts truth

- To sustain attachment → one abandons integrity

Thus, sensuality is linked directly to **moral degradation**.

**7. The Illusion of Wisdom**

*"Paṇḍitoti samaññāto..."* --- "Though known as a wise one..."

This is a critical warning: Social recognition does not equal realization.

Even one established in: *"Ekacariyaṁ adhiṭṭhito"* --- solitary discipline

If bound again: *"Methune yutto"* --- "yoked to sexual intercourse"

Then: *"Mandova parikissati"* --- "he suffers like a fool"

Thus: wisdom without restraint is unstable, Insight without discipline collapses.

**8. The Strategic Response: Firm Establishment in Solitude**

*"Ekacariyaṁ daḷhaṁ kayirā"* --- "Make solitary conduct firm"

This is **structural necessity**. And explicitly:

*"Na nisevetha methunaṁ"* --- "Do not engage in sexual intercourse"

The instruction is absolute because the mechanism is absolute: Engagement → entanglement → downfall

**9. Viveka as the Highest Training**

*"Vivekaññeva sikkhetha, etaṁ ariyānamuttamaṁ"*\
"Train specifically in seclusion/ solitude---this is the highest of the Noble Ones"

Viveka here includes:

- Physical solitude

- Sensory restraint

- Mental non-entanglement

But the sutta adds a critical correction: *"Na tena seṭṭho maññetha"* --- "Do not think oneself superior"

Thus: seclusion is not for pride. But:

*"Sa ve nibbānasantike"* --- "He is near Nibbāna"

**10. Ritta: The Sexual Desire Emptied**

The final verse introduces a profound term:

*"Rittassa munino"* --- "the sage who is empty (of sexual desire)"

The training from seclusion / solitude is **purification through removal**.

What is removed?

- Sexual craving

- Sexual expectation (*kāmesu anapekkhino*)

- Psychological dependency

The result is a **body without content of sexual desire**.

**11. Inversion of Desire: The World's Response**

*"Oghatiṇṇassa pihayanti"* --- "They long for the one who has crossed the flood"

Those immersed in sensuality desire the one who has renounced it. Why? Because:

- They perceive freedom

- But cannot yet enact it

Thus, the liberated one becomes an **object of aspiration**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**The Structural Role of Sexual Renunciation**

This sutta does not present sexual restraint as moral conservatism. It presents it as **a non-negotiable condition for liberation**.

**The chain is explicit:**

- Sexual indulgence → mental proliferation

- Proliferation → loss of Dhamma

- Loss of Dhamma → wrong practice

- Wrong practice → ethical collapse

- Ethical collapse → full regression into worldliness

**The counter-path is equally clear:**

- Seclusion (viveka)

- Solitary Life (ekacariya)

- Completely Abandon Sexual Intercourse (methuna-vippahāna)

- Sexual Desire Emptied (ritta)

- Nearness to Nibbāna

Sexual intercourse is identified as a **central binding force that reattaches the citta to the cycle of becoming**. To become **ritta**---only an sexual desire emptied one can hold the Deathless.

#### SNP 4.7: Tissa Metteyya Sutta 

**Tissa Metteyya** is a name, specifically belonging to one of the sixteen **māṇavas** (thinkers) from the *Māṇava-pucchā (Snp 5.1)*.

- **Tissa**: This is his **given name** (personal name). In ancient India, Tissa was a common name, often associated with the auspicious **Tiṣya** (Pusya) lunar mansion (nakshatra).

- **Metteyya**: This is his **gotta** (clan or family name).

**Metteyya the Gotra Analysis:** The name **Metteyya** (Pāli) corresponds to the Sanskrit **Maitreya**. This is a patronymic name, meaning \"a descendant of **Mitra**.\"

- **Mitra (The Sun/The Friend):** In the Vedas, Mitra is a primary solar deity associated with **loyalty, friendship, and the sun**. He is the \"all-seeing\" eye of the world.

- **The Lineage of the \"Friendly\" or \"Solar\" Clan:** By being a Metteyya, Tissa was identified as belonging to a high-status Brahmana family.

It is critical to distinguish this historical **Tissa Metteyya** (the seeker in the *Sutta Nipāta*) from the later concept of **Metteyya Bodhisattva** (the future Buddha).

- **The Historical Seeker:** Tissa Metteyya was a contemporary of Gautama the Supreme Arahant. His question focused on the \"distress\" (**daratha**) caused by sensory engagement. He was a **māṇava** (thinker) trying to break out of the **System**.

- **The Future Buddha:** The name \"Metteyya\" was later applied to a figure during the Bodhisattva movement invented as the future Buddha symbolizing a return to the \"Mitra\" (wholesome/ friendly) quality of the Citta.

**Linguistic Bridge between Mitra, Messiah, and Metteyya**

In **Comparative Religion** and **Theosophy**, there is linguistic bridge for Mitra, Messiah, and Metteyya. The most direct \"link\" found by scholars is the etymological root of the name:

- **Mitra (Vedic/Sanskrit):** Metteyya (Maitreya) comes from the root **Mitra**, the solar deity of \"friendship\" and \"covenants.\"

- **Mashiach (Hebrew):** The Jewish word for **Messiah** means \"the anointed one.\"

- **Maitreya (Sanskrit):** Some researchers suggest that the \"M-T-R\" root in Indo-Iranian languages influenced the conceptual development of a \"Future Savior\" figure across the Middle East.

**The Dental-to-Sibilant Shift: T/Th → S**

In the transition from Indo-European roots to various daughter languages, the **dental** sounds (**t, th, d**) often shift into **sibilants** (**s, sh, z**).

- **Sanskrit/Pāli (T/Th):** In the name **Metteyya** (Pāli) or **Maitreya** (Sanskrit), the core of the word is **Mitra** (the Sun/Friend). The dental \"t\" is crisp and localized at the teeth.

- **Avestan/Persian (Th/S):** As the concept moved West into Persia (Zoroastrianism), **Mitra** became **Mithra**. Here, the \"t\" softened into a \"th\" sound.

- **Hebrew/Semitic (S/Sh):** In Semitic languages, similar concepts of \"anointing\" or \"measuring\" often utilize the **s/sh** sounds. The Hebrew **Mashiach** (Messiah) uses the **Shin (sh)**.

Scholars suggest that as the Persian **Mithra** (the covenant-keeper/savior) influenced the surrounding regions, the phonetic \"th\" was adapted by Hebrew speakers into the sibilant **s/sh** to fit their phonetic system. Thus, the \"Anointed Savior\" (Messiah) and the \"Solar Friend\" (Maitreya) share a structural and phonetic lineage.

**The Structural \"Dual-Redemption\"**

Comparative scholars note that **Metteyya**, the **Jewish Messiah**, and the **Returning Christ** share the same mechanical \"End Times\" function:

- **The Descent:** All three are prophesied to arrive when the world has reached a state of extreme moral decay and darkness.

- **The Restoration:** They serve as the \"Great Reformer.\" Metteyya is said to restore the **Brahmacariya** and the path to liberation, just as the Messiah/Christ restores the kingdom of righteousness.

- **The Life Span:** In the *Cakkavatti Sutta*, it is said that when Metteyya arrives, human life span will have increased due to a return to virtue---aligning with the \"Eternal Kingdom\" motifs in Abrahamic faiths.

**The Path: Mashiach → Christos → Christ**

When the Hebrew Bible was translated into Greek (the Septuagint) around the 3rd century BCE, the translators needed a Greek equivalent for *Messiah /Mashiach.* They chose the Greek verb **chriō**, for Mashiach's assumed meaning of anointed, which also means \"to anoint.\" Thus, **Christos** (Χριστός) became the direct literal translation. It is not a name; it is a **title** meaning \"The Anointed.\"

- **Messiah:** This is the English phonetic transliteration of the Hebrew *Mashiach*.

- **Christ:** This is the English phonetic transliteration of the Greek *Christos*.

- **The Synthesis:** By the time of the New Testament, \"Jesus the Christ\" was used to identify him as the \"Mashiach\" prophesied in Hebrew scripture.

**The Shift: From Common to Exclusive**

In ancient Greek, **Christos** (Χριστός) was originally a very practical, mechanical word before it became a \"spiritual\" or \"transcendental\" title. Understanding its evolution requires stripping away the modern religious \"glitter\" and looking at the **engineering of the term**.

**1). The Common Meaning: \"To be** anointed **\"**

Before it was a title, *christos* was a simple verbal adjective derived from the verb **chriō** (χρίω).

- **Literal Meaning:** \"anointed.\"

- **Common Use:** It was used to describe anything that had a substance applied to its surface. If you put whitewash on a wall, that wall was *christos*. If you applied medicinal salve to a wound, that salve was a *chrisma*.

- **The \"** anointing**\":** this is the literal **Abhilepana**. It is the \"coating\" applied to an object.

**2). The Special Meaning: Ritual Anointing**

Because the Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible (the Septuagint) used *Christos* to translate the Hebrew **Mashiach** (Messiah), the word underwent a massive \"upgrade\":

- It moved from being a common description (\"anointed\") to a unique, singular title (\"The Anointed One\").

- It became a \"transcendental\" word because it implied the arrival of a being who would \"reset\" the laws of the world.

In a social and \"spiritual\" context, the word became \"special\" when it was applied to people. To be \"anointed\" meant a person had been anointed with sacred oil (usually olive oil mixed with spices).

The ritual use of oil---whether as a \"sacred anointing\" (**Abhilepana**) on the face or a full-body anointing---is a universal in human ritual. In anthropology, oil is viewed as a **conductor of power** and a **marker of boundary**.

**1). The Anthropological Lens: The \"Fluid of Transition\"**

Scholars such as **Arnold van Gennep** (in *The Rites of Passage*) and **Victor Turner** analyzed oil as a tool for \"Liminality\" (the state of being between two worlds).

- **The Barrier and the Bridge:** Oil is unique because it is a liquid that stays on the surface. Anthropologically, putting oil on the face marks the individual as \"anointed\" (**Christos**) or \"set apart.\" It creates a physical boundary between the \"mundane\" person and the \"ritual\" persona.

- **The Translucency of Power:** Unlike thick mud or paint, oil often makes the skin shine. In shamanic cultures, this \"shine\" is seen as the **manifestation of the spirit** or the \"Inner Sun\" (aligning with the **Mitra** or solar-savior concept).

**2). Shamanic Empowerment through the \"** anointment**\"**

In tribal and shamanic contexts, oil is rarely just oil; it is a \"carrier wave\" for intent.

- **Extraction of Essence:** Shamans often use oils infused with psychoactive or medicinal plants. The oil acts as the **abhilepanaṁ** (the anointing) that allows the power of the plant to penetrate the \"skin\" of the practitioner.

- **The Face as the Interface:** The face is the primary site of identity. By anointing of oil on the face, the shaman is mechanically \"deleting\" their common identity and replacing it with the certain deity identity or the ancestral spirit.

- **Protection:** In many cultures, ritual oil is believed to allowing protection from evil spirits in the spirit world.

**3). Scholarly Perspectives on \"Sacred Grease\"**

- **Theodore Gaster:** In his studies of ancient Near Eastern rituals, Gaster noted that anointing was a way of \"investing\" a person with a permanent quality. The oil doesn\'t just sit there; it is absorbed, symbolizing that the \"higher standard\" has become part of the person's essence.

- **Catherine Bell:** A prominent ritual theorist who argued that \"ritualization\" (like anointing oil) is a way of \"bodying forth\" power. The act of anointment creates a \"ritualized body\" that is distinct from the \"common body.\"

1\. "For one indulges in (**Anuyuttassa**, actively yoked to) sexual intercourse (**Methuna**), (said the Venerable Bhikkhu Tissa Metteyya) Tell me (Brūhi) the distress (**vighāta**), O noble friend (**Mārisa**); Having heard your teaching, we shall train in solitude/ seclusion (viveke)."

"Methunam-anuyuttassa, *(icc-*ā*yasmā tisso metteyyo)* Vighātaṁ brūhi mārisa; Sutvāna tava sāsanaṁ, Viveke sikkhissāmase".

**Mārisa** is a respectful, yet somewhat familiar or collegial term of address used among equals or by those of high status toward someone they recognize as having great dignity. It is a polite address among Brahmanas.

2\. "For one indulges in sexual intercourse, (Metteyya, said the Blessed One) even the teaching is forgotten/lost; And he practices wrongly, This is what is ignoble in him."

"Methunamanuyuttassa, *(metteyyāti bhagavā)* Mussate vāpi sāsanaṁ; Micchā ca paṭipajjati, Etaṁ tasmiṁ anāriyaṁ.

3\. "Having formerly lived alone, he who then indulges in sexual intercourse; Is like a carriage that has strayed off the track in the world, they call that person a low, common-born (puthujjana)."

Eko pubbe caritvāna, methunaṁ yo nisevati; Yānaṁ bhantaṁva taṁ loke, hīnamāhu puthujjanaṁ.

4\. "Whatever fame and reputation that were his before, these fade away from him; Having seen this, one should train to completely abandon sexual intercourse."

Yaso kitti ca yā pubbe, hāyate vāpi tassa sā; Etampi disvā sikkhetha, methunaṁ vippahātave.

5\. "Overwhelmed by his (sexual) thoughts, he broods like a miserable man; Hearing the blame of others, he becomes troubled ---such is the way."

Saṅkappehi pareto so, kapaṇo viya jhāyati; Sutvā paresaṁ nigghosaṁ, maṅku hoti tathāvidho.

6\. "Then he prepares weapons, being incited by the arguments of others; This, indeed, is his great entanglement--- He plunges deep into false speech."

Atha satthāni kurute, paravādehi codito; Esa khvassa mahāgedho, mosavajjaṁ pagāhati.

7\. "Though known by the reputation of a \'Wise One,\' Having committed to the solitary life; Yet, being yoked / bound to sexual intercourse, He suffers like a fool."

Paṇḍitoti samaññāto, ekacariyaṁ adhiṭṭhito; Athāpi methune yutto, mandova parikissati.

8\. "Having known this danger, here the Sage, in the past and the future; Should make the solitary conduct firm, one should not associate with sexual intercourse."

Etamādīnavaṁ ñatvā, Muni pubbāpare idha; Ekacariyaṁ daḷhaṁ kayirā, Na nisevetha methunaṁ.

9\. "One should train specifically in solitude/seclusion; This is the highest \[practice\] of the Noble Ones (**ariyānam-uttama**); Not by that should one think themselves superior, He indeed is near Nibbāna (nibbana-santike)."

Vivekaññeva sikkhetha, etaṁ ariyānamuttamaṁ; Na tena seṭṭho maññetha, sa ve nibbānasantike.

10\. "For the Sage who lives without (sexual act), Without expectation to sexual pleasures (Kāmesu); Those people still bound yearn for him, who has crossed over the flood (Oghatiṇṇassa)."

Rittassa munino carato, Kāmesu anapekkhino; Oghatiṇṇassa pihayanti, Kāmesu gadhitā pajā"ti. 

Comment: **Ritta** is the Pāli form of the Sanskrit **Rikta** (from the root **ric**, meaning \"to empty,\" \"to leave,\" \"to release,\" or \"to purge\"). In Sanskrit, *Rikta* describes a vessel that has been drained of its contents. It implies the absence of something that was previously there.

## Pillar II: The Mechanics of Purification (Internal Alchemy)

**1. The Nature of Purification**

Pillar II addresses the most critical dimension of the path: **purification as an active, structural process**, as a precise inner operation---an **internal alchemy**---through which the practitioner dismantles the sustaining mechanisms of worldly existence.

Purification does not mean becoming "better within the world." It means **ending the processes that construct the worldly existence as an experienced reality**. The worldly existence, as lived and known, is not an independent entity; it is a continuously generated system dependent on specific external inputs, conditions, and reinforcing loops. As long as these conditions remain active, the worldly existence persists. When they are fully understood and no longer sustained, the system ceases.

The three suttas chosen here form a coherent architecture of this process. The **Puttamaṃsa Sutta (SN 12.63)** defines the *fuel system*---the four nutriments that sustain existence. The **Ajita-māṇava-pucchā (Snp 5.1)** analyzes the *structure of the system itself*---how bondage operates and how it can be brought to cessation. The **Uraga Sutta (Snp 1.1)** presents the *final act of completion*---the total shedding of the world, like a serpent abandoning its worn-out, old skin. Together, they reveal purification as a **disengagement from the universe of conditioned existence**.

**2. The Worldly Existence as a Sustained System: Nāma--Rūpa and Its Dependency**

The starting point of purification lies in understanding that what is commonly called "life" is in fact a **dependent system of nāma--rūpa**.

Rūpa constitutes the structural base---the embodied platform governed by physical processes and sensory capacities. Nāma comprises the mental-operational layer---feeling (vedanā), perception, intention (cetanā), and attention. These form a **dynamic processing system**, constantly updating, interpreting, and reconstituting experience.

This system appears continuous and stable. In reality, it persists only because it is continuously **fed**.

The Puttamaṃsa Sutta identifies the four nutriments (āhāra) that sustain this system. These nutriments describe a functional architecture: Physical food maintains the biological structure. Contact (phassa) generates experiential tone within the field of feeling. Mental volition (manosañcetanā) constructs identity, continuity, and becoming. Worldly consciousness (viññāṇa) sustains the entire field of nāma--rūpa, allowing it to manifest as a coherent experiential world.

This forms a **vertical dependency structure**. Each layer relies on the previous, yet all are interdependent in sustaining the total system. Worldly existence, therefore, is not self-existing. It is **nutriment-dependent**. This insight is decisive: **what is sustained can be unsustained.**

**3. The Generation of Bondage: From Contact to Identity**

The Ajita-māṇava-pucchā provides a penetrating diagnosis of how beings remain bound within this system. Bondage does not arise randomly; it follows a precise operational sequence.

At the interface between the organism and the world, the five cords of sensuality---forms, sounds, odors, flavors, and tangibles---enter through the sense faculties. When sense base, object, and attention converge, **contact (phassa)** arises. This contact gives rise immediately to **feeling (vedanā)**: pleasant, painful, or neutral.

Feeling functions as a **triggering signal**. Pleasant feeling inclines toward grasping; painful feeling inclines toward rejection. In this way, feeling conditions **craving (taṇhā)**. Craving unfolds in three modes. Kāma-taṇhā seeks satisfaction in sensory pleasure of Kāma realm. Bhava-taṇhā seeks continuity, identity, and existence. Vibhava-taṇhā seeks escape through negation or annihilation. These are **operational drives** embedded in the system.

From craving arises **mental volition**---the active structuring of experience. This is the domain where identity is constructed. Through intention, interpretation, and repetition, the sense of "I" and "mine" is continuously generated. This process does not merely accompany experience; it **creates the continuity of worldly existence as a lived narrative**.

Worldly consciousness (viññāṇa) then sustains this entire configuration. It is not an independent entity, but a **dependent illumination process**. It arises with conditions and, in turn, maintains the visibility and coherence of nāma--rūpa. Thus, bondage is **procedural**. It is the result of an ongoing cycle of contact, feeling, craving, volition, and consciousness, each feeding and reinforcing the other.

**4. Internal Alchemy: The Purification Process**

Purification begins when this cycle is **seen directly,** anupassanā---higher seeing---through which each layer of human existence is penetrated and understood as conditioned, unstable, and not true self. The process unfolds as a **progressive disengagement** from the nutriment structure.

At the level of the body, the practitioner sees clearly that the physical form is sustained by food, subject to decay, and not the true self. This loosens identification with the physical body (sakkāya-diṭṭhi). The body is no longer taken as self or refuge.

At the level of contact and feeling, the practitioner trains to observe the arising of vedanā without reaction. Pleasant feeling no longer commands pursuit; painful feeling no longer provokes resistance. Feeling becomes known simply as feeling---an arising phenomenon with no authority, not part of the true self.

At the level of mental volition, a deeper shift occurs. The practitioner sees that intention toward the worldly phenomena is continuously constructing worldly identity. The sense of the worldly self, or the ego, is revealed as an **ongoing fabrication**, not an intrinsic permanent reality, not part of the true self. When worldly volition is no longer appropriated---when it is seen but not owned---the construction of worldly identity begins to cease, it then shifts to world-transcending identities.

Finally, at the level of worldly consciousness (viññāṇa), the most subtle attachment is penetrated. Viññāṇa is seen as worldly dependent, arising with conditions, and incapable of serving as part of the independent true self.

At this point, the entire structure of nāma--rūpa is fully understood. Nothing within it can serve as refuge, true identity, or foundation.

**5. The ending of Feeding: The Core of Purification**

The critical transformation in this process is not destruction but **non-participation**.

As worldly craving fades, nutriment is no longer taken up. Without craving, there is no feeding. Without feeding, the system loses its worldly support. Worldly volitional construction stops. Volitional union with the world-transcending identities, and the realm of Nibbana emerges. Consciousness is no longer supported by the worldly and become purified and independent (anissito).

This is the essence of internal alchemy: **the transformation of worldly participation into pure and independent consciousness**.

**6. The Uraga Sutta: The Final Shedding**

The Uraga Sutta expresses the culmination of this process through a precise and powerful image: "So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ."

The bhikkhu abandons the total enclosure of the universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

This image must be understood structurally. The serpent transcends its own limit by giving up its old skin, and to become something new, better.

Likewise, the practitioner transcends his/her own limited existence with dukkha within saṃsāra, to become a better existence with permanent life and happiness by giving up the worldly, saṃsāric identity. He **abandons the entire structure of orapāraṁ**---the inner and outer limits of the world.

This abandonment is necessary for purification. It includes:

- the body as identity

- feeling as guide

- volition as self-construction

- consciousness as ground

Nothing within the world remains appropriated to support our existence. Human existence thus becomes independent (anissito) of the world, without bondage. With this independence, liberation is possible.

**7. The Transformation and Transcendence**

When the four nutriments are fully understood, their operation ceases to bind being with the world. Without worldly craving, there is no intake of nutriment. Without nutriment, the sustaining mechanisms of worldly existence collapse.

The process unfolds naturally: The withdrawal of fuel leads to the ending of worldly volitional construction. Without worldly construction, consciousness no longer sustains worldly identity of nāma--rūpa as an ongoing system. Without that support, the entire structure is purified.

The culmination of purification is a **two-phase transformation**: the **ending of worldly support** and the **emergence of independent, purified existence**.

When the four nutriments are fully understood through direct anupassanā, their binding function ceases. This means their **worldly conditioning power is neutralized**. The key shift lies in the ending of **worldly feeding**.

As kāma-taṇhā, bhava-taṇhā, and vibhava-taṇhā fade, the practitioner no longer participates in the cycle of appropriation. Nutriment, in its worldly sense, is no longer taken up. Physical processes may still operate at a residual level, but they are no longer **owned**, no longer **appropriated as part of the true self**, and no longer **fueling worldly identity construction**.

At the level of mental volition, this produces a decisive transformation. Worldly-directed intention---the force that previously constructed ego and continuity within saṃsāra---comes to cessation. In its place arises a **reorientation toward the world-transcending identities**. This is not a continuation of becoming within the world system, but a **shift in alignment**. The energy that once sustained saṃsāric identity is no longer invested in it.

At the level of consciousness, the most profound change occurs. Previously, viññāṇa functioned as **worldly consciousness**, dependent on sense contact and bound to nāma--rūpa. Now, freed from the support of worldly craving and appropriation, consciousness is no longer anchored in the world. It becomes **purified and independent (anissito)**---no longer leaning on worldly objects, no longer conditioned by the sensory matrix.

This independence must be understood precisely. It is not the continuation of the old consciousness in a refined form; nor is it annihilation. It is the **release of consciousness from its dependency on the world-system**. The binding interface between consciousness and nāma--rūpa is severed.

Nāma--rūpa, as a *worldly construct*, is fully understood and no longer functions as a ground of identity. It no longer serves as the field in which existence is defined. The practitioner stands **independent of it**.

This is why the Buddha's statement---"when worldly consciousness as nutriment is fully understood, nāma--rūpa is fully understood"---marks a point of completion. Nothing further needs to be done because the **mechanism of bondage has been dismantled at its root**, and the **basis for worldly continuity has been removed**.

What follows is not re-entry into worldly becoming, nor a void of non-existence, but the opening to the realm of Nibbāna. In this way, the endpoint of purification is not destruction, but **complete independence from the world (anissito)**. The nutriment-driven system of saṃsāra no longer governs existence. The practitioner has **transcended the necessity of that worldly support structure altogether**.

This is the true completion of internal alchemy: the **liberation from all worldly conditioned support**, and the realization of the Deathless realm beyond the world.

### Uraga Sutta (Snp 1.1): A Structural Analysis of Total Withdrawal from the Universe

The **Uraga Sutta** is a **precise engineering manual of disengagement from the world (loka)**. The central refrain---*"So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ"*---must be taken literally and structurally:

The bhikkhu **abandons the total enclosure of the universe (orapāra)**, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

This is a **complete systemic exit for transcendence**. Like the serpent that sheds its skin upon reaching its limit, the bhikkhu releases the entire structure of worldly manifestation (bhava). What follows is not a new becoming within the world, but a transcendence of its boundaries---no longer confined by the inner and outer limits of the world and the universe.

**I. Orapāra: The Total System Boundary**

**Orapāra** establishes the scope:

- **Ora** = inner, lower, immediate structural containment

- **Pāra** = outer, upper, ultimate boundary

Together, this is **The entire bounded system of manifestation---from inner conditioning to outer cosmic limit.**

Thus, every verse is not about partial purification. It is about **removing the structural conditions that bind the citta within this total cosmic enclosure**.

**II. The Serpent Mechanism: Shedding as Structural Release**

The serpent metaphor is exact and technical: It **abandons it's old shell entirely when it becomes a constraint rather than a container**. The shedding is not destruction of the snake, but **release from a limiting structure**.

Likewise: The bhikkhu does not refine worldly existence (bhava); he **outgrows and discards the entire system of worldly manifestation** when its limits are fully seen.

This "outgrowing" is precise: the **termination of confinement within the inner--outer cosmic enclosure (orapāra)**

Thus, the act of "shedding" marks a critical transition:

The end of worldly existence (bhava) as a containing structure, and the **non-return to it**.

This establishes the governing principle of the sutta:

**Liberation (Vimutti) is not transformation within the system of the world, but a complete disengagement from it---an exit beyond its structural limits.**

**III. Sequential Deconstruction of Worldly Binding**

Each verse targets a **specific structural component** that sustains the worldly system.

**1. Anger (Kodha): Immediate Reaction Field**

"One who removes arisen anger..."

Anger is not merely emotional---it is a **reactive ignition point**.\
Like snake venom spreading through a body, anger:

- Rapidly **activates the system**

- Forces **engagement with worldly objects**

Removing anger = neutralizing **reactive propulsion**.

Without reaction, the system loses its immediate activation layer.

**2. Rāga: Adhesion to worldly Form**

"Cut off rāga entirely..."

Rāga is **adhesive attraction**---the force that binds the citta to worldly objects.

The lotus metaphor is precise:

- The root is embedded **deep in the medium (world)**

- Removal requires **entering and uprooting completely**

Thus: Rāga is not surface-level liking---it is **structural embedding into worldly form**.

Cutting it off removes the **binding interface between citta and loka**.

**3. Taṇhā: Flow of Continuity**

"Cut off craving... dried up the swift-flowing stream"

Taṇhā is dynamic---it is **movement toward worldly continuity**: **ongoing propulsion toward worldly becoming**.

Drying the stream means: The **supply line of worldly becoming collapses**.

No flow → no continuation → no renewal of worldly existence.

**4. Māna: Structural Positioning**

"Uprooted conceit..."

Māna is not pride in the modern sense. It is:

The **relational positioning system** (I am higher, lower, equal).

This creates: Identity coordinates and Comparative existence

The flood destroying a weak bridge indicates: When insight arises, the entire **identity-structure collapses instantly**. Without māna, there is no **reference point to exist within the system**.

**5. Bhava: The Engine of Worldly Manifestation**

**Bhava = the process of worldly manifestation (becoming).**\
It is the **active arising of worldly forms within the laws of the universe (loka)**---the moment something takes shape, enters structure, and becomes subject to decay and dukkha.

Bhava is therefore **dynamic and conditioned**:

- It is the movement from potential into formed reality

- It is the binding of that formation to the system of the world

The udumbara analogy is exact: one searches for essence (sāra) within worldly existence, and one finds none.

Conclusion: **There is no stable core, no enduring heartwood, anywhere within the process of worldly becoming (bhava).**

What appears substantial for any worldly form is only **constructed manifestation without intrinsic essence**.

Thus, the implications are structural:

- **Bhāvanā (development) within the world** = continuation of manifestation, the deliberate cultivation of further formations within bhava

- As long as one "develops" within this world, one **remains bound to its laws** of decay and death

By contrast: **Exhaustion of worldly bhava (parikkhīṇa)** = termination of the process of worldly arising itself

This is the **cessation of the engine that produces and sustains worldly manifestation**, and the end of participation in the cycle of worldly formation, decay, and re-arising.

In this way, the bhikkhu does not discover a purer essence within the world--- he **sees the absence of essence in all worldly becoming**, and thereby brings the entire process of worldly bhava to exhaustion.

**IV. Collapse of Internal Fabrication Systems**

**6. Beyond Bhava / Abhava**

"Transcended the cycle of worldly existence and annihilation"

This removes both: **worldly** **Becoming (bhava)** and **annihilation /Non-becoming (abhava)**.

Thus: Even the idea of "escaping into nothingness" is rejected. The world system itself is transcended.

**7. Vitakkā and Kappa: Mental Construction**

"Mental projections... fabrications carved away"

- **Vitakkā** = directional projection toward worldly objects

- **Kappa** = internal mental fabrication architecture

Together they form: The **internal modeling engine of reality**

Their removal means: No mental projection outward, and no mental construction inward.

**8--13. Papañca: Total Mental Expansion**

Papañca = the multiplication engine of conceptual proliferation

Nāccasārī / Na paccasārī define **precision cognition**:

- No exaggeration of facts (no inflation of meaning)

- No understatement of facts (no denial of fact)

This results in: **Direct perception without distortion.**

From here, a sequence unfolds:

- Seeing the world as **vitatha (unreal / not as it appears)**

- Leading to:

  - **vītalobho** (no grasping)

  - **vītarāgo** (no passion)

  - **vītadoso** (no irritation)

  - **vītamoho** (no confusion)

This is the **shutdown of interpretive overlays that sustain the world's apparent solidity.**

**V. Root-Level Eradication**

**14. Anusayā: Latent Source Code**

"Underlying tendencies... uprooted"

Anusayā = dormant trigger programs in the citta

They are not active---but they **wait for stimulation** and then re-activate the entire system.

Removing them means no future reactivation is possible.

**15--16. Removal of Return Pathways**

Two critical eliminations:

**Darathajā + Paccayāse**

- No distress-born seeds

- No conditions for return to the world

**Vanathajā + Hetukappā**

- No worldly entanglement structures

- No fabricated causal chains for worldly becoming

Thus: **The loop is broken permanently**.

No input → no process → no output → no re-entry.

**VI. Final State: System Shutdown**

**17. Removal of Hindrances + Extraction of the Thorn**

- **Nīvaraṇe** = operational blockers of clarity

- **Visalla** = removal of the embedded "hook"

The thorn is the **internal anchor that allows objects to penetrate and trigger the system**

Once removed: objects no longer "stick", no contraction occurs, and no papañca is initiated.

Result: **Anigho --- complete absence of internal agitation.**

**VII. The Complete Picture**

Across all 17 verses, the process is systematic:

1.  Neutralize reactions (anger)

2.  Remove attachment (rāga)

3.  Cut dynamic craving (taṇhā)

4.  Collapse identity structure (māna)

5.  Recognize no essence in worldly becoming (bhava)

6.  Stop projection to external worldly objects and internal mental fabrication (vitakkā, kappa)

7.  Eliminate conceptual proliferation (papañca)

8.  Uproot latent triggers (anusayā)

9.  Remove causal chains for return to the world

10. Extract the root "thorn"

The result is total disengagement from the bounded cosmic system.

**VIII. Conclusion: The Meaning of the Serpent**

The serpent abandons it's worn-out old skin when its limit is reached.

Likewise, the bhikkhu sees worldly manifestation (bhava) as bound to decay and dukkha, and releases it entirely.

This is **Vimutti**:

Not annihilation, but freedom from the world (loka)---\
the shedding of the total enclosure of the universe: Orapāra,\
like a serpent leaving behind its worn-out skin, without return.

#### SNP 1.1 Uraga Sutta (The Serpent Sutta)

In the etymology of the Pāli and Sanskrit texts, **Uraga** and **Nāga** are often used as synonyms, but they describe the entity from different perspectives.

**1). Etymology of Uraga (उरग)**

The word is a compound of **Uras** + **Ga**:

- **Uras (Chest/Breast):** The front part of the body.

- **Ga (To go/move):** The root for motion.

- **Literal Meaning:** \"The breast-goer\" or \"one who moves on its chest.\"

This refers to the **mode of locomotion**. An **Uraga** is a being that is bound to the surface it moves on. It has no legs to lift it off the ground.

**2). Etymology of Nāga (नाग)**

The word **Nāga** has a more prestigious status:

- **Agas (Mountain/Immovable):** Some etymologies link it to \"that which does not move\" (the mountain), implying a being of immense power and stability.

- **The Arahant:** In the Sutta Nipāta and other texts, the **Tathāgata** and Arahants are frequently called \"Nāgas\" because they are \"unshakeable\" and \"do no evil\" (*na āgu*).

**Nāgas** are generally friendly to humans, they passed to knowledges to humans and protect humans as well. Humans describe them respectfully with their *internal power*. An **Uraga** can be evolved to become when it's genetic lock is transcended, much like the catepillaar evolved to become butterfly. A \"True Nāga\" is one who has mastered the mental forces of the enclosure.

1\. One who removes arisen anger, as one \[cures\] spread snake-venom with antidotes; That bhikkhu gives up (**jahāti**) the whole universe (**orapāra**, the hither/ Interior /Lower and the yonder/ Exterior /Upper), as (**iva**) a serpent \[gives up/ sheds\] its worn-out (**jiṇṇam**), old (**purāṇa**) skin (**attaca**).

Yo uppatitaṁ vineti kodhaṁ, Visaṭaṁ sappavisaṁva osadhehi; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇam-iva-attacaṁ purāṇaṁ. 

Comment: **Orapāra** refers to the **dual boundaries** or the **totality of the enclosure** that defines the universe.

- **Ora (ओर / अव):** Derived from the Vedic *avara*, meaning **\"Hitherward,\" \"Interior,\"** or **\"Lower.\"** It refers to the immediate, structural \"Below\" or the inner boundary of the world system.

- **Pāra (पार):** From the root *pṛ* (to pass through), meaning **\"Yonder,\" \"Exterior,\"** or **\"Upper.\"** It refers to the furthest possible limit, the \"Outside,\" or the outer boundary of the system.

Combined, **Orapāra** literally translates to **\"The Hither and the Yonder\"** or **\"The Inner and the Outer limits.\"**

2\. He who has cut off **rāga** entirely, as one might pull up a lotus flower by plunging into the lake; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo rāgamudacchidā asesaṁ, Bhisapupphaṁva saroruhaṁ vigayha; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

3\. He who has cut off craving (**taṇhā**) entirely, as one having dried up the swift-flowing stream; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo taṇhamudacchidā asesaṁ, Saritaṁ sīghasaraṁ visosayitvā; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

4\. He who has uprooted conceit (**māna**) entirely, as a great flood sweeps away a very weak bridge of reeds; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo mānamudabbadhī asesaṁ, Naḷasetuṁva sudubbalaṁ mahogho; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

5\. He who has not found any essence (**sāra,** heartwoord) in worldly existence (**bhava**), as one searching for flowers on udumbara trees finds none; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nājjhagamā bhavesu sāraṁ, Vicinaṁ pupphamivā udumbaresu; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

Comment: **Bhava**

**1). The Vedic and Sanskrit Root of Bhava: √bhū (The Becoming)**

The root is **√bhū**, which in Sanskrit and Vedic means **\"to be,\" \"to become,\" \"to arise,\" or \"to manifest.\"**

- **The Process:** In the Vedas, **√bhū** is not a static state. It is the transition from potentiality to actuality. It is the act of a seed **becoming** a sprout.

- **Bhavati:** \"It becomes\" or \"It happens.\"

- **Bhāva:** The state of becoming.

**2). Bhāvanā: The Development**

**Bhāvanā** (Development) comes from the causative form of **√bhū**.

- It literally means \"causing to become\" or \"bringing into being.\"

- **In Practice:** When you develop **Concentration (Samadhi)**, you are performing **Bhāvanā**---you are cultivating, you are taking a mental potential and \"manifesting\" it into a stable reality within the physical and mental world.

**\**

**3). Bhava as \"Worldly Manifestation\"**

**Bhava** shall be translated as **\"Worldly Existence\"** is superior to the generic \"existence.\" Here is why:

- **Loka (The World):** The environment where things arise.

- **Bhava (The Arising):** The actual \"taking shape\" within that environment.

- If **Bhava** were just \"existence,\" it would be an abstract concept. But **Bhava** is the **Worldly Process**. It is the \"becoming\" that happens because of the laws of cause and effect.

- **The Logic:** If something arises (**bhava**), the laws of this universe are embedded into this new being, it belongs to the world (**loka**). If it belongs to the world, it is subject to death and decay (**dukkha**).

**4). The Engineering of \"Exhausting Bhava\"**

In verse 415, the term **Kāma-bhava-parikkhīṇa** (Exhaustion/depletion/destruction of Sex and Bhava) describes the end of this \"Arising.\"

- Without knowledge of the liberation path, beings constantly seek to \"Become\" (**Bhava**) something in this world---a better person, a more successful author, a higher being in a heaven.

- With knowledge of the liberation path, beings recognize that all \"becoming\" is worldly. It is a bound to the universal laws of power, decay, and death.

- **The Result:** The **Citta** disenchants the process of arising. It stops \"developing\" (**bhāvanā**) worldly identities. When you stop the \"becoming,\" the fuel for **Bhava** is **parikkhīṇa** (exhausted).

6\. He in whom no anger (**kopa**) exists internally, and who has transcended the cycle of **Worldly Existence** and **annihilation** (**iti-bhava-abhava-tañca**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yassantarato na santi kopā, Iti-bhava-abhavatañca vītivatto; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

7\. He whose mental **projections** (**vitakkā**) (to external worldly objects) are completely eradicated, Internally, all mental fabrications (Kappa) are thoroughly carved away; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yassa vitakkā vidhūpitā, Ajjhattaṁ suvikappitā asesā; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

8\. He who does not exaggerate the facts and does not understate them, and has entirely transcended this whole Mental Expansion (**papañcaṁ**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nāccasārī na paccasārī, Sabbaṁ accagamā imaṁ papañcaṁ; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

Comment: **Nāccasārī, Na paccasārī** and **Papañca**

**Nāccasārī**: Does not exaggerate, you don\'t add to the truth. If a thing is worldly, it is just worldly. You don\'t puff it up with \"future meaning\" or \"special importance.\" You don\'t make it more than it is.

**Na paccasārī**: Does not understate, you don\'t ignore the truth or try to hide from it by looking backward. You don\'t pretend a fact isn\'t happening.

**Papañca**: Proliferation / Mental Expansion. This is the engineering of the mind where it multiplies concepts and fabrications (**kappa**) until the **Worldly Manifestation** appears complex and overwhelming.

9\. He who does not exaggerate the facts and does not understate them, having known that everything in the world (**loke**) is **unreal** (**vitatham**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nāccasārī na paccasārī, Sabbaṁ vitathamidanti ñatva loke; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

10\. He who does not exaggerate the facts and does not understate them, having known that everything in the world is unreal, be **free from clinging** (**vītalobho**) \[to them\]; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nāccasārī na paccasārī, Sabbaṁ vitathamidanti vītalobho; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

11\. He who does not exaggerate the facts and does not understate them, having known that everything in the world is unreal, be **free from passion** (**vītarāgo**) \[toward them\]; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nāccasārī na paccasārī, Sabbaṁ vitathamidanti vītarāgo; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

12\. He who does not exaggerate the facts and does not understate them, having known that everything in the world is unreal, be **free from irritation** (**vītadoso**) \[at them\]; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nāccasārī na paccasārī, Sabbaṁ vitathamidanti vītadoso; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

13\. He who does not exaggerate the facts and does not understate them, having known that everything in the world is unreal, and being **free from confusion** (**vītamoho**) \[toward them\]; that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nāccasārī na paccasārī, Sabbaṁ vitathamidanti vītamoho; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

14\. He who has no underlying tendencies (**anusayā**) whatsoever, and has completely uprooted the roots of the unwholesome (**akusalā**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yassānusayā na santi keci, Mūlā ca akusalā samūhatāse; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

Comment: **Anusayā**: Underlying tendencies / latent predispositions. These are the \"sleeping\" triggers or \"source\" programs that remain dormant in the **Citta** until a worldly object activates them.

15\. He who has no **distress** (**darathajā**) whatsoever, nor any **conditions** (**paccayāse**) for returning (**Āgamanāya**) to this world (**oraṁ**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yassa darathajā na santi keci, Oraṁ āgamanāya paccayāse; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

16\. He who has no worldly **entanglements** (**vanathajā**) whatsoever, nor any **Fabricated causes** (**hetukappā**) that serve as causes for worldly **clinging** and **becoming** (**vinibandhāya bhavāya**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yassa vanathajā na santi keci, Vinibandhāya bhavāya hetukappā; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇaṁ.

17\. He who has abandoned the **five obstacles** (**nīvaraṇe**), and is **free from distress** (**anigho**), having crossed over doubt (**tiṇṇa-kathaṅ-katho**) and free from the thorn/arrow (**visallo**); that bhikkhu gives up the whole universe, just as a serpent sheds its worn-out old skin.

Yo nīvaraṇe pahāya pañca, Anigho tiṇṇakathaṅkatho visallo; So bhikkhu jahāti orapāraṁ, Urago jiṇṇamivattacaṁ purāṇanti.

Comment: **Nīvaraṇe and Visalla**

**Nīvaraṇe**: Obstacles / Hindrances. These are the five mental hindrances (**Kāmacchanda**: desire /wish for sexual pleasure, **Vyāpāda**: irritation / ill-will; **Thīna-Middha:** sluggishness and drowsiness; **Uddhacca-Kukkucca**: restlessness and worry; and **Vicikicchā**: doubt) that block the **Citta** from seeing the exit.

**Visalla**: **Vi-Salla,** free from the thorn/arrow**.** A \"thorn\" (**Salla**) is an external object that has penetrated the boundary of the system and become an internal source of infection, inflammation, and constant \"thermal noise.\"

**1). The Thorn as the \"Projective Hook\"**

Here the \"thorn\" is the **latent trigger** in the mind.

- **The Mechanism:** When a worldly object is encountered, if the \"thorn\" of craving is still embedded in the **Citta**, it creates an immediate contraction. This contraction is the \"sting\" that forces the mind to start the process of **Papañca** (mental expansion).

- **Visallo:** To be \"without the thorn\" means the **Citta** no longer has that internal hook. You can encounter a worldly fact, and because there is no thorn to catch it, the fact does not \"pull\" the mind into an fabrication.

**2). The Extraction of the \"Source-Code\" of Pain**

The thorn is often described in the ancient texts as having several layers---sexual passion, irritation, and confusion.

- **Subsurface Infection:** Even if you aren\'t currently \"feeling\" the pain, if the thorn is still inside, the system is not pure. It is still \"born of distress\" (**darathajā**).

- **Total Extraction:** **Visallo** means the root cause of the \"sting\" has been pulled out. This is the \"uprooting\" we discussed earlier (**samūhatāse**). Once the thorn is gone, the **Citta** becomes **Anigho**---completely free from the \"fever\" or the \"inner burning\" that drives the mental expansion.

### Ajita-māṇava-pucchā (Snp 5.1): A Structural Analysis of Human Worldly Existence and Its Shutdown

**I. The Problem Statement: A Covered and Non-Luminous World**

Ajita, as a **Māṇava (thinker)**, begins with a diagnostic question:

Why does the world not shine? This is not metaphysical curiosity---it is a **signal failure analysis**.

The answer defines the system condition:

The world is **covered (nivuto)** by **Avijjā (ignorance)**\
It **does not shine** due to **Vevicchā (**selfishness, distinction-seeking (from *viccha*), stinginess**)** and **Pamādā (zeallessness)**\
It is **tainted/defiled (abhilepanaṁ)** by **Jappa (**muttering or whispering desire**)**\
Its great danger is **Dukkha**

This establishes a **four-layer obstruction model**:

**1. Avijjā --- Total System Covering**

The world is not dim---it is **structurally covered** from accessing truth.

**2. Vevicchā + Pamādā --- Functional Degradation**

- **Vevicchā**: fragmentation through distinction-seeking

- **Pamādā**: loss of zealousness, energetic collapse

Together: They degrade the system's **clarity and coherence**, preventing luminosity.

**3. Jappa --- The Tainted/Defiled Layer**

Jappa is **Continuous low-level mental whispering that coats/defiles perception**

The system is not only covered externally---it is **internally defiled**.

**4. Dukkha --- Structural Instability**

The **inherent systemic risk of the entire constructed process,** a **built-in instability of the architecture itself**.

**II. The Streams (Sotā): Continuous Input Feeding the System**

Ajita identifies the next layer: "The streams flow everywhere..."

These **streams (sotā)** are the **input channels of worldly existence**:

- Sensory inflow

- Cognitive inflow

- Continuous activation of the system

Without streams: The system cannot sustain its operation.

**III. Control Mechanism: Sati and Paññā**

The response defines a precise control architecture:

**Sati = obstruction**\
**Paññā = closure**

**1). Sati --- Interception Layer**

- Operates at **point-of-contact**

- Prevents automatic continuation of input

It **halts the propagation of incoming streams**

**2). Paññā --- Structural Shutdown**

Paññā is the **understanding that removes the necessity of the streams entirely.** So Sati is a tactical restraint and Paññā is **strategic termination**.

**IV. The Core System: Nāma-Rūpa as Worldly Existence**

Ajita then asks the decisive question: Where does nāma-rūpa end?

This is the architecture:

- **Rūpa** = physical system (hardware)

- **Nāma** = processing layer (mental operations)

Together: Nāma-rūpa is **the total interface of human worldly existence**

It is a **constructed experiential system dependent on worldly conditions\**

**V. The Processor: Viññāṇa as Worldly Consciousness**

Key structural dependency: Nāma-rūpa persists only through **viññāṇa**

And viññāṇa is generated by **6 sense doors × 6 objects** and is entirely **dependent on the world-system**. Therefore Viññāṇa is not the citta---it is a **produced process within the worldly system.**

**VI. The Shutdown Sequence**

The sutta gives a **precise termination chain**:

**Step 1: Remove Fuel**

- Eliminate **rāga, dosa, moha**

**Step 2: Processor Ceases**

- Without fuel, **viññāṇa stops**

**Step 3: Interface Collapses**

- Without viññāṇa, **nāma-rūpa ceases without remainder**

This is critical: The world does not fade---it **ceases structurally**.

**VII. The Final Operational Instruction**

Ajita's final question concerns **post-understanding conduct**.

The answer is a **live operational protocol**:

- Do not crave sensory pleasures

- Keep the mind unmuddied

- Be skilled in all mental objects

- Move with continuous mindfulness

This is the **maintenance mode required for shutdown**.

**VIII. Conclusion: The End of the Human World-System**

The teaching is exact: The human world is not an ultimate reality---it is a **constructed system sustained by conditions**. It is covered, fed, processed and rendered. When its conditions are removed, **The system ceases without remainder.** This is not annihilation. It is the **shutdown of human worldly existence (nāma-rūpa)**, and the release of the citta from the trap of the world.

#### SNP 5.1 Ajita-Māṇava-Pucchā (The Questions of Ajita, the Thinker)

Comment: **Māṇava**

The word **Māṇava** (माणवक/माणव) is derived from the Sanskrit root **√man** (मनु).

- **Man (To Think/Measure):** The root refers to the faculty of \"measuring\" or \"calculating\" the world. A *Mānuṣa* (mankind) or *Māṇava* is literally \"one who measures\" or \"the thinker.\"

- **The Veda Analysis (Lineage of Manu):** In the Vedic sense, **Māṇava** means \"a descendant of Manu.\" Manu is the archetypal \"First Man\" or the \"Standardized Mind.\" To be a Māṇava is to exist within the framework of the **Standard Mind**---the blueprint of the human experience.

- **The Diminutive \"ka\":** Sometimes seen as *Māṇavaka*, the suffix indicates a \"small\" or \"young\" version. It implies a being who is still under the training of the \"Standard\" (Brahma/Manu).

------------------------------------------------------------------------

1\. \"By what is the world covered? (asked the venerable āyasmā Ajita) Why does it not shine? What do you say it is tainted/ defiled (the sticky coating)? What is its great dnager?\"

"Kenassu nivuto loko, *(icc-āyasmā ajito)* Kenassu nappakāsati; Kissābhilepanaṁ brūsi, Kiṁsu tassa mahabbhayaṁ".

Comment: Ajita is asking why the \"Light of Truth\" or the \"Source of Mankind\" (**Anu**) is not visible. What is dampening the signal?

2\. \"The world is covered by ignorance (Avijjā), Ajita,\" said the Blessed One. \"Due to selfishness (Vevicchā, distinction-seeking (from *viccha*), stinginess) and zeallessness (Pamādā), it does not shine. Muttering or whispering desire (Jappa) is its defilment/taint, I say. Dukkha is its great danger.\"

"Avijjāya nivuto loko, *(ajitāti bhagavā)* Vevicchā pamādā nappakāsati; Jappābhilepanaṁ brūmi, Dukkhamassa mahabbhayaṁ".

3\. "Everywhere the streams are flowing," (said the Venerable Ajita). "What is the barrier against the streams? Speak of the restraint of the streams: by what are the streams closed off?"

\"The streams (Sotā) flow everywhere,\" (said the venerable Ajita). \"What is the obstruction for the streams? Tell me the restraint for the streams (Sensory Restraint). By what are the streams shut off?\"

"Savanti sabbadhi sotā, *(iccāyasmā ajito)* Sotānaṁ kiṁ nivāraṇaṁ; Sotānaṁ saṁvaraṁ brūhi, Kena sotā pidhiyyare".

4\. \"Whatever streams there are in the world, Ajita,\" said the Blessed One, \"Mindfulness (Sati) is their obstruction. I tell you the restraint of the streams; By Wisdom (Paññā) they are shut off.\"

"Yāni sotāni lokasmiṁ, *(ajitāti bhagavā)* Sati tesaṁ nivāraṇaṁ; Sotānaṁ saṁvaraṁ brūmi, Paññāyete pidhiyyare".

5\. \"Wisdom (Paññā) and mindfulness (Sati),\" (said the venerable Ajita), \"And Name-and-Form (nāma-rūpa), my noble friend (mārisa), when asked by me please tell me: Where does this \[nāma-rūpa\] come to an end?\"

"Paññā ceva sati yañca, *(iccāyasmā ajito)* Nāmarūpañca mārisa; Etaṁ me puṭṭho pabrūhi, Katthetaṁ uparujjhati".

6\. \"Regarding this question you asked, Ajita, I tell you: Where Name-and-Form (Nāma-rūpa) stops without remainder--- by the stopping of worldly consciousness (Viññāṇa) \[aggregate\], right there this \[Name-and-Form\] ceases.\"

"Yametaṁ pañhaṁ apucchi, ajita taṁ vadāmi te; Yattha nāmañca rūpañca, asesaṁ uparujjhati; Viññāṇassa nirodhena, etthetaṁ uparujjhati".

Comment: the end of **Nāma-rūpa**

**Nāma-rūpa** is the literal **Worldly Existence**. It is the \"Simulation Interface\" that the Citta perceives as a solid reality.

- **Rūpa (The Hardware):** The physical manifestation and the laws of physics that govern it.

- **Nāma (The Operating System):** The mental factors (feeling, perception, intention) that process data from that physical hardware.
