- Together, they constitute the total **worldly existence**. This existence is not ultimate existence; it is a manifest of the **true existgence (citta)** in this world. Because it is bound by the laws of cause and effect and death, it is **dukkha**.

**Why Viññāṇa is Worldly**

The reason **Viññāṇa** (Consciousness) is classified as \"worldly\" is because of its **Source Dependency**. It is not an independent entity; it is a **generated product** of the person system:

- **The 6 Sense Doors:** The eye, ear, nose, tongue, body, and **Mano** (the internal sense door).

- **The Worldly Contact:** When these 6 doors respond to the 6 external objects (sights, sounds, smells, tastes, touches, and thoughts), they generate 6 types of **Viññāṇa** consciousness.

- **The Logic:** Since the sense doors are physical/mental structures of the world, and the objects are part of the world (**loka**), the consciousness produced by their collision is fundamentally **Worldly Consciousness**. It belongs to the world. It is not our true self, nor part of our true self, nor belonging to our true self.

**The Shutdown: Ending the Worldly Existence**

The \"ending\" of the worldly existence is a mechanical chain reaction. The simulation cannot run without the processor.

1)  **Stop the Processor:** By the complete eradication of **rāga, dosa, and moha**, the \"energy\" that fuels the 6 sense doors is cut off. This results in the **ending** of the worldly consciousness.

2)  **The Resulting Collapse:** Once the worldly **Viññāṇa** stops, the rendering of **Nāma-rūpa** (worldly existence) ceases \"without remainder\" (**asesaṁ**).

3)  **The Final Shedding:** This is the moment the **Citta** realizes that the entire interface was just an **Abhilepanaṁ** (defilement) or a **Jiṇṇam-attacaṁ** (worn-out skin).

7\. \"Those who have fully understood (Saṅkhāta) the mental objects (dhammāse), and the many (Puthū) who are still in training (Sekhā) here; being asked, noble friend (mārisa), tell me their conduct (Iriya)--- How does a prudent one (Nipako) behave?\"

"Ye ca saṅkhāta-dhammāse, ye ca sekhā puthū idha; Tesaṁ me nipako iriyaṁ, puṭṭho pabrūhi mārisa".

8\. \"One should not crave after (bhigijjha) sensory pleasures; The mental volition (mana) should be (Siyā) not-muddy (anāvilo); Skilled in all your mental objects (dhammāna); a bhikkhu should moving around mindfully.\"

"Kāmesu nā-bhigijjheyya, Manasā-anāvilo siyā; Kusalo sabba-dhammānaṁ, Sato bhikkhu paribbaje"ti.

### Puttamaṃsa Sutta (SN 12.63): [The Four Nutriments (Āhāra)](https://href.li/?https://arahanism.org/2025/07/31/transcending-the-four-nutriments-ahara-a-path-to-liberation/) in the Multi-Layer Human Existence Model

In the Buddha's profound analysis of existence, he identifies four kinds of *āhāra*---nutriments or sustenance---that condition and sustain life in *saṃsāra*, the cycle of birth and death. These encompass the entire framework that binds beings to conditioned existence. In *SN 12.63 (Puttamaṃsa Sutta)*, the Buddha's teaching on the four nutriments (kabalīkāra-āhāra, phassa-āhāra, manosañcetanā-āhāra, viññāṇa-āhāra) is a **functional systems model** describing how worldly existence (nāma--rūpa) is continuously generated and sustained. This becomes:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Nutriment**      **Layer Fed**                        **Function**
  ------------------ ------------------------------------ ---------------------------------------
  Physical food      Physical body                        Maintains biological structure

  Contact (phassa)   Feeling / emotional body             Generates experiential tone

  Mental volition    Mental body                          Constructs ego & becoming

  Consciousness      Whole nāma--rūpa worldly existence   Sustains the system simulation itself
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

This is a **vertical dependency stack**.

**1). Physical Nutriment - Feeding the Rūpa Base**

"Bhikkhus, if people knew as I know the danger in sensual pleasures, they would not even think of sensual pleasures."\
--- *AN 9.41*

Edible food is the most obvious form of sustenance. It sustains the physical body, Level 1 kāya (gross embodiment) . The five cords of sensuality as input channels. While necessary for bodily survival, the clinging to taste, texture, and indulgence becomes a root of craving (*taṇhā*). For those on the path, food is not to be sought for pleasure, pride, or beautification, but for the maintenance of health and the support of practice.

In *MN 2 (Sabbāsava Sutta)*, the Buddha instructs that one should reflect wisely when consuming food: *"Not for fun, not for intoxication, not for fattening, not for beautification, but just for the maintenance and nourishment of this body..."*

**Liberation Approach:**\
We do not "give up" edible food through starvation, but we **liberate** the mind from *craving* it. When food is seen as fuel, not entertainment, the grip of sensuality weakens.

**2). Contact / Sense Impressions (Phassa-āhāra)**

**Phassa-āhāra** feeds the **vedanā layer**. Every experience arises through *phassa*---the contact of sense organ, object, and consciousness. Contact = sense base + object + consciousness → collision. This is the root condition for feeling (*vedanā*), which in turn fuels craving and clinging. Contact feeds the **experiential tonal field**. This corresponds to Level 2--3 (reactive + emotional layers), the "patterned body". The Buddha likens this nutriment to a flayed cow tormented by insects (SN 12.63): exposed and helpless at every contact point.

We live in a world addicted to stimulation---sight, sound, touch, thought---feeding endlessly on what pleases or distracts. Yet, every act of contact, unless understood wisely, is a moment of conditioning.

**Liberation Approach:**\
Contact is the **gateway between physical and psychological existence**. It is the first point where the world becomes "experienced" and the system begins to bind. The path is not to shut down the senses entirely, but to **restrain them** (*indriya-saṃvara*) **through mindfulness and wisdom**. Sense restraint guards the mind from being hijacked by craving and aversion. Through *Satipaṭṭhāna*, especially *vedanānupassanā*, we observe contact without reaction, and eventually, the allure fades. As the Buddha taught: *"Without contact, no feeling arises; without feeling, no craving arises...no further mental construction happens"* (SN 12.2).

**3). Mental Volition / Intention (Manosañ-cetanā-āhāra) - Feeding Identity Construction**

Volition (*cetanā*) is the seed of kamma---the intentional energy that propels action, becoming, and rebirth. This nutriment sustains our becoming because "*cetanāhaṃ, bhikkhave, kammaṃ vadāmi*---Bhikkhus, I call volition kamma" (AN 6.63).

**Mental Volition** does not merely mean "thought." It means **intentional structuring of experience**. This feeds both Level 4 (conceptual mind) and Level 3--4 transition (pattern → narrative identity). Mental volition selects, interprets, and organizes experience; constructs "I", "me", "mine" and generates continuity across moments. This is what says "I think, therefore I am" layer, we become what we think. it is **intentional structuring**. We are constantly feeding the future through our intentions---desiring, projecting, planning, fearing. This subtle but potent fuel perpetuates becoming (*bhava*) through thought formations (*saṅkhāras*).

Here the three taṇhā fully operate:

- Kāma-taṇhā → organizes experience toward pleasure

- Bhava-taṇhā → builds identity and continuity

- Vibhava-taṇhā → attempts to negate experience

Thus, mental volition is the **engine of worldly becoming (bhava)**.

**Liberation Approach:**\
The path is not to suppress intention but to **purify** it through *sammā-saṅkappa* (right intention). From renunciation (*nekkhamma*) and non-cruelty arise wholesome volitions.

**\**

**4). Consciousness (Viññāṇa-āhāra) - Feeding Nāma--Rūpa Itself**

The subtlest and most profound nutriment is consciousness itself. **Viññāṇa-āhāra** feeds the **entire worldly existence itself**. Just as a fire burns dependent on fuel, so too name-and-form (*nāma-rūpa*) dependes on worldly consciousness **Viññāṇa**, and in turn, perpetuals rebirth. This is the wheel of becoming.

**Nāma-rūpa** is the literal **Worldly Existence**. **Rūpa (The Hardware)** is the physical manifestation and the laws of physics that govern it. **Nāma (The Operating System)** is the mental factors (feeling, perception, intention) that process data from that physical hardware. Together, they constitute the total **worldly existence**. This existence is not the true, ultimate existence; it is a manifest of the **true existgence (citta)** in this world. Because it is bound by the laws of cause and effect and death, it is **dukkha**.

The reason **Viññāṇa** (Consciousness) is classified as \"worldly\" is because of its **Source Dependency**. It is not an independent entity; but a **generated product** of the person system including t**he 6 Sense Doors:** The eye, ear, nose, tongue, body, and **Mano** (the internal sense door). When these 6 doors respond to the 6 external objects (sights, sounds, smells, tastes, touches, and thoughts), **These Worldly Contacts** generate 6 types of **Viññāṇa** consciousness. Since the sense doors are physical/mental structures of the world, and the objects are part of the world (**loka**), the consciousness produced by their collision is fundamentally **Worldly Consciousness**. It belongs to the world. It is not our true self, nor part of our true self, nor belonging to our true self.

**Liberation Approach:**\
The \"ending\" of the worldly existence is a mechanical chain reaction. The simulation cannot run without the processor.

1)  **Stop the Processor:** By the complete eradication of **rāga, dosa, and moha**, the \"energy\" that fuels the 6 sense doors is cut off. This results in the **ending** of the worldly consciousness.

2)  **The Resulting Collapse:** Once the worldly **Viññāṇa** stops, the rendering of **Nāma-rūpa** (worldly existence) ceases \"without remainder\" (**asesaṁ**).

3)  **The Final Shedding:** This is the moment the **Citta** realizes that the entire interface was just an **Abhilepanaṁ** (defilement, taint) or a **Jiṇṇam-attacaṁ** (worn-out skin).

Liberartion is not physical renunciation alone, but **the ending of dependence on all forms of nutriment**. As long as there is feeding, there is becoming; and as long as there is becoming, there is suffering.

The arahant is described as one who "has cut off the stream, has broken the cycle of nutriment, has no fuel left for becoming." This is not nihilism, but the **freedom from worldly conditioned existence**. The four nutriments are not "bad"---they are just part of a system of bondage. When their danger is seen clearly, dispassion naturally arises.

**Reflection for Practice**

- Do I eat with mindfulness or craving?

- Do I indulge in sensory contact or guard my faculties?

- Are my intentions rooted in renunciation or in becoming?

- Does my worldly consciousness rest in stillness or feed endlessly on stimulation?

Let us reflect: *"What is the ending of nutriment? It is the ending of craving."* And where does craving stops? In the heart that has realized the deathless and unconditioned---*Nibbāna-dhātu*.

May all beings be nourished not by clinging, but by wisdom.\
May all paths converge in the end of nutriment, the end of worldly becoming, and the end of suffering.***\***

#### SN 12.63 Putta-maṃsa Sutta (Son's Flesh Sutta)

At S̄vattı̄. "Bhikkhus, there are these four kinds of nutriment for the maintenance of beings that have already come to be and for the assistance of those about to come to be.

*Sāvatthiyaṁ ... "cattārome, bhikkhave, āhārā bhūtānaṁ vā sattānaṁ ṭhitiyā sambhavesīnaṁ vā anuggahāya.*

What four? The nutriment edible food, gross or subtle; second, contact; third, mental volition; fourth, consciousness.

*Katame cattāro? Kabaḷīkāro āhāro oḷāriko vā sukhumo vā, phasso dutiyo, manosañcetanā tatiyā, viññāṇaṁ catutthaṁ.*

These are the four kinds of nutriment for the maintenance of beings that have already come to be and for the assistance of those about to come to be.

*Ime kho, bhikkhave, cattāro āhārā bhūtānaṁ vā sattānaṁ ṭhitiyā sambhavesīnaṁ vā anuggahāya.*

"And how, bhikkhus, should the nutriment edible food be seen? Suppose a couple, husband and wife, had taken limited provisions and were travelling through a desert. They have with them their only son, dear and beloved. Then, in the middle of the desert, their limited provisions would be used up and exhausted, while the rest of the desert remains to be crossed. The husband and wife would think: 'Our limited provisions have been used up and exhausted, while the rest of this desert remains to be crossed. Let us kill our only son, dear and beloved, and prepare dried and spiced meat. By eating our son's flesh, we can cross the rest of this desert. Let not all three of us perish!'

*Kathañca, bhikkhave, kabaḷīkāro āhāro daṭṭhabbo? Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dve jāyampatikā parittaṁ sambalaṁ ādāya kantāramaggaṁ paṭipajjeyyuṁ. Tesamassa ekaputtako piyo manāpo. Atha kho tesaṁ, bhikkhave, dvinnaṁ jāyampatikānaṁ kantāragatānaṁ yā parittā sambalamattā, sā parikkhayaṁ pariyādānaṁ gaccheyya. Siyā ca nesaṁ kantārāvaseso anatiṇṇo. Atha kho tesaṁ, bhikkhave, dvinnaṁ jāyampatikānaṁ evamassa: 'amhākaṁ kho yā parittā sambalamattā sā parikkhīṇā pariyādiṇṇā. Atthi cāyaṁ kantārāvaseso anittiṇṇo. Yannūna mayaṁ imaṁ ekaputtakaṁ piyaṁ manāpaṁ vadhitvā vallūrañca soṇḍikañca karitvā puttamaṁsāni khādantā evaṁ taṁ kantārāvasesaṁ nitthareyyāma, mā sabbeva tayo vinassimhā'ti.*

"Then, bhikkhus, the husband and wife would kill their only son, dear and beloved, prepare dried and spiced meat, and by eating their son's flesh they would cross the rest of the desert. While they are eating their son's flesh, they would beat their breasts and cry: 'Where are you, our only son? Where are you, our only son?'

*Atha kho te, bhikkhave, dve jāyampatikā taṁ ekaputtakaṁ piyaṁ manāpaṁ vadhitvā vallūrañca soṇḍikañca karitvā puttamaṁsāni khādantā evaṁ taṁ kantārāvasesaṁ nitthareyyuṁ. Te puttamaṁsāni ceva khādeyyuṁ, ure ca paṭipiseyyuṁ: 'kahaṁ, ekaputtaka, kahaṁ, ekaputtakā'ti.*

"What do you think, bhikkhus? Would they eat that food for amusement or for enjoyment or for the sake of physical beauty and attractiveness?"

*Taṁ kiṁ maññatha, bhikkhave, api nu te davāya vā āhāraṁ āhāreyyuṁ, madāya vā āhāraṁ āhāreyyuṁ, maṇḍanāya vā āhāraṁ āhāreyyuṁ, vibhūsanāya vā āhāraṁ āhāreyyun"ti?*

"No, Bhante." * "No hetaṁ, bhante".*

"Wouldn't they eat that food only for the sake of crossing the desert?"

*"Nanu te, bhikkhave, yāvadeva kantārassa nittharaṇatthāya āhāraṁ āhāreyyun"ti?*

"Yes, Bhante." * "Evaṁ, bhante".*

"It is in such a way, bhikkhus, that I say the nutriment edible food should be seen. When the nutriment edible food is fully understood, lust for the five cords of sensual pleasure is fully understood. When lust for the five cords of sensual pleasure is fully understood, there is no fetter bound by which a noble disciple might come back again to this world.

Comment: The five cords are defined by the sensory input received through the physical faculties:

1.  **Visible forms** cognizable by the eye.

2.  **Sounds** cognizable by the ear.

3.  **Odors** cognizable by the nose.

4.  **Flavors** cognizable by the tongue.

5.  **Tangibles** cognizable by the body.

*"'Evameva khvāhaṁ, bhikkhave, kabaḷīkāro āhāro daṭṭhabbo'ti vadāmi. Kabaḷīkāre, bhikkhave, āhāre pariññāte pañca kāmaguṇiko rāgo pariññāto hoti. Pañca kāmaguṇike rāge pariññāte natthi taṁ saṁyojanaṁ yena saṁyojanena saṁyutto ariyasāvako puna imaṁ lokaṁ āgaccheyya.*

"And how, bhikkhus, should the nutriment contact be seen? Suppose there is a flayed cow. If she stands exposed to a wall, the creatures dwelling in the wall would nibble at her. If she stands exposed to a tree, the creatures dwelling in the tree would nibble at her. If she stands exposed to water, the creatures dwelling in the water would nibble at her. If she stands exposed to the open air, the creatures dwelling in the open air would nibble at her. Whatever that flayed cow stands exposed to, the creatures dwelling there would nibble at her.

*Kathañca, bhikkhave, phassāhāro daṭṭhabbo? Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, gāvī niccammā kuṭṭañce nissāya tiṭṭheyya. Ye kuṭṭanissitā pāṇā te naṁ khādeyyuṁ. Rukkhañce nissāya tiṭṭheyya, ye rukkhanissitā pāṇā te naṁ khādeyyuṁ. Udakañce nissāya tiṭṭheyya, ye udakanissitā pāṇā te naṁ khādeyyuṁ. Ākāsañce nissāya tiṭṭheyya, ye ākāsanissitā pāṇā te naṁ khādeyyuṁ. Yaṁ yadeva hi sā, bhikkhave, gāvī niccammā nissāya tiṭṭheyya, ye tannissitā pāṇā te naṁ khādeyyuṁ.*

"It is in such a way, bhikkhus, that I say the nutriment contact should be seen. When the nutriment contact is fully understood, the three kinds of feeling are fully understood. When the three kinds of feeling are fully understood, I say, there is nothing further that a noble disciple needs to do.

Comment: The three kinds of feeling are:

1.  **Pleasant feeling** (*sukhā vedanā*): Experiences that are agreeable or physically/mentally comfortable.

2.  **Painful feeling** (*dukkhā vedanā*): Experiences that are disagreeable or physically/mentally uncomfortable.

3.  **Neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling** (*adukkhamasukhā vedanā*): Neutral experiences, often described as equanimous or indifferent.

*Evameva khvāhaṁ, bhikkhave, 'phassāhāro daṭṭhabbo'ti vadāmi. Phasse, bhikkhave, āhāre pariññāte tisso vedanā pariññātā honti. Tīsu vedanāsu pariññātāsu ariyasāvakassa natthi kiñci uttarikaraṇīyanti vadāmi.*

"And how, bhikkhus, should the nutriment mental volition be seen? Suppose there is a charcoal pit deeper than a man's height, filled with glowing coals without flame or smoke. A man would come along wanting to live, not wanting to die, desiring happiness and averse to suffering. Then two strong men would grab him by both arms and drag him towards the charcoal pit. The man's volition would be to get far away, his longing would be to get far away, his wish would be to get far away \[from the charcoal pit\].

*Kathañca, bhikkhave, manosañcetanāhāro daṭṭhabbo? Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, aṅgārakāsu sādhikaporisā puṇṇā aṅgārānaṁ vītaccikānaṁ vītadhūmānaṁ. Atha puriso āgaccheyya jīvitukāmo amaritukāmo sukhakāmo dukkhappaṭikūlo. Tamenaṁ dve balavanto purisā nānābāhāsu gahetvā taṁ aṅgārakāsuṁ upakaḍḍheyyuṁ. Atha kho, bhikkhave, tassa purisassa ārakāvassa cetanā ārakā patthanā ārakā paṇidhi.*

For what reason? Because he knows: 'I will fall into this charcoal pit and on that account, I will meet death or deadly suffering.'

*Taṁ kissa hetu? Evañhi, bhikkhave, tassa purisassa hoti: 'imañcāhaṁ aṅgārakāsuṁ papatissāmi, tatonidānaṁ maraṇaṁ vā nigacchāmi maraṇamattaṁ vā dukkhan'ti.*

"It is in such a way, bhikkhus, that I say the nutriment mental volition should be seen. When the nutriment mental volition is fully understood, the three kinds of craving are fully understood. When the three kinds of craving are fully understood, I say, there is nothing further that a noble disciple needs to do.

Comment: The three kinds of craving are:

1.  **Kāma-taṇhā (Craving for Sexual Pleasures):** The drive to find satisfaction, delight, and nourishment through the five cords of sensual pleasure (sights, sounds, smells, tastes, and tangibles) rooted in the **Kāma realm**.

2.  **Bhava-taṇhā (Craving for Worldly Becoming/Existence):** The drive to be, to become, or to continue existing in the world. This is the thirst for worldly identity, ego-expansion, and the continuation of the ego into future existence.

3.  **Vibhava-taṇhā (Craving for Non-existence/annihilation):** The drive to end existence in absolute sense, often rooted in the desire to escape suffering via the nihilistic approach by merging with universal source. It is the craving for annihilation through arupa realm.

*Evameva khvāhaṁ, bhikkhave, 'manosañcetanāhāro daṭṭhabbo'ti vadāmi. Manosañcetanāya, bhikkhave, āhāre pariññāte tisso taṇhā pariññātā honti. Tīsu taṇhāsu pariññātāsu ariyasāvakassa natthi kiñci uttarikaraṇīyanti vadāmi.*

"And how, bhikkhus, should the nutriment consciousness be seen? Suppose they were to arrest a bandit, a criminal, and bring him before the king, saying: 'Sire, this man is a bandit, a criminal. Impose on him whatever punishment you wish.' The king says to them: 'Go, men, in the morning strike this man with a hundred spears.' In the morning, they strike him with a hundred spears. Then at noon the king asks: 'Men, how's that man?'-- 'Still alive, sire.'-- 'Then go, and at noon strike him with a hundred spears.' At noon they strike him with a hundred spears. Then in the evening the king asks: 'Men, how's that man?'-- 'Still alive, sire.' -- 'Then go, and in the evening strike him with a hundred spears.' In the evening, they strike him with a hundred spears.

*Kathañca, bhikkhave, viññāṇāhāro daṭṭhabbo? Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, coraṁ āgucāriṁ gahetvā rañño dasseyyuṁ: 'ayaṁ te, deva, coro āgucārī, imassa yaṁ icchasi taṁ daṇḍaṁ paṇehī'ti. Tamenaṁ rājā evaṁ vadeyya: 'gacchatha, bho, imaṁ purisaṁ pubbaṇhasamayaṁ sattisatena hanathā'ti. Tamenaṁ pubbaṇhasamayaṁ sattisatena haneyyuṁ. Atha rājā majjhanhikasamayaṁ evaṁ vadeyya: 'ambho, kathaṁ so puriso'ti? 'Tatheva, deva, jīvatī'ti. Tamenaṁ rājā evaṁ vadeyya: 'gacchatha, bho, taṁ purisaṁ majjhanhikasamayaṁ sattisatena hanathā'ti. Tamenaṁ majjhanhikasamayaṁ sattisatena haneyyuṁ. Atha rājā sāyanhasamayaṁ evaṁ vadeyya: 'ambho, kathaṁ so puriso'ti? 'Tatheva, deva, jīvatī'ti. Tamenaṁ rājā evaṁ vadeyya: 'gacchatha, bho, taṁ purisaṁ sāyanhasamayaṁ sattisatena hanathā'ti. Tamenaṁ sāyanhasamayaṁ sattisatena haneyyuṁ.*

"What do you think, bhikkhus? Would that man, being struck with three hundred spears, experience pain and displeasure on that account?"

*Taṁ kiṁ maññatha, bhikkhave, api nu so puriso divasaṁ tīhi sattisatehi haññamāno tatonidānaṁ dukkhaṁ domanassaṁ paṭisaṁvediyethā"ti?*

"Bhante, even if he were struck with one spear, he would experience pain and displeasure on that account, not to speak of three hundred spears."

*"Ekissāpi, bhante, sattiyā haññamāno tatonidānaṁ dukkhaṁ domanassaṁ paṭisaṁvediyetha; ko pana vādo tīhi sattisatehi haññamāno"ti.*

"It is in such a way, bhikkhus, that I say the nutriment consciousness should be seen. When the nutriment consciousness is fully understood, name-and-form is fully understood. When name-and-form is fully understood, I say, there is nothing further that a noble disciple needs to do."

*"Evameva khvāhaṁ, bhikkhave, viññāṇāhāro daṭṭhabboti vadāmi. Viññāṇe, bhikkhave, āhāre pariññāte nāmarūpaṁ pariññātaṁ hoti, nāmarūpe pariññāte ariyasāvakassa natthi kiñci uttarikaraṇīyanti vadāmī"ti.\*

## Pillar III: Transcending the \"Cave\" of Views (Mental Deconstruction)

Pillar III gathers three suttas from the *Atthakavagga* into a single technical trajectory: the **deconstruction of subtle attachment to the loka**. Here, the target is no longer only coarse attachment, but the **subtle structures of moha, diṭṭhi, and saññā** through which the worldly becoming is sustained. The work is not additive---it is **extractive**, removing layer by layer the grounds on which the defiled citta abides.

The *Guhaṭṭhaka Sutta (Snp 4.2)* establishes the starting point: the being "in the cave," covered and immersed in **moha**. This "cave" is not a place but a condition---**deep immersion in constructed reality from the world**, where one remains far from **vivekā (seclusion)**. The sutta operates as a full diagnostic: it identifies how the being is bound within the structure of worldly existence, and then outlines the **progressive disengagement** required. Each verse functions operationally, moving from worldly entanglement toward worldly **non-abiding**, where neither this world nor the next world is taken as a ground. The direction is clear: exit is achieved by **transcending** the world.

The *Pāsūra Sutta (Snp 4.8)* advances this by exposing the **intellectual dimension of the cave**. Here, the binding force is **view (diṭṭhi)**. The error is: taking view as purity (suddhi), and asserting "this is the only purity." From this, disputation arises. Each party, "leaning" (nissita) on its position, constructs **individualized truths (paccekasacca)** and enters into conflict, seeking **praise (pasaṁsā)**. The sutta shows that this entire process---adoption, defense, victory, defeat---is driven by **attachment and measurement/ conceit (māna)**, not by liberation. Even success produces further **māna (conceit)** and becomes a ground for distress. Seeing this, the instruction is to **withdraw from the field of dispute altogether**, since it is rooted in grasping. The intellect, when appropriated, becomes another enclosure.

The *Māgandiya Sutta (Snp 4.9)* completes the pillar by removing the **final supports of that enclosure**. It begins with the rejection of **coarse stickiness**---no *chanda* even for sexual intercourse, having seen **taṇhā, arati, and rāgā**. But it does not stop there. It directly dismantles the assumption that purity is achieved through **view, tradition, knowledge, or precepts and vows (sīlabbata)**. At the same time, it blocks the opposite extreme: purity is not attained by their absence either. What remains is precise: transcendence. And the goal is **peace (santo), independent (anissāya), one should not crave for worldly becoming (bhavaṁ na jappe)**.

The sutta then defines the mode of living: **secluded (vivitto) from those views**, not seizing or asserting them. Like a **water-born lotus (jalambuja)** not tainted by water or mud, the Sage is **anūpalitto**---not tainted/ defiled by sensualities and the world. This is the condition of a **frictionless citta**: present in the world, yet not adhering to it. The **Vedagū** (Master of Veda, one of the title Gautama the Supreme Arhant also bears) does not measure by view or thought, is not led by kamma or tradition, and is not established in mental abodes. With **saññā-viratta** (dispassionate /detached (*viratta*) toward perception/ mental formation (Saññā)), there are no ties (ganthā); with **paññā-vimutta** (liberation from wisdom), there are no delusions (mohā). In contrast, those who grasp views and perceptions move around in the world and **collide (ghaṭṭayantā)** with others.

Taken together, the three suttas form a single system:

- *Guhaṭṭhaka*: identifies the **cave of moha** and maps the extraction from total immersion.

- *Pāsūra*: exposes **views as structures of entanglement**, sustained by dispute and praise.

- *Māgandiya*: removes **all remaining grounds of grasping**, establishing non-adherence and ending craving for worldly becoming.

Pillar III therefore defines **mental deconstruction as the path of exit** from the world. The loka is not transcended by replacing one view with another, nor by refining knowledge, nor by relying on precepts. It is transcended when the citta **no longer grasps, no longer leans, and no longer adheres to the worldly**---when it is **peace (santo), independent (anissāya), and no longer moving toward worldly becoming (bhava)**.

### Guhaṭṭhaka Sutta (Snp 4.2): The Octad on the Cave

This sutta presents a **complete diagnostic and extraction model** of worldly existence, structured as a movement from **total immersion (moha)** toward **final non-abiding beyond both this world and the next**. Each verse is **operational**, showing the mechanics of bondage and the precise counter-movements required for release.

**1. The Cave as Total Immersion in Moha**

The "cave" is not merely a metaphor---it is a **functional enclosure**. It signifies the **condition of being covered (bahunābhichanno)**, where the Citta is **submerged in moha**, unable to distinguish itself from the aggregates and the world (loka).

Vivekā here is introduced as the **counter-force**:

- Not withdrawal in a passive sense, but **active separation through discernment**.

- The inability to abandon sexual pleasures is not moral weakness---it is **structural entanglement**. These pleasures act as **binding interfaces** that keep the Citta circulating within the world-system.

Thus, the first verse establishes: Without Vivekā, the Citta cannot even begin disengagement.

**2. Icchā as the Binding Mechanism of Bhava**

"Icchā-nidānā bhava-sāta-baddhā" identifies **desire (icchā)** as the **root-link (nidāna)** binding beings to the *pleasantness of worldly existence (bhava-sāta)*. Key structural points:

- **Baddhā (bound)**: this is **actual confinement within the system of worldly becoming**.

- **Duppamuñcā**: Release is difficult because the system is **self-reinforcing**---desire generates continuity.

- **Temporal projection (past/future)**: The mind oscillates across time, but always in relation to **kāma**.

Thus: The being is not only bound to pleasure---but bound to **the continuity of seeking pleasure across time**.

**3. Cognitive Collapse Under Kāma**

Here the sutta shows the **degradation of cognition and ethics** under sexual captivity:

- **Giddhā / Pasutā / Pamūḷhā**: A progressive tightening---captivation → entanglement → delusion.

- **Avadāniyā / Niviṭṭhā**: The mind becomes **ungenerous and fixed in crookedness (visama)**.

The existential crisis emerges: "What shall we become after passing away?"

**4. Sikkhā as Immediate Correction**

"Tasmā hi sikkhetha idheva jantu" --- training must occur **here**. Critical instruction:

- Recognize **visama** (the crooked, uneven, misaligned) in the world.

- Do **not act in accordance with it**, even when driven by pleasure.

Why urgency?

- **"Appañhidaṁ jīvitaṁ"** --- life is short in a **functional window for disengagement**.

Thus: Sīla here functions exactly as **withdrawal from participation in the crooked structure of the world**.

**5. Universal Condition: Trembling in Bhava**

"Passāmi loke pariphandamānaṁ" --- this is a **direct seeing (passāmi)** of the entire population:

- **Pariphandamānaṁ**: trembling---indicating **instability under craving**.

- **Taṇha-gataṁ bhavesu**: beings are **carried within states of worldly becoming by craving**.

"Maccu-mukhe" (mouth of death):

- this is the **present condition of all beings**.

Thus: The world is not stable existence---it is **a field of beings already inside the process of death**.

**6. Mamāyita: The Engine of Struggle**

The simile of fish in a drying stream is precise:

- **Resource collapse + attachment = panic and struggling** **(phandamāne)**.

"Mamāyite" (mine-making) is identified as:

- The **appropriation mechanism** that converts neutral phenomena into **objects of struggle**.

Instruction:

- **Amamo careyya** --- live without "mine-ness." This is not philosophical---it is **a direct deconstruction of ownership-identity binding**.

Thus: Without dismantling "mine," the being remains structurally incapable of exiting worldly existence.

**7. Neutralization at the Sensory Interface**

This verse shifts to a **high-resolution operational level**:

- **Ubhosu antesu**: both extremes (worldly existence/non-existence)

- **Phassaṁ pariññāya**: fully understanding sensory contact---the **entry point of experience construction**.

Key mechanism: Do not act on what one internally recognizes as blameworthy (**yad-atta-garahī**).

Result: **Na lippatī diṭṭhasutesu** --- no stained by what is seen/heard.

Thus: Liberation work occurs at the **moment of contact**, before proliferation.

**8. Final Crossing: From Saññā to Non-Longing**

The final verse completes the trajectory:

- **Saññā pariññā**: perception itself is fully understood, be **seen as constructed labeling**.

- **Vitareyya oghaṁ**: this enables crossing the flood of craving and involuntary rebirth.

Critical characteristics of the Muni:

- **Nopalitto pariggahesu**: no contamination by possession/grasping.

- **Abbūḷhasallo**: the arrow (craving) is extracted at the root.

Final marker: **Nāsīsatī lokamimaṁ parañca** --- does not long for this world or the next.

Not only disengagement from the present world---but **complete non-orientation toward any form of worldly existence**.

This sutta outlines an exact progression:

1.  **Immersion** (moha, cave)

2.  **Binding** (icchā → bhava)

3.  **Distortion** (kāma → delusion → crookedness)

4.  **Training** (recognize and refuse visama)

5.  **Direct seeing** (all beings in death-bound trembling)

6.  **Deconstruction** ("mine" toward the worldly removed)

7.  **Sensory mastery** (phassa understood, no adhesion/attachment)

8.  **Final release** (no longing for any worldly)

This is a complete architecture of liberation:\
**from enclosure → to separation → to non-abiding.**

#### SNP 4.2 Guhaṭṭhaka Sutta (The Octad on Cave)

1\. \[Like\] a being in cave is very much covered, A person (may) remain deeply immersed in moha (ignorance and delusion); For such a one is indeed far from seclusion (vivekā), For sexual pleasures in the world are truly not easily abandoned.

Satto guhāyaṁ bahunābhichanno, Tiṭṭhaṁ naro mohanasmiṁ pagāḷho; Dūre vivekā hi tathāvidho so, Kāmā hi loke na hi suppahāyā.

Comment: **Vivekā: The Power of Sifting and Separation**

**Vivekā** is the process of the **Citta** pulling away from the manifestations of the world. It is the active distinguishment between what is worldly and what is transcendent.

**The Worldly Immersed State vs. Vivekā**

A being \"in the cave\" is overwhelmed and covered. This immersion is the opposite of Vivekā.

- **The Cave as the Fog of Moha:** The cave symbolizes the physical body and the world (**loka**). When a person remains deeply immersed in **moha**, they are bound to the world because they cannot discriminate between the **Citta** and the worldly **dhamma**.

- **The Bondage of Pleasures:** The verse notes that sexual pleasures are not easily abandoned. These are the strongest \"clinging\" forces that keep the **Citta** anchored to involuntary reincarnation.

**Vivekā as Functional Discrimination**

Vivekā is the function of **Paññā** that begins the \"sifting\" process. It is the clarity required to see that:

1)  **Everything** belongs to this world contains the laws of cause and effect and the related laws of death. Because they belong to the world, they rise and fall, which is **dukkha**.

2)  **The Citta is the Observer:** Vivekā allows the **Citta** to stop identifying with the fog of the cave. The **Citta** recognizes that these worldly things belong to **loka**; consequently, the **Citta** will disenchant them and leave them behind.

2\. Bound (baddhā) by the desire (Icchā-nidānā) for pleasure (sāta) in worldly existence (bhava), They are difficult to release (Duppamuñcā), for there is indeed no other liberation (añña-mokkhā); Looking (Apekkhamānā) back \[to the past\] (Pacchā) or forward \[to the future\] (Pure), They long for (Jappa) these present sexual pleasures (kāme) or for those of the past.

Icchā-nidānā bhava-sāta-baddhā, Te duppamuñcā na hi añña-mokkhā; Pacchā pure vāpi apekkhamānā, Ime va kāme purime va jappaṁ.

3\. Captivated (Giddhā) in sexual pleasures (Kāmesu), attached (Pasutā) and blinded by **moha** (pamūḷhā), They are stingy (Avadāniyā) and settled (Niviṭṭhā) in the uneven/ crooked/ wrong \[way\]; Led unto dukkha (Dukkha-ūpanītā), they lament (Paridevayanti): \"What shall we become after passing away from here?\"

Kāmesu giddhā pasutā pamūḷhā, Avadāniyā te visame niviṭṭhā; Dukkhūpanītā paridevayanti, Kiṁsū bhavissāma ito cutāse.

4\. Therefore, a person should train (Sikkhetha) right here \[in this life\], Whatever one may know as \"uneven/ crooked/ unrighteous\"(Visamanti) in the world (Loke); One should not live (Careyya) the uneven way for the sake of that \[pleasure\], For the wise (Dhīrā) say this life (Jīvita) is indeed brief (Appañhidaṁ).

Tasmā hi sikkhetha idheva jantu, Yaṁ kiñci jaññā visamantiloke; Na tassa hetū visamaṁ careyya, Appañhidaṁ jīvitamāhu dhīrā.

5\. I see (Passāmi) this generation (Paja) in the world (loke) trembling (pariphandamāna), Caught in craving (taṇha-gata) for states of worldly existence (bhavesu); Inferior (Hīnā) people (Narā) lament (Lapanti) in the mouth of death (Maccu-mukhe), Their craving (taṇhāse) for becoming and re-becoming (Bhavā-bhavesu) not yet gone (Avīta).

Passāmi loke pariphandamānaṁ, Pajaṁ imaṁ taṇhagataṁ bhavesu; Hīnā narā maccumukhe lapanti, Avītataṇhāse bhavābhavesu.

6\. See (Passatha) those struggling (Phandamāne) over things they call \"mine (Mamāyite),\" Like fish (Maccheva) in a stream (Appodake) with little water and a dried-up stream (Khīṇa-sote); Having seen (Disvā) this too, one should live (Careyya) without \"mine-ness (Amamo),\" Not forming attachment (Āsatti-m-akubbamāno) to states of worldly existence (Bhavesu).

Mamāyite passatha phandamāne, Maccheva appodake khīṇasote; Etampi disvā amamo careyya, Bhavesu āsattimakubbamāno.

7\. Having removed (Vineyya) wish (Chanda) for both (Ubhosu) extremes \[worldly existence/non-existence\] (Antesu), Having fully understood (Pariññāya) sensory contact (Phassa), being not captivated (Anānu-giddho); Not doing that (Tad-akubbamāno) which he would reproach in himself (Yad-atta-garahī), The wise one (Dhīrā) is not tainted (Lippatī) by what is seen (Diṭṭha) or heard (sutesu).

Ubhosu antesu vineyya chandaṁ, Phassaṁ pariññāya anānugiddho; Yadattagarahī tadakubbamāno, Na lippatī diṭṭhasutesu dhīro. 

8\. Having fully understood (Pariññā) perception/ recognition/ mental labeling (Sañña), he would cross (Vitareyya) the flood (Ogha, the flood of craving and involuntary reincarnation); The Sage (Muni), not tainted (Nopalitto) by possessions/ grabbings (Pariggahesu); With the arrow (salla) pulled out (Abbūḷha), living (Cara) zealously (appamatto), He does not long for (Nāsīsatī) this world (Lokam-ima) or the next (Parañca).

Saññaṁ pariññā vitareyya oghaṁ, Pariggahesu muni nopalitto; Abbūḷhasallo caramappamatto, Nāsīsatī lokamimaṁ parañcāti. 

### Pāsūra Sutta (Snp 4.8): The Futility of Views and the Mechanics of Disputation

The *Pasūra Sutta* exposes with precision a recurring structural error in the human world: the confusion of **view (diṭṭhi)** with **purity (suddhi)**. From the opening verse, the problem is not merely disagreement---it is the fixation on *"this is the only purity"*, accompanied by the dismissal of all other teachings. Each party, "leaning" (nissita) on its chosen ground, beautifies its own position while becoming entrenched in *individualized truths* (paccekasacca). This is not a search for liberation; it is the construction of intellectual territories.

What is being revealed here is a **psychological mechanism of attachment**. A view is first adopted, then inhabited, and finally defended as identity. Once this identification is complete, the view is no longer a tool---it becomes a *self-extension*. Thus, to challenge the view is experienced as a personal attack. This explains why, in the second and third verses, debate quickly degenerates into mutual accusations of foolishness, emotional agitation, and the craving for validation. The assembly (parisā) becomes a theatre, not of truth, but of **status competition**.

The sutta carefully maps the **emotional economy of disputation**. Entering debate, one desires praise; while speaking, one becomes tense; when refuted, one collapses into dejection; when criticized, one retaliates with anger and fault-finding. This oscillation---pride when affirmed, distress when defeated---is not incidental. It reveals that the true object being pursued is not truth, but **praise (pasaṁsā)**. Debate is thus exposed as a form of *sensual engagement at the level of the mind*, where recognition and superiority function as subtle nutriments.

Verses four and five deepen this diagnosis. When a position collapses under scrutiny, the one who held it does not relinquish attachment but instead falls into lamentation: "He has surpassed me." The concern is comparative, not liberative. The sutta then generalizes: *these disputes among samaṇas inevitably produce both pride and dejection*. This duality itself is the mark of entanglement. Seeing this, the instruction is not to refine debate, but to **abstain from it altogether**, since its underlying purpose is merely the acquisition of praise.

The sixth and seventh verses expose the **hidden cost of victory**. Even when one "wins" and is praised, the outcome is not purification but inflation---laughter, arrogance, and conceit (māna, atimāna). This pride becomes the very ground for future distress (vighātabhūmi). Thus, both defeat and victory bind equally. The structure is closed: whether one rises or falls within the field of views, one remains within the same domain of agitation. Therefore, the wise (kusalā) do not recognize purity as arising from such processes.

The striking simile in verse eight---the hero nourished by the king's food---illustrates how disputants become **addicted to confrontation**. Empowered and sustained by recognition, they roam in search of opponents. Yet the sutta dismantles this posture by pointing out that the "battle" itself has no inherent necessity. Without the prior establishment of opposition, there is nothing to fight. The hero's identity depends entirely on the existence of rivals.

In verse nine, a strategic instruction is given: when encountering those who assert "this is the only truth," one should not engage on their terms. Instead, one points out the absence of a true opponent. This is not an evasive move but a **deconstruction of the dualistic framework** that sustains debate. If no "other" is established as a rival, the structure of contention collapses.

Verse ten presents the contrast: those who have **disbanded the army (visenikatvā)**---that is, dismantled the forces of defilement---do not oppose view with view. They no longer operate within the economy of positions. For them, nothing is grasped as "supreme" (paramuggahīta). This absence of grasping is crucial. It does not mean the absence of discernment, but the absence of **appropriation**. Without appropriation, there is no ground for dispute.

The final verse brings the sutta to its decisive conclusion. Pasūra, representing the speculative mind, is described as one who "spins theories" (pavitakka), mentally proliferating views and then confronting the *Purified One (dhona)*. But this confrontation is fundamentally unequal. The one who is free from defilements does not stand within the same plane as the one constructing views. Therefore, the sutta declares plainly: **you cannot keep pace with him**. The purified one is not operating within the domain of conceptual contestation at all.

Taken as a whole, the *Pasūra Sutta* is not merely a critique of argumentation; it is a **systematic exposure of how the mind binds itself through views**. It shows that the entire structure of debate---adoption, defense, victory, defeat---is rooted in craving, conceit, and the search for validation. Purity, therefore, cannot arise from any position that is grasped, defended, or opposed.

The implication is direct: **liberation requires a withdrawal from the field of views as objects of attachment**. As long as one leans on any constructed standpoint and seeks affirmation through it, one remains within the oscillation of gain and loss, praise and blame. The one who sees this clearly does not refine their argument---they step out of the arena altogether.

#### SNP 4.8 Pasūra Sutta

1\. \"This is purity,\" so they say; They say there is no purity in others' teachings. Whatever they are leaning on, they call that \"beautiful,\" Settled in their various individual \"truths.\"

Idheva suddhī iti vādayanti, Nāññesu dhammesu visuddhimāhu; Yaṁ nissitā tattha subhaṁ vadānā, Paccekasaccesu puthū niviṭṭhā.

2\. Desiring debate, they plunge into the assembly, And mutually branding each other as \"fools\"; Leaning on others, they engage in debate, Desiring praise, claiming to be experts.

Te vādakāmā parisaṁ vigayha, Bālaṁ dahantī mithu aññamaññaṁ; Vadanti te aññasitā kathojjaṁ, Pasaṁsakāmā kusalā vadānā.

3\. Engaged in talk in the middle of the assembly, Desiring praise, he becomes distressed; But when his argument is defeated, he becomes downcast, He gets angry at the criticism, searching for flaws \[in others\].

Yutto kathāyaṁ parisāya majjhe, Pasaṁsamicchaṁ vinighāti hoti; Apāhatasmiṁ pana maṅku hoti, Nindāya so kuppati randhamesī.

4\. When those who examine the questions say his argument is lost, When it has been struck down \[refuted\]; The one whose argument is defeated laments and grieves; He wails, \"He has surpassed me.\"

Yamassa vādaṁ parihīnamāhu, Apāhataṁ pañhavimaṁsakāse; Paridevati socati hīnavādo, Upaccagā manti anutthunāti.

5\. These disputes have arisen among the samaṇas, In them \[these disputes\], there is elation and distress; Having seen this too, one should abstain from debate, For there is no other purpose than the gain of praise.

Ete vivādā samaṇesu jātā, Etesu ugghātinighāti hoti; Etampi disvā virame kathojjaṁ, Na haññadatthatthi pasaṁsalābhā.

6\. And if he is praised there, Having explained his argument in the midst of the assembly; He laughs and becomes arrogant because of that, Having attained that goal as his mind \[had intended\].

Pasaṁsito vā pana tattha hoti, Akkhāya vādaṁ parisāya majjhe; So hassatī unnamatī ca tena, Pappuyya tamatthaṁ yathā mano ahu.

7\. That pride is the ground for his distress, For he speaks with conceit and arrogance; Having seen this also, one should not dispute, For the experts indeed do not say that purity is \[attained\] by that.

Yā unnatī sāssa vighātabhūmi, Mānātimānaṁ vadate paneso; Etampi disvā na vivādayetha, Na hi tena suddhiṁ kusalā vadanti.

8\. Like a hero nourished by the king\'s food, He goes about roaring, seeking a rival hero; Go where that \[rival\] is, O \"hero\"! Before this, there was nothing of that which is for a fight.

Sūro yathā rājakhādāya puṭṭho, Abhigajjameti paṭisūramicchaṁ; Yeneva so tena palehi sūra, Pubbeva natthi yadidaṁ yudhāya.

9\. Those who, having grasped a view, dispute, and maintain, \"This is the only truth\"; You should speak to them: \"There is no one here \[among us\], To be a rival when a dispute has arisen.\"

Ye diṭṭhimuggayha vivādayanti, Idameva saccanti ca vādayanti; Te tvaṁ vadassū na hi tedha atthi, Vādamhi jāte paṭisenikattā.

10\. But those who live having disbanded the army \[of defilements\], Not opposing view with view; What would you gain \[by challenging\] them, Pasūra? For whom there is nothing here grasped as the \"supreme.\"

Visenikatvā pana ye caranti, Diṭṭhīhi diṭṭhiṁ avirujjhamānā; Tesu tvaṁ kiṁ labhetho pasūra, Yesīdha natthī paramuggahītaṁ.

11\. Then you came, spinning theories, Contemplating various views in your mind; You have come into confrontation with the Purified One, Indeed, you will not be able to keep pace with him.

Atha tvaṁ pavitakkamāgamā, Manasā diṭṭhigatāni cintayanto; Dhonena yugaṁ samāgamā, Na hi tvaṁ sakkhasi sampayātaveti.

### Magandiya Sutta (Snp 4.9): The Mechanics of the Frictionless Citta

The **Māgandiya Sutta** (Snp 4.9) stands as a definitive technical manual for the **ancient path of liberation**. It moves from the coarse rejection of physical \"stickiness\" (sexual desire) to the subtle eradication of mental \"stickiness\" (views and perceptions). It begins with the body and ends with views and perception, showing how the citta becomes **non-adherent (anūpalitto)**.

The first verse establishes the initial condition. Having seen **Craving (taṇhā), Aversion (arati), and Passion (rāgā)**, there is no wish (*chanda*) even for sexual intercourse. The body is recognized as "filled with urine and excrement," and thus it is no longer taken up as an object of contact. The statement that one would not touch it even with the foot indicates the absence of **physical stickiness**---there is nothing there for the mind to adhere to.

Māgandiya immediately shifts the ground. What is rejected as impure is, for him, a "precious thing," desired by many rulers. When sensual valuation fails, he turns to another structure: *view (diṭṭhigata), virtue and vows (sīlavata), lifestyle (jīvita), and rebirth (bhavūpapatti)*. This marks the transition from **gross attachment** to **subtle attachment**. If the body is no longer held, then perhaps a system, a view, or a future state can be held.

The reply of the Sage blocks this movement. It does not occur to him to say, *"I assert this."* There is no taking up of any "settled conclusion" among dhammas. Instead, **seeing in views (diṭṭhīsu)** without grasping them, he saw **inner peace (ajjhattasanti)**. The mechanism is exact: views are not denied, but they are not seized. Without seizing, there is no adhesion. Without adhesion, the citta does not generate friction.

Māgandiya presses further, asking how such "inner peace" is to be understood if no formulated conclusions are grasped. The answer is structurally complete. Purity (suddhi) is not by **view, tradition, knowledge, or precepts and vows**; yet it is not by their absence either. This double exclusion prevents the mind from relocating its attachment. It cannot adhere to possession, nor can it adhere to rejection. The field for stickiness is removed on both sides.

What remains is the operative instruction: **relinquishing these, without grasping, calm (santo), independent (anissāya), one should not crave for worldly becoming (bhavaṁ na jappe)**. Here the citta is described in functional terms. It is **calm** because it does not cling; **independent** because it does not rely on worldly conditioning; and it does not move toward more worldly **becoming (bhava)** because there is nothing in the world being appropriated. This is the state of **frictionlessness**.

Māgandiya's confusion arises from continued reliance on views. Questioning proceeds from what has already been grasped, and therefore confusion persists within that grasping. From that position, even "the slightest notion" is not seen. The problem is not lack of information; it is **adhesion to what is already held**.

The sutta then identifies another layer of stickiness: **pride**/**measurement (māna)**. To think in terms of "equal," "superior," or "inferior" is already to establish contact. These three modes produce **friction**, and from friction, dispute arises. One who does not move within these three does not enter into dispute, because there is no basis for comparison.

The description of the brāhmaṇa and the wandering Sage continues this same line. Without "equal" or "uneven," there is no one to oppose. Having left the home, without fixed abode, not forming ties, empty of sexual pleasures, and not led by future desires, the Sage has no surface on which friction can arise. Engagement in dispute requires contact; here, contact has been removed.

The image of the **water-born lotus** gives the exact formulation. Though it exists in water and mud, it is **not tainted (anūpalitto)** by them. In the same way, the Sage, a **speaker of peace (santivādo)**, without attachment (agiddho), is not tainted by sensualities and the world. This is not withdrawal from the world, but **non-adherence within it**.

The final verses define the structure of a fully frictionless citta. The **Vedagū** does not go to measurement by view or thought, for he is "not made of that" (na hi tammayo). He is not led by action /kamma or by tradition (suta), and is not brought into mental abodes (nivesana). There is no settling, no lodging, no fixation. For one detached from perception (saññā-viratta), there are no ties (ganthā); for one liberated through wisdom (paññā-vimutta), there are no delusions (mohā).

In contrast, those who grasp perception and views move about in the world **colliding (ghaṭṭayantā)** with others. This collision is the direct opposite of frictionlessness---it is the result of continual contact, grasping, and reactivity.

The sutta, taken as a whole, outlines a complete progression:\
from the removal of **stickiness to the body**,\
to the removal of **stickiness to views**,\
to the removal of **stickiness to perception itself**.

When nothing is seized---neither body, nor view, nor perception---the citta becomes **anūpalitto**: not sticking, not colliding, not moving toward world becoming. This is the operational condition of **ajjhattasanti**, inner peace (**santa**) and independence (**anissāya**), ready to exit this world and go to the deathless realm of Nibbana.

#### SNP 4.9 Māgandiya Sutta

1\. \"Having seen Craving (*Taṇhā*), Aversion (*Arati*), and Passion (*Rāgā*), There was no wish/desire (*chanda*) even for sexual intercourse. What is this \[body\] anyway, filled with urine and excrement? I would not want to touch it even with my foot.\"

"Disvāna taṇhaṁ aratiṁ ragañca, Nāhosi chando api methunasmiṁ; Kimevidaṁ muttakarīsapuṇṇaṁ, Pādāpi naṁ samphusituṁ na icche".

2\. \"If you do not wish such a precious thing, a woman sought after by many lords of men; Then what kind of view, virtue and vows, lifestyle, and future rebirth do you assert?\"

"Etādisañce ratanaṁ na icchasi, Nāriṁ narindehi bahūhi patthitaṁ; Diṭṭhigataṁ sīlavataṁ nu jīvitaṁ, Bhavūpapattiñca vadesi kīdisaṁ".

3\. \"It does not occur to him \[the Sage\] to say, \'I assert this\' (O Māgaṇḍiya, said the Blessed One), Regarding any settled conclusion grasped among the **dhammas**; But seeing \[the danger\] in views, without grasping \[any\], Searching, I saw inner peace.\"

"Idaṁ vadāmīti na tassa hoti, *(māgaṇḍiyāti bhagavā),* Dhammesu niccheyya samuggahītaṁ; Passañca diṭṭhīsu anuggahāya, Ajjhattasantiṁ pacinaṁ adassaṁ".

4\. \"Those settled conclusions that have been formulated, You, O Sage, say you do not grasp them; (said Māgaṇḍiya). This goal that is called \'inner peace\'--- How indeed is that proclaimed by the wise?\"

"Vinicchayā yāni pakappitāni, Te ve munī brūsi anuggahāya; *(iti māgaṇḍiyo).* Ajjhattasantīti yametamatthaṁ, Kathaṁ nu dhīrehi paveditaṁ taṁ".

5\. \"Not by view, nor by tradition, nor by knowledge,\" (O Māgaṇḍiya, said the Blessed One), \"nor by precepts and vows (*sīlabbata*) does one speak of purity (*suddhi*). Yet not by lack of view, lack of tradition, lack of knowledge, nor by lack of virtue and vows is it \[purity\] attained. Relinquishing these without grasping, Calmed (santo) and independent (anissāya), one should not crave for worldly becoming (bhava).\"

"Na diṭṭhiyā na sutiyā na ñāṇena, *(māgaṇḍiyāti bhagavā),* Sīlabbatenāpi na suddhimāha; Adiṭṭhiyā assutiyā añāṇā, Asīlatā abbatā nopi tena; Ete ca nissajja anuggahāya, Santo anissāya bhavaṁ na jappe".

6\. \"If indeed not by view, nor by tradition, nor by knowledge,\" (said Māgaṇḍiya), \"Nor by virtue and vows does one claim purity; And yet not by lack of view, lack of tradition, lack of knowledge, Nor by lack of virtue and vows is it \[purity\] attained; Then I consider this doctrine to be sheer confusion, For some \[other teachers\] believe that purity is by means of a view.\"

"No ce kira diṭṭhiyā na sutiyā na ñāṇena, *(iti māgaṇḍiyo),* Sīlabbatenāpi na suddhimāha; Adiṭṭhiyā assutiyā añāṇā, Asīlatā abbatā nopi tena; Maññāmahaṁ momuhameva dhammaṁ, Diṭṭhiyā eke paccenti suddhiṁ".

7\. \"Relying on your views, you keep asking questions,\" (O Māgaṇḍiya, said the Blessed One), \"You have fallen into confusion regarding those things you have grasped; From this \[perspective\], you have not perceived even the slightest notion \[of truth\], Therefore, you regard \[my teaching\] as sheer confusion.\"

"Diṭṭhañca nissāya anupucchamāno, *(māgaṇḍiyāti bhagavā),* Samuggahītesu pamohamāgā; Ito ca nāddakkhi aṇumpi saññaṁ, Tasmā tuvaṁ momuhato dahāsi.

8\. \"One who thinks himself \'equal,\' \'superior,\' or \'inferior,\' By that \[very thought\], he would enter into disputes; But regarding one who does not quiver in this three modes \[of comparison\], The thought \'equal\' or \'superior\' does not occur to him.\"

Samo visesī uda vā nihīno, Yo maññati so vivadetha tena; Tīsu vidhāsu avikampamāno, Samo visesīti na tassa hoti.

9\. \"That **brāhmaṇa**, what would he say is \'the truth\'? Or with whom would he dispute, saying \'it is a lie\'? In whom there is no \'equal\' or \'uneven,\' With whom would he join in a dispute?\"

Saccanti so brāhmaṇo kiṁ vadeyya, Musāti vā so vivadetha kena; Yasmiṁ samaṁ visamaṁ vāpi natthi, Sa kena vādaṁ paṭisaṁyujeyya.

10\. \"Having left behind the home, wandering without a fixed abode, In the village, the Sage does not form intimate ties; Empty of sexual pleasures, not looking forward \[to anything\] / not being \"led\" by future desires, How would he engage in disputes with people?\"

Okaṁ pahāya aniketasārī, Gāme akubbaṁ muni santhavāni; Kāmehi ritto apurakkharāno, Kathaṁ na viggayha janena kayirā.

11\. \"Separated/ secluded (Vivitto) from those \[views\] (Yehi), one should live (Vicareyya) in the world (loke), A **Nāga** would not seize (udgṛhya) and assert/ speak (Vadeyya) those \[views\] (Tāni). Just as (yathā) a water-born \[lotus\] (Jalambuja), though thorny-stalked (*kaṇḍaka*), Is not tainted (ca-anūpalitta) by water (Jalena) or mud (Paṅkena); Even so the Sage (munī), the Proclaimer/ Speaker of Peace (Santivādo), without stickiness/ viscosity / worldly attachment (Agiddho, agṛdhya), Is not tainted (Anūpalitto) by sexualities (Kāme) and the world (loke).\"

Yehi vivitto vicareyya loke, Na tāni uggayha vadeyya nāgo; Jalambujaṁ kaṇḍakavārijaṁ yathā, Jalena paṅkena canūpalittaṁ; Evaṁ munī santivādo agiddho, Kāme ca loke ca anūpalitto.

12\. \"The **Vedagū** (Master of Veda, one of the title Gautama the Supreme Arhant also bears) does not go to conceit/measurement (**māna**) by view/ theory (Diṭṭhi) or by thought/ opinion (Mati, muti), (The **Vedagū** does not use his views to measure himself against others), For he is not made of that (*tad* + *maya*); He is not led by action/ **kamma** nor by tradition (*suta*), He is not brought into (Anūpanīto) mental abodes (*nivesana*).\"

Na vedagū diṭṭhiyāyako na mutiyā, Sa mānameti na hi tammayo so; Na kammunā nopi sutena neyyo, Anūpanīto sa nivesanesu.

13\. \"For one \[**Vedagū**\] is dispassionate /detached (*viratta*) toward perception/ mental formation (Saññā), there are no knots/ ties (ganthā); For one liberated through wisdom (Paññā-vimutta), there are no delusions (mohā); But those who have grasped at perception/ mental formation and views, They wander in the world (vicaranti loke), colliding with others (Te ghaṭṭayantā).\"

Saññā-virattassa na santi ganthā, Paññā-vimuttassa na santi mohā; Saññañca diṭṭhiñca ye aggahesuṁ, Te ghaṭṭayantā vicaranti loke"ti. 

## Pillar IV: The Realization of the Arahant (The Way to Nibbāna-Dhātu)

The culmination of the path taught by Gautama the Supreme Arahant is the realization of Arahantship: the complete liberation of the citta from the conditioned world and its transition toward Nibbāna-Dhātu, the Deathless Realm. This pillar defines both the destination and the final transformation required for transcendence beyond the loka. The path is not merely psychological purification or ethical refinement within worldly existence, but the complete exit from the conditioned universe itself.

The *Muni Sutta (Snp 1.12)* presents the arahant as the true sage (muni): one who has withdrawn from entanglement with the world, severed craving, restrained speech, abandoned violence, and no longer participates in the compulsive fabrications that sustain worldly becoming. The muni moves silently through the world without clinging, detached from possessions, views, identity, and sensual attachment. This withdrawal is the necessary disentanglement of the citta from the gravitational pull of saṃsāric existence.

The *Dhammapada* reinforces this vision by repeatedly emphasizing that the world is governed by impermanence (aniccā), instability, craving, hatred, delusion, and death. The ordinary mind remains trapped because it delights in conditioned phenomena, identifies with fleeting dhammas, and continually seeks satisfaction within what is unstable and perishable. The Dhammapada therefore directs the practitioner toward mindfulness, restraint, wisdom, and purification of mind so that the citta gradually ceases to bind itself to the conditioned world.

This liberation reaches its full meaning in the revelation of Nibbāna-Dhātu found in *Itivuttaka 43 Ajāta Sutta* and the *Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta*. Here Gautama declares the existence of the realm of the unborn (Ajāta), unArise/unmanifest/unbecome (Abhūta), unmade (Akata), and unconditioned (Asaṅkhata). This realm exists entirely beyond the structures of worldly becoming. Unlike all saṅkhāra---constructed phenomena held together by craving and conditions---Nibbāna-Dhātu has no dependence upon causes, no arising, no decay, no fragmentation, and no death.

Because the Asaṅkhata exists, escape from the conditioned world is possible. If there were no unborn and unconditioned realm, no liberation from the born, made, and conditioned could ever be discerned. Thus the realization of the arahant is not annihilation into nonexistence, but the liberation of the citta from the conditioned universe and its release into the Deathless Realm (Amata-Dhātu).

The *Parāyana Sutta* further defines this destination through the thirty-three qualities of Nibbāna-Dhātu. It is the Other Shore (Pāra), the Refuge (Saraṇa), the Shelter (Leṇa), the Island (Dīpa), and the Final Destination (Parāyana). It is the Permanent (Dhuva), the Undecaying (Ajajjara), the Non-manifestive (Anidassana), the Unproliferated (Nippapañca), and the Deathless (Amata). These qualities collectively establish that Nibbāna is not the cessation of suffering inside the world, but transcendence beyond the entire conditioned world itself.

The realization of the arahant therefore represents the final completion of the training triad of Sīla--Samādhi--Paññā and the liberation triad of Nibbidā--Virāga--Vimutti. Through Sīla, the practitioner withdraws from participation in worldly entanglement. Through Samādhi, the citta becomes concentrated, elevated, and disentangled from sensory domination. Through Paññā, the conditioned nature of all worldly dhammas is fully penetrated. This gives rise to Nibbidā (disenchantment toward the world), Virāga (fading of attachment to the world), and finally Vimutti (liberation from the world).

At the moment of realization, the citta no longer seeks footing within conditioned becoming. Craving is extinguished, the taints are destroyed, and the mechanisms sustaining rebirth cease. The arahant recognizes directly: "My liberation is unshakeable; this is my last birth; now there is no renewal of worldly becoming." Thus the way to Nibbāna-Dhātu opens---the final transcendence of the conditioned loka and the realization of the Deathless Realm beyond the universe.

### Muni Sutta (Snp 1.12): The Sage's Exit from the Loka

The Muni Sutta defines the **Sage (Muni)** not by physical appearance, but by a radical severance from the laws of the universe (*loka*). The Sage is a \"Free Sovereign\" who has identified the \"glue\" of intimacy and the \"nesting\" of the mind as the primary sources of danger and defilement. By becoming **Aniketa** (homeless), the Citta refuses to \"set up house\" in any state of worldly existence.

The Sutta concludes with a stark contrast: the householder, bound by family and the \"labor-for-food\" cycle, is like a crested peacock. No matter how beautiful, it can never match the speed or the \"flight\" of the swan---the Sage---who practices Jhāna in the wild, walking on the **ancient path to Nibbāna**.

**The Sage's Vision:** \"Home-less and intimacy-less---this is indeed the Sage\'s insight. Having understood all dwellings of the mind, and not desiring even a single one of them, he is indeed a Sage, for he has transcended to the further shore.\"

#### SNP 1.12 Muni Sutta (The Sage)

1\. From intimacy (\"standing/establishing (*thū*) together (saṃ)\" of two entities---the \"glue\" of social and emotional bonding), fear/ danger (Bhaya) is born (Jāta); from a home (Niketā, dwelling/abode, nest), dust / passion/ defilement/ pollen (*rajas, the \"viscosity\" or \"agitation\" that accumulates wherever the mind has established a surface (a niketa)*) is produced; Home-less and intimacy-less---this is indeed the Sage\'s vision/ insight (dassana).

Santhavāto bhayaṁ jātaṁ, niketā jāyate rajo; Aniketamasanthavaṁ, etaṁ ve muni-dassanaṁ.

2\. He who, having uprooted (*ucchijja*) what has arisen /born (*jāta*), would not cause to grow (ropayeyya), And would not provide (anuppavecche) \[nourishment\] for it to be born/ arising (Jāyantam-assa); Him they call a Sage (Muni), living/ wandering (caranta) alone (Eka), The Great Seer (Mahesi, Maha-rṣi, Divine Human, Demi-god, 仙) who has seen (Addakkhi) the foundation of peace (Santi-pada, the realm of Nibbana).

Yo jātamucchijja na ropayeyya, Jāyantamassa na-anuppavecche; Tam-āhu ekaṁ muninaṁ carantaṁ, Addakkhi so santipadaṁ mahesi.

3\. Having thoroughly understood (Saṅkhāya) the base/ grounds (Vatthūni) \[of existence\] and crushed (Pamāya) the seed (Bīja), He would not provide (anuppavecche) \[it\] with moisture/ oil/grease/ affection/love (Sineham-assa); He is indeed a Sage (munī), who sees (dassī) the end (anta) of birth\'s destruction (jāti-khaya), Having left behind (Pahāya) speculation/ guess (Takka, \"turning over\" of a thought, is seen as a circular process---it can prove anything but realizes nothing. *Tarka* is used to remove doubts, but it cannot produce the \"Truth\" on its own. It is a \"supporting\" mechanism of the intellect (*Buddhi*)), he does not enter into an \'coordinate\'(saṅkha, Sanskrit: Saṃkhyā, it comes from **saṃ** (together) + **khyā** (to make known). a **Saṃkhyā** means to have a \"place in the order,\" an identification/labeling, can be a name, a form, or a number, it is the \"coordinate\" that allows the laws of death and birth to find you).

Saṅkhāya vatthūni pamāya bījaṁ, Sinehamassa nānuppavecche; Sa ve munī jāti-khaya-anta-dassī, Takkaṁ pahāya na upeti saṅkhaṁ.

4\. Having understood (Aññāya) all dwellings \[of the mind\] (Nivesanāni, In the **loka**, these are the points where the **Citta** tries to \"set up house\"---be it in a physical body, a feeling, a view, or a state of meditation), Not desiring (Anikāmaya) even a single one of them; He is indeed a Sage, free from this longing (Vītagedho) and without viscosity/ stickiness (Agiddho), He does not accumulate (āyūhati), for he has transcended /gone to the further shore (Pāragato).

Aññāya sabbāni nivesanāni, Anikāmayaṁ aññatarampi tesaṁ; Sa ve munī vītagedho agiddho, Nāyūhatī pāragato hi hoti.

5\. The Best (abhibhu) of all, the Knower (vidū) of all, the highly wise (sumedha) In all dhammas, untainted/undefiled (anu-upalitta); Having given up (jaha) all, liberated (vimutta) through the destruction of craving (taṇha-kkhaye)--- Him, indeed, the wise (Dhīrā) recognize as a Sage (Muni).

Sabba-abhibhuṁ sabba-viduṁ sumedhaṁ, Sabbesu dhammesu anu-upalittaṁ; Sabbañ-jahaṁ taṇha-kkhaye vimuttaṁ, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

6\. endowed with (Upapanna) the strength (bala) of wisdom (Paññā), precepts (sīla) and vows (vat), Concentrated (Samāhitaṁ), delighting in jhāna, mindful (sati); Released (pamutta) from group (saṅgā), without obstruction/ barrenness (akhila, *Khila* refers to the \"wasteland\" or \"stakes\" in the mind (stubbornness, hard-heartedness) that prevent the inner flow of Dhamma), without leakages/ outflows/ taints (Āsava, outflow, taints, defilements, is the \"leak\" of the Citta into the world) --- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Paññā-balaṁ sīla-vata-ūpapannaṁ, Samāhitaṁ jhānarataṁ satīmaṁ; Saṅgā pamuttaṁ akhilaṁ anāsavaṁ, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

7\. Living (caranta) alone (eka), the sage (muni) is zealous (appamatta), Unshaken (Avedhamāna) in blame and praise (Nindā-pasaṁsāsu); Like a lion not trembling at sounds, Like the wind not caught in a net, Like a lotus untainted by water; A leader of others (Netāraṁ-añña), not to be led by others (Anañña-neyya) --- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Ekaṁ carantaṁ munim-appamattaṁ, Nindā-pasaṁsāsu avedhamānaṁ; Sīhaṁva saddesu asantasantaṁ, Vātaṁva jālamhi asajjamānaṁ; Padmaṁva toyena alippamānaṁ, Netāramaññesamanaññaneyyaṁ; Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

Comment: "Ekaṁ carantaṁ" here is purposely translated to "Living alone" instead of the more popular "Wandering alone" in order to be more precise, de-emphasize the focus on physical movement. If we over-emphasize the \"traveling\" aspect, we risk missing the structural reality of the **Citta**. The literal spirit of this translation---and the mechanical reality of the **Ancient Path**---is about **solitary independence** and the **refusal to settle into the world\'s structures**, regardless of the body\'s physical coordinates.

**1). Solitude is the Primary Condition (Ekaṁ)**

Whether the Sage is in a cave for twenty years or walking across a continent, he is **Ekaṁ** (Alone).

- **The Internal Separation:** This isn\'t just physical isolation; it is the **Citta** having been sifted away from all worldly things.

- **Non-Collision:** Being \"alone\" means the mind is no longer \"clumping\" (*saṅga*) with the views, desires, or identities of others.

**2). \"Carantaṁ\" as \"Functioning\"**

In the Sanskritic sense, **√car** is often used to describe how a mechanism *operates* or how a person *conducts* themselves.

- **The Operating System:** **Carantaṁ** describes the Sage\'s \"operating frequency.\" He is \"faring\" through life in **Brahmacariya** standard.

- **Stationary but Faring:** A lighthouse doesn\'t move, but its light \"fares\" across the ocean. Similarly, a Sage can stay in one place, but his **Citta** is functioning in a state that is completely detached from the **loka**.

**3). The Rejection of \"Settling\" (Niketa)**

The prejudice in favor of \"wandering\" usually stems from a desire to avoid **Niketa** (the home/dwelling). The danger isn\'t physical; it\'s the **Citta** \"nesting\" in a state of existence. As long as the Sage is **Aniketa** (homeless in mind), his physical location is irrelevant.

8\. He who stands like a pillar in the flood (Ogahaṇe), When others speak to him with limit-reaching words (This refers to insults, praise, or complex arguments designed to \"reach the limit\" of one\'s patience or logic); Him, with rāga departed (Vīta-rāga) and senses well-concentrated (Su-samāhita-indriya)--- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Yo ogahaṇe thambhorivābhijāyati, Yasmiṁ pare vācāpariyantaṁ vadanti; Taṁ vītarāgaṁ susamāhitindriyaṁ, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

9\. He indeed (Yo ve) is established (ṭhita) in the True Self/Soul (atta/ātman) and upright /straight /truthful (ujju) like a shuttle (Tasaraṁ-va), Who loathes (Jigucchati) from unwholesome /evil actions (Kammehi pāpakehi); whose mind discriminates (Vīmaṁsa-māno) the uneven (Visama) and the even (Sama) (the dualistic way is the **civilized and noble (ariyan) mode of existence, lead to liberation**) --- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Yo ve ṭhita-Atto tasaraṁva ujju, Jigucchati kammehi pāpakehi; Vīmaṁsamāno visamaṁ samañca, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

10\. He who is self-restrained (Saññata-atto, saṃyamati) and does no unwholesome/ evil (Na karoti pāpa), Whether a young, middle-aged, or \[elder\] Sage, he is self-controlled/ disciplined/ striving (**Yata**-**Atto**); He is not irritable/ to be provoked (Arosaneyyo), nor does he provoke anyone (roseti kañci) --- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Yo saññatatto na karoti pāpaṁ, Daharo majjhimo ca muni yatatto; Arosaneyyo na so roseti kañci, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

11\. Whether from the best, the middle, or the remainder, He receives his alms-food (Piṇḍa), living on what is given by others (Para-datta-ūpajīvī); He is not prone to praise (**Na** + **alaṁ** + **thutu, *Thuti*** is flattery or \"hymning\" the donor to get the \"top\" portion next time. not capable of \"sweet-talking\" for the sake of better food.), nor is he a disparaging speaker (**Na** + **api** + **nipaccavādī, *Nipacca*** (from *ni* + √pat) implies prostrating oneself or \"falling down\" in speech to serve an agenda. not one who speaks humbly to manipulate or who complains about poor quality.)--- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Yadaggato majjhato sesato vā, Piṇḍaṁ labhetha paradattūpajīvī; Nālaṁ thutuṁ nopi nipaccavādī, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

Comment: **Paradattūpajīvī** (Para + datta + upajīvī) translates literally to \"one whose life depends on what is given by others.\" In the **Brahmacariya**, this is not a position of weakness, but a high-level **mechanical severance** from the \"work-for-food\" cycle that characterizes the involuntary experience of the **loka**.

By becoming a **Paradattūpajīvī**, the **bhikkhu** performs a radical \"exit\" from the economic and biological gravity of the universe.

**1). Breaking the \"Labor-for-Existence\" Loop**

Sumerian texts tell stories of the origin of human race on earth as a slave race, which suggests the deep subconscious of humanity is wired with a \"slave mentality\"---the belief that one must \"produce\" or \"labor\" to justify their existence or to secure their next meal.

- **The Trap:** This cycle of labor and production creates **Saṅkhāra** (constructions). Every time a being \"works\" to create a result/kamma in the world, they are further \"defiling\" (*lippati*) their **Citta** with the world\'s mechanics.

- **The Exit:** The **bhikkhu** stops all production. He creates no gold, no crops, and no digital artifacts for trade. By refusing to \"produce,\" he ceases to be a \"cog\" in the universe\'s engine.

**2). The Interface of Generosity**

The Sage acts as an **\"Interface\"** or a \"Field of Merit.\"

- **The Mechanics:** In the **loka**, energy usually moves through \"transaction\" (I give this to get that). The **Paradattūpajīvī** breaks the transaction. He receives without \"buying\" and gives (the Dhamma) without \"selling.\"

- **The Sifting:** This lifestyle forces the **Citta** to rely entirely on the \"Law of Dhamma\" rather than the \"Law of Labor.\" It shifts the mind\'s dependency from the physical **loka** to the spiritual **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

**\**

**3). The Psychology of Unreliability as a Tool**

The \"uncomfortable, unreliable, and unsafe\" nature of alms-dependence is the specific **vīmaṁsā** (discriminating) required to break the slave mind.

- **Safety is a Lie:** The \"safety\" of a job or a bank account is an illusion of the **loka**. It tethers the being to a \"dwelling\" (*Niketa*).

- **The Liberation from Anxiety:** When a **bhikkhu** stands for alms, if he receives food, he maintains the body; if he does not, he investigates the nature of **Dukkha**.

**4). Addressing the \"Slave Race\" Subconscious**

If we look at the perspective that humans were historically conditioned to labor (whether through ancient records like the Sumerian texts or biological evolution), the **Paradattūpajīvī** is the **Ultimate Rebel**.

- **The Refusal to Dig:** If the \"Anunnaki\" or the \"System\" requires gold/labor, the Sage is the one who puts down the shovel. He proves that the **Citta** does not belong to the masters of the world.

- **The Sovereignty:** By living on what is given, the Sage demonstrates that he is **Anaññaneyyaṁ**---not to be led or \"owned\" by any producer. His value is not aligned with what and how much to be able to produce, but rather aligned with the Dhamma.

**5). The Way to the Nibbāna-Dhātu**

This lifestyle is the final \"detachment\" from the umbilical cord of the universe. By not participate in the economic of the world, the **Citta** have zero \"debt\" to the world. A being who is still \"laboring for food\" is still \"investing\" in the universe. Because the **bhikkhu** is **Paradattūpajīvī**, he has no \"storehouse\" (**Aniketa**) to return to. Upon the dissolution of the final physical body, the purified **Citta**, having already severed the \"slave-bond\" of labor and hunger, has no \"momentum\" to return for more \"work.\"

**Summary:** The **Paradattūpajīvī** is not a beggar; he is a **Free Sovereign** who has opted out of the universe\'s labor camp. By breaking the \"work-to-eat\" program, he reclaims the **True Self** from the mechanical slavery of the **loka**, aligning himself with the ancient path of the **Tathāgatas**.

12\. The Sage living (caranta) by turning away (Virata) from sexual union (methuna), Who in his youth (yobbana) is not bound (**Na** + **upa** + **nibajjhate**) to anything (Kvaci); Turned away (Virata) from drunkenness (**Mada**) and Zeallessness (pamādā), totally released/ completely set free (vippamutta)--- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Muniṁ carantaṁ virataṁ methunasmā, Yo yobbane nopanibajjhate kvaci; Madappamādā virataṁ vippamuttaṁ, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

13\. Having fully known (Aññāya) the world (loka), seeing (dassi) the Supreme Goal (parama-attha), Having crossed over/ transcended (Atitariya) the flood (Ogha) and the ocean (Samudda), such a one; With knots/ties /bind (**Gantha**) severed (**Chinna**), independent/ unattached (Asita), without outflows/ leaks (asava) --- Him, indeed, the wise recognize as a Sage.

Aññāya lokaṁ parama-attha-dassiṁ, Oghaṁ samuddaṁ atitariya tādiṁ; Taṁ chinna-ganthaṁ asitaṁ anāsavaṁ, Taṁ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.

14\. Unequal and far apart are these two modes of living: The householder supporting a wife, and the one without ownership (**Amama**) and good vows (subbato, suvata); The householder is unrestrained, causing harm to other living beings, While the Sage, being governed/ Reined (Yato), constantly (Nicca) protects (Rakkhati) living beings (Pāṇine).

Asamā ubho dūravihāravuttino, Gihī dāraposī amamo ca subbato; Parapāṇarodhāya gihī asaññato, Niccaṁ munī rakkhati pāṇine yato.

Comment: "**Niccaṁ munī rakkhati pāṇine yato**" provides a profound insight into why **restraint** is not a moral \"politeness,\" but a strategic **frequency shift**.

The **loka** and the universeoperates under the law of power, or the **Law of Consumption**. From the smallest organism to the most complex human systems, the engine of the universe runs on **competition, exploitation, and power**. The strong prey on the weak; either consume or being consumed. Within this framework, the \"un-reined\" mind naturally moves toward survival at the expense of others.

**The Collision of Two Laws**

**1). The Law of the Loka: Consumption & Power**

The universe functions as a **Closed System of Energy**. For one being to \"gain,\" another must \"lose.\" Under this law, the soul is **Asaññato** (unrestrained). It acts like a predator because it is programmed to believe that \"more for me\" is the only path to safety. This leads to **Rodha** (obstruction and harm).
