Even if one does not \"want\" to hurt others, the act of \"winning\" in a competitive system requires the \"loss\" of another.

**2). The Law of Nibbāna-Dhātu and Mankind (Manussa): Values & Protection**

The **Nibbāna-Dhātu** is not just a destination; it is a **Realm governed by a different set of Laws** such as the law of values. Here the strong protects the weak, equality and Fairness etc.. Under the influence of this law, the Sage recognizes that to reach this realm, the **Citta** must be \"re-coded.\" You cannot enter a realm of **Fairness** while still operating on the software of **Exploitation**.

**Why \"Governing\" (Yato) is Necessary**

The Sage uses **restraint** to build an \"Internal Firewall\" against the universe\'s predator-logic.

- **Reining in the Impulse:** When the mind is **Yato** (governed), it is \"pulled back\" from the biological/collective subconscious urge to compete. This is not because the soul is \"bad,\" but because the **soul is currently trapped in a machine (the body/loka)** that demands competition.

- **Protecting as Training:** By **protecting** living beings (**rakkhati pāṇine**), the Sage is performing a **simulation of Nibbāna-Dhātu laws** while still trapped in the physical world.

  - In the world: The strong consume the weak.

  - In the Sage\'s Mind: The strong (the Muni) protect the weak (the beings).

By consistently choosing protection over consumption, the Sage creates a **\"Dhamma-Space\"** around himself where the laws of the universe no longer apply.

Governing the mind is the act of **defying the universe\'s gravity**. The Sage protects beings not to \"be a good person\" in a worldly sense, but to **divest his Soul from the Law of Power**. He is a \"Citizen of the Future Realm\" who refuses to play by the rules of the universe, ensuring his path to the **ancient path** is clear.

15\. Just as the crested, blue-necked peacock Never matches the speed of the swan; So the householder does not equal the **bhikkhu**, The Sage, secluded (Vivitta), practicing Jhāna (Jhāyati) in the forest/ wild (Vana).

Sikhī yathā nīlagīvo vihaṅgamo, Haṁsassa nopeti javaṁ kudācanaṁ; Evaṁ gihī nānukaroti bhikkhuno, Munino vivittassa vanamhi jhāyatoti.

Comment: **Jhāyati in Vana**

**Jhāyati** comes from the root of Vedic **√dhī**, and the Pāḷi **√jhā**. In the archaic sense, meditation was not a passive \"relaxation,\" but an **active, luminous observation**---a way of \"seeing\" with the internal eye of the **Soul**.

**1). The Vedic Ancestor: √dhī (to See, to Perceive)**

In the Rigveda, the root **√dhī** (which becomes *dhyā* in Sanskrit and *jhā* in Pāḷi) is intimately connected to **Dhīra** (the wise, the steady-minded).

- **The Visionary Faculty:** **Dhī** refers to the faculty of \"visionary insight.\" It is the ability of the **Soul** to perceive the **Rta** (true reality) behind the manifestations of the physical world.

- **The Luminous Flash:** A *dhī* was often considered a \"flash of insight\" or a \"luminous prayer.\" When a being is **Jhāyatoti**, they are engaging this archaic \"vision-fire\" to see through the **loka**.

- **The Connection to Agni:** The Vedic texts often describe the god of fire, **Agni**, as the \"Lord of **Dhī**.\" This reinforces **Jhāyati** as a steady \"internal fire.\" The Sage \"burns\" with zeal (**Appamada**) to keep the darkness of **moha** (delusion) at bay.

**2). The Shift to √dhyā: To Hold and Sustain**

As the language shifted toward Classical Sanskrit, **√dhyā** (Dhyāna) began to emphasize the \"sustained\" nature of this vision.

- **Dhyai + Ana:** The suffix *-ana* indicates a continuous process.

- **The Mechanical Grip:** To be in **Jhāna** means the **Citta** has \"gripped\" the object of meditation so firmly that the \"Mental Phenomena\" of the external world (noise, pain, distraction) can no longer shake it.

- **The Sifting:** In Sanskrit analysis, **Dhyāna** is the \"unbroken stream of awareness\" toward an object. For the **Muni**, this \"stream\" is directed solely at the **Nibbāna-Dhātu**, sifting it away from the turbulent \"eddies\" of the **loka**.

**3). The Pāḷi Evolution: √jhā as Combustion**

The Pāḷi **√jhā** (Jhāyati) preserves a specific nuance that the later Sanskrit *Dhyāna* often lost: the sense of **combustion**.

- **Jhāyati (to burn):** In Pāḷi, the verb for \"to meditate\" and \"to burn\" is the same (**Jhāyati**).

- **The Slave-Cords:** To reach the **Nibbāna-dhātu**, the **Gantha** (knots) and **Anusaya** (latent tendencies) must be burned away. **Jhāyatoti** is the Sage sitting in the forest, \"cremating\" his own attachments.

- **The Smoke vs. The Flame:** The householder's mind is \"smoky\" (filled with **moha**). The Sage's mind is a \"smokeless flame\" (**Nīta-dhūma**).

**The Alchemy of the Un-Plowed: Why the *Vana* is Essential for *Jhāyati***

In the **ancient path to Nibbāna**, the **Vana** (Forest) is far more than a scenic backdrop for contemplation; it is a necessity to allow the **Citta** to achieve the velocity required for transcendence. To understand why the **Muni** must be **Vanamhi** (in the forest) to truly be **Jhāyatoti** (burning/meditating), we must sift through the Vedic and Pāḷi roots.

**1). The Veda: The Forest as the \"Womb of the Gods\"**

In the *Rigveda*, the forest is personified as **Araṇyānī** (the Goddess of the Forest). The *Araṇyānī Sūkta* (RV 10.146) describes her as \"The Mother of all forest creatures\" who \"tills not, yet has stores of food.\" The Veda suggests that the **Vana** is where one hears the \"music of the gods\" (the *cichika* or cricket-sounds) and where the \"Super-beings\" reside. These areas were seen as **Tīrthas** (crossings). Because the forest is not \"claimed\" by human or the kings, it remains a territory where high beings (gods) may manifest.

**2). Isipatana: The \"Descent of the god/demigod\"**

**Isipatana** (Sarnath), which etymologically supports the idea of the forest as a \"Landing Zone.\" **Isi** (Rishi/divine human/demigod/god) + **Patana** (Falling/Descending). It is literally \"The Place where the god/demigod Fall/Descend.\" Ancient tradition holds that high-level gods would descend from the upper realms and \"land\" at places such as Isipatana to reach the Earth. To a high-frequency being, a city is a \"Zone of Noise and Sludge.\" A forest, however, is a \"Clean Interface.\"

**3). Solitude (Vivekā) as Frequency Shielding**

Gautama and the renunciates went to the forest for **structural solitude**. Every human being in the **loka** acts as a \"Broadcaster\" of **rāga, dosa, and moha**. If you are near them, their mental \"static\" interferes with your **Jhāyatoti** (internal fire). The forest acts as a \"Faraday Cage.\" It blocks the psychological \"gravity\" of the world. This is why the **Muni** is **Vivitta** (secluded). You cannot sift a grain of sand if you are still in the middle of a sandstorm. You must go where the the worldly winds doesn\'t blow.

### Dhammapada (Selected Chapters): The Eternal Treasury of Wisdom

The *Dhammapada* stands as a direct expression of the living wisdom of the Gautama the Supreme Arahant, preserving the clarity and force of his insight into the nature of the world and the way beyond it. Composed as a collection of concise and penetrating verses, it distills essential principles of the path into forms that are both memorable and exacting.

These verses address the full scope of practice: ethical restraint, disciplined conduct, and the training of the mind. They reveal the instability of the worldly existence, directing the practitioner toward right mindfulness, firm self-mastery, and clear understanding. In this way, the text functions not as abstract philosophy, but as a practical manual for transforming one's mode of existence.

Accessible to both monastics and lay followers, the *Dhammapada* establishes a complete orientation toward liberation from the world. It affirms personal responsibility as the decisive factor, highlights the formative power of the mind, and places wisdom and compassion at the center of the path. Through the cultivation of moral integrity, mental discipline, and penetrative insight, it guides the practitioner toward liberation (Vimutti)---the complete release from the world.

#### Chapter 1: Yamaka Vagga -- The Pairs

**1. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā,\
    Manasā ce paduṭṭhena, bhāsati vā karoti vā,\
    Tato naṃ dukkhamanveti, cakkaṃ'va vahato padaṃ.**

Will is the forerunner of all dhamma. Will is their chief; they are all produced by will. With impure will one speaks or acts, suffering follows, like the wheel follows the hoof of the ox. 

**2. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā,\
    Manasā ce pasannena, bhāsati vā karoti vā,\
    Tato naṃ sukhamanveti, chāyā'va anapāyinī.**

Will is the forerunner of all dhamma. Will is their chief; they are all produced by will. With pure intention one speaks or acts, Happiness follows, like one's own shadow that never leaves. 

**3. Akkocchi maṃ avadhi maṃ, ajini maṃ ahāsi me;\
    Ye taṃ upanayhantim, veraṃ tesaṃ na sammati.**

"He has abused, beaten, ill-treated and robbed me", those who harbor and entertain such thoughts will not cease the hatred in them. 

**4. Akkocchi maṃ avadhi maṃ, ajini maṃ ahāsi me;\
     Ye taṃ na upanayhanti, veraṃ tesūpasammati.**

"He has abused, beaten, ill-treated and robbed me", those who don't harbor and entertain such thoughts will cease the hatred in them.

**5. Na hi verena verāni, sammantī'dha kudācanaṃ;\
     Averena ca sammanti, esa dhammo sanantano.**

In this world, hatred can never be appeased by hatred. Only non-hatred will do, this is an eternal dhmma (law).

**6. Pare ca na vijānanti, mayamettha yamāmase;\
    Ye ca tattha vijānanti, tato sammanti medhagā.**

Most people do not understand that here we are all subject to death; those who understand this, their conflicts subside. 

**7. Subhānupassiṃ viharantaṃ, indriyesu asaṃvutaṃ,\
    Bhojanamhi amattaññuṃ, kusītaṃ hīnavīriyaṃ,\
    Taṃ ve pasahati māro, vāto rukkhaṃ'va dubbalaṃ.**

Whoever lives contemplating pleasant worldly things, with senses unrestrained, in food immoderate, lazy, having poor strength, will be defeated by māra (deva of death), like a weak tree. 

**8. Asubhānupassiṃ viharantaṃ, indriyesu susaṃvutaṃ,\
    Bhojanamhi ca mattaññuṃ, saddhaṃ āraddhavīriyaṃ,\
    Taṃ ve nappasahati māro, vāto selaṃ'va pabbataṃ.**

Whoever lives contemplating the unsatisfactory impure nature of the world, with senses restrained, in food moderate, full of faith, having firm strength, will not be defeated by māra (deva of death), like a solid rock. 

**9. Anikkasāvo kāsāvaṃ, yo vatthaṃ paridahissati;\
     Apeto damasaccena, na so kāsāvam arahati.**

Whoever with an orange robe but is not stainless yet, when putting on his robe, should be aware that without dhamma of the truth, he is not worthy of the orange robe.

**10. Yo ca vantakasāv'assa, sīlesu susamāhito;\
       Upeto damasaccena, sa ve kāsāvam arahati.**

He who has purged all stains, well-established in sīla, has the dhamma of the truth, is worthy of the orange robe. 

**11. Asāre sāramatino, sāre cāsāradassino;\
       Te sāraṃ nādhigacchanti, micchāsaṅkappagocarā.**

Those who regard the essence as non-essence, perceive the non-essence as essence, they will not find the superior essence through wrong strives.

**12. Sārañca sārato ñatvā, asārañca asārato;\
       Te sāraṃ adhigacchanti, sammā saṅkappagocarā.**

What is essence they regard as essence, what is non-essence they regard as non-essence, they will find the superior essence with right strives. 

Comment: "essence" (*sāra*) vs. "non-essence" (*asāra*)?

*Sāra* here refers to that which is **stable, reliable, and capable of leading out of worldly becoming**. At the highest level, *sāra* points toward:

- The **path factors** that lead to liberation

- The **purification of citta** from rāga, dosa, moha

- The realization of **Nibbāna-dhātu** --- the realm beyond the world (loka)

More operationally, within practice, *sāra* includes:

- **Sīla** --- withdrawal from unwholesome entanglement

- **Samādhi** --- stabilization and lifting of the mind beyond sensory turbulence

- **Paññā** --- correct seeing that dismantles false valuation

*Sāra* is **that which participates in the Middle Exit (Majjhimā Nissaraṇa)**.

*Asāra* is everything that:

- **Decays, shifts, or collapses under worldly conditions**

- Generates or reinforces **worldly becoming (bhava)**

- Feeds the proliferation of **viññāṇa (worldly consciousness) rooted in defilement**

This includes:

- Sensual pleasures (kāma)

- Worldly Identity constructions (views, roles, narratives of "ego")

- Emotional intoxications

- Intellectual fascination without liberative direction

Even highly praised worldly attainments---status, knowledge, influence---are *asāra* if they **do not terminate the cycle of worldly entanglement**.

**Verse 11:** "They regard the non-essence as essence..."

This is not simple ignorance---it is **systematic misperception (micchā-saṅkappa)**. Examples of inversion:

- Treating **sensory stimulation** as fulfillment

- Treating **emotional intensity** as depth

- Treating **conceptual knowledge** as realization

- Treating **worldly** **existence itself** as inherently worthwhile

This aligns precisely with:

- The generation of **defiled viññāṇa** through worldly contact

- The subsequent **binding of citta to the world**

- The inability to generate disenchantment (*nibbidā*)

Thus, such beings "do not attain the essence" because their entire perceptual system is **oriented toward the wrong field (gocara)**.

**Verse 12:** "Knowing essence as essence, non-essence as non-essence..."

This is **sammā-saṅkappa functioning as discriminative clarity**: **direct recognition of what leads out of the world vs what binds** **with the world**

This recognition produces:

- **Dispassion toward asāra** (not suppression, but clear devaluation)

- **Directed effort toward sāra** (precision in practice)

At this point, practice becomes **economical and surgical**---energy is no longer wasted on non-essential pursuits.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**13. Yathā'gāraṃ ducchannaṃ, vuṭṭhi samativijjhati;\
       Evaṃ abhāvitaṃ cittaṃ, rāgo samativijjhati.**

Just as rain penetrates a badly-thatched house, so does rāga penetrates an undeveloped mind.

**14. Yathā'gāraṃ succhannaṃ, vuṭṭhi na samativijjhati;\
       Evaṃ subhāvitaṃ cittaṃ, rāgo na samativijjhati.**

Just as rain cannot penetrate a well-thatched house, so does rāga cannot penetrate a well-developed mind. 

**15. Idha socati pecca socati, pāpakārī ubhayattha socati;\
      So socati so vihaññati, disvā kammakiliṭṭham attano.**

He grieves in this world, he grieves in the next one, a wrong-doer grieves in both. He grieves and is defeated, perceiving the impure kamma of his own.

**16. Idha modati pecca modati, katapuñño ubhayattha modati;\
      So modati so pamodati, disvā kammavisuddhim attano.**

He rejoices in this world, he rejoices in the next one, a wholesome-doer rejoices in both. He rejoices and is delighted, perceiving the pure kamma of his own. 

**17. Idha tappati pecca tappati, pāpakārī ubhayattha tapati;\
      "Pāpaṃ me katan"ti tappati, bhiyyo tappati duggatiṃ gato.**

He laments in this world, he laments in the next one, a wrong-doer laments in both.  "I have done evil," he laments. He laments again, having gone to a bad state.  

**18. Idha nandati pecca nandati, katapuñño ubhayattha nandati;\
      "Puññaṃ me katan"ti nandati, bhiyyo nandati suggatiṃ gato.**

He is happy in this world, he is happy in the next one, a wholesome-doer is happy in both. "I have done good," he is happy. He is happy again, having gone to a good state.

**19. Bahum pi ce saṃhita bhāsamāno, na takkaro hoti naro pamatto;\
      Gopo'va gāvo gaṇayaṃ paresaṃ, na bhāgavā sāmaññassa hoti.**

Although he plans and talks a lot, but does not act accordingly, he is the negligent man.  Hence, he is like a heedless cowherd who counts the cattle of others, not a bhāgavā (the blessed one), not a sāmana.

**20. Appam pi ce saṃhita bhāsamāno, dhammassa hoti anudhammacārī,\
      Rāgañca dosañca pahāya mohaṃ, sammappajāno suvimuttacitto,\
      Anupādiyāno idha vā huraṃ vā, sa bhāgavā sāmaññassa hoti.**

Although he plans and talks little, but always practice according to the dhamma he learns, giving up rāga and dosa and no more moha, strives to the perfect liberation of the mind, not-clinging to this world and the next one, he is a bhāgavā (the blessed one) and a true sāmana.

#### Chapter 2: Appamāda Vagga -- Zeal

**1(21). Appamādo amatapadaṃ, pamādo maccuno padaṃ;\
            Appamattā na mīyanti, ye pamattā yathā matā.**

Zeal is the base for the deathless, zealless is the base for death. The zealous do not die; the zealless are like the dead. 

**2(22). Etaṃ visesato ñatvā, appamādamhi paṇḍitā;\
            Appamāde pamodanti, ariyānaṃ gocare ratā.**

Distinctly understanding this, the wise embraces zeal, rejoices in zeal, delighting in the realm of the **Ariyāna** (Noble Ones). 

Comment: **The Primacy of Zealousness (Appamāda) as the Foundation of the Deathless**

The paired verses declare a radical principle: **"Appamādo amatapadaṁ, pamādo maccuno padaṁ."** Zealousness is the base of the Deathless; zeallessness is the base of death.

Here, **appamāda** is not presented as one factor among many. It is the **decisive ground**---the *pada*---upon which the entire movement toward the Deathless stands. Appamāda is **the generative foundation** of the path: when zealousness is present, the path comes alive; when it collapses, everything collapses with it.

**(1). Appamāda as the Generative Foundation of the Path**

**Appamāda (zealousness)** is not merely supportive; it is **productive**. It is the living force from which the path-factors arise and are sustained:

- **saddhā (confidence/faith)** arises when the heart is seized by urgency and commitment

- **vīriya (energy)** is the natural expression of sustained zealous engagement

- **sati (mindfulness)** stabilizes because zealousness refuses lapse or forgetfulness

- **samādhi (collectedness)** deepens because attention is not allowed to scatter

- **paññā (discernment)** emerges within uninterrupted, intense observation

Thus, zealousness is: **the ignition force and continuous current of the entire path-system.** Without it, the faculties do not merely weaken---they **fail to arise in their true form**.

**(2). Gautama's Final Instruction: A Singular Emphasis**

At the closing moment of the teaching life, Gautama the Supreme Arahant, the Supreme Arahant, did not summarize the path in many factors. He gave a single operational directive in the Mahāparinibbāna Sutta:

**"Vayadhammā saṅkhārā; appamādena sampādetha."**\
"All worldly conditioned things are subject to decay; accomplish (the goal) through zealousness."

This is not incidental. It is **a final compression of the entire training** into its generative core.

- Not "be mindful"

- Not "be balanced"

- Not "be calm"

But: **"Complete the path through zealousness."** Because only zealousness can **carry the path to completion without break**.

**(3). The Wise Delight in Zealousness**

The next verse reinforces the existential divide: "Understanding this distinction, the wise rejoice in zealousness, delighting in the realm of the **Ariyāna** (Noble Ones)."

Here, zealousness is not burden---it is **joyful engagement**. The Ariyā (Noble Ones) do not drift, moderate, or idle; they **delight in the intensity of the path**.

**(4). Why "Zealousness" Is the Correct Translation**

Modern translations often render **appamāda** as:

- "mindfulness"

- "heedfulness"

- "carefulness"

- "balanced awareness"

These are **deflationary translations**. They flatten the force of the term. They fail because they remove what is essential: **the intensity, urgency, and driving force of engagement**

"Zealousness" is correct because it preserves:

- **energetic commitment**

- **refusal of slackening**

- **continuous application**

- **existential seriousness**

Appamāda is not a passive condition. It is not neutral observation. It is **the fire that keeps the path alive**.

**(5). The Danger of Modern Overemphasis on "Mindfulness"**

In contemporary buddhist culture, there is a heavy emphasis on **mindfulness (sati)**---often isolated from the rest of the path and even presented as sufficient in itself. This is a serious distortion.

- Mindfulness without zealousness becomes **passive observation**

- It can coexist with comfort, worldliness, and even complacency

- It lacks the **driving force toward liberation**

When **sati is detached from appamāda**, it degenerates into: **mere awareness without transformation**

This is precisely the condition of **pamāda** in refined form: not gross negligence, but **subtle slackening disguised as practice**.

**(6). Zealousness vs Zeallessness: Life and Death**

The verse states with uncompromising clarity:

- **The zealous do not die**

- **The zealless are like the dead**

This is not biological death. It is **spiritual condition**:

- Zealousness = living path, active liberation

- Zeallessness = inert existence, bound to decay

Thus: **Life on the path is measured by intensity of engagement, not mere awareness**

**(7). Final Synthesis**

**Appamāda (zealousness) is the generative foundation of the path. It gives rise to all faculties, sustains their operation, and carries the practitioner to completion.** This is why:

- it is called the **base of the Deathless**

- it is the **Gautama's final instruction**

- it is the **delight of the Noble Ones**

And this is why it must be translated not as a neutral or balanced state, but as: **Zealousness --- intense, continuous, and unwavering engagement with the path to liberation.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**3(23). Te jhāyino sātatikā, niccaṃ daḷhaparakkamā;\
            Phusanti dhīrā nibbānaṃ, yogakkhemaṃ anuttaraṃ.**

They stay continuously meditative (Jhāyi, practice jhana), constantly with strong effort, the wise (dhīra) touch Nibbāna, have union with this Supreme Peace.

Comment: **Dhīra --- A Precise Linguistic and Doctrinal Clarification**

To understand **dhīra** in verse (23), we need to move beyond loose translation ("wise," "calm") and return to its **root structure and early semantic meaning**. When examined through the Vedic--early Indo-Aryan lens, *dhīra* is precisely a **technical designation of a stabilized, insight-bearing mind**.

**(1). Root Structure: √dhṛ and dhī**

The word **dhīra** is built from two closely related elements:

- **√dhṛ (धृ)** --- *to hold, to support, to bear, to sustain without collapse*

- **dhī (धी)** --- *vision, insight, higher intelligence, intuitive seeing*

So *dhīra* literally encodes: **"one who can hold (dhṛ) vision (dhī) steadily"**

This is not abstract wisdom. It is: **stabilized insight --- vision that does not shake, distort, or dissipate**

In early terms, the **dhamma-cakkhu (Dhamma-eye)** or **paññā-cakkhu (wisdom-eye)** refers to the direct vision of truth. A **dhīra** instead is **one who can hold the seeing**. The dhīra is the one whose citta is firm enough to **sustain that vision without distortion or collapse**. Through zealousness (appamāda), the mind becomes steady, unshaken by the world, and capable of holding insight continuously.

**The Dhamma-eye sees; the dhīra does not lose what is seen.** Thus, dhīra is the **stable bearer of insight**---the condition that allows the vision of truth to remain, deepen, and ultimately make contact with Nibbāna.

**(2). Vedic Context: The "Fixed Visionary"**

In the Vedic layer (e.g., the milieu of the Rigveda), *dhīra* is closely associated with **ṛṣis (seers)**.

**they are active holders of vision**

They do not receive truth through possession or external filling. Rather:

- their internal faculty (**dhī**) is refined

- their mind becomes **stable enough to sustain vision without distortion**

So: **Dhīra = fixed visionary, not passive vessel**

**(3). Relation to Dhyāna (Meditative Holding)**

The connection deepens when we consider:

- **dhī → dhyā (to contemplate, to meditate)**

- **dhyana (Jhana)** = sustained meditative holding

Thus: the dhīra is one whose **meditative holding is unbroken**

This aligns directly with the verse: "te jhāyino sātatikā, niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā" (continuously meditative (practice jhana), constantly with firm exertion)

So linguistically and doctrinally:

**Dhīra = one who can sustain dhyana (Jhana) without collapse**

**(4). Not Channeling --- but Sovereign Stabilization**

**Channeling (modern concept):**

- passive receptivity

- external entity enters or speaks

- loss of internal sovereignty

**Dhīra (early context):**

- active stabilization

- mastery over internal processes

- no surrender to external agency

So: **The dhīra is sovereign, not permeable.** They do not "open" to something external. They **stabilize the citta so completely that reality reveals itself without distortion**

**(5). Dhīra and Brahmacariya (Internal Fire Preservation)**

In the broader ancient framework:

- **brahmacariya** = alignment with higher order (Brahma-standard)

- **tapas** = preserved inner fire / energy

The dhīra is one who:

- does not dissipate energy into the world

- maintains internal intensity

- preserves continuity of practice

So: **Dhīra = one who holds the inner fire without leakage.** This directly connects with **appamāda (zealousness)**:

- zealousness sustains effort

- effort stabilizes dhyāna

- dhyāna enables dhī

- dhī held steadily → dhīra

**(6). Dhīra in Arahanism**

The dhīra has already:

- seen that all **loka (worldly dhamma)** are specifically conditioned

- recognized the **cosmic taxation (aging, dissolution)**

- abandoned identification with worldly phenomena

Thus the citta becomes:

- **unshaken by kāma (sensual cravings and worldly attachment)**

- **unshaken by dosa (survival instincts of fear and anger)**

- **unshaken by moha (ignorance of the liberation path and delusion of true reality)**

This produces: **a stable, non-reactive, non-entangled citta.** Which is precisely: **a dhīra**

**(7). Why the Dhīra Can "Touch" Nibbāna**

The verse says: **phusanti nibbānaṁ --- "they touch Nibbāna"**

Now, with linguistic precision, it implies **direct contact when conditions allow**. So the logic is:

- ordinary mind = unstable → cannot hold contact

- dhīra mind = stable and penetrative → contact becomes possible

Thus: **Nibbāna is not far --- instability is the barrier**

**(8). The "Sword-Horn" Insight (Khaggavisāṇa Parallel)**

The rhinoceros horn metaphor (from the Khaggavisāṇa tradition) suggests:

- singular direction

- non-branching movement

- independence from worldly entanglement

So the dhīra:

- moves in one direction only

- does not disperse into worldly relationships, desires, or distractions

- cuts through rāga, dosa, moha

Hence: **firm because undivided**

**Dhīra is one who, through zealousness (appamāda), has stabilized the citta such that it can hold insight (dhī) without distortion or collapse. This unshakable stability allows direct contact with Nibbāna, the supreme security and ultimate peace beyond all worldly entanglement.**

**4(24). Uṭṭhānavato satīmato, sucikammassa nisammakārino;\
            Saññatassa ca dhammajīvino, appamattassa yaso'bhivaḍḍhati.**

Be diligent in mindfulness, pure in deeds and be considerate, restraint and live (righteously) according to the dhamma, zeal steadily increases.

**5(25). Uṭṭhānenappamādena, saṃyamena damena ca;\
            Dīpaṃ kayirātha medhāvī, yaṃ ogho n'ābhikīrati.**

With zeal arisen, and thoughts well controlled, the wise make himself an island, which no flood can overwhelm.

Comment: **Reflect On Gautama the Supreme Arahant's Final Exhortation**

**"Atta-dīpā viharatha, atta-saraṇā, anañña-saraṇā;\
dhamma-dīpā, dhamma-saraṇā, anañña-saraṇā."**

**Dhammapada 25 and Gautama the Supreme Arahant's final exhortation form a single, continuous instruction.** Through uṭṭhāna (aroused energy), appamāda (zeal), saṃyama (restraint), and dama (discipline), the practitioner construct the inner island (*dīpaṃ kayirātha*)---a stabilized and purified citta that cannot be overwhelmed by the floods (ogha) of the world: sensuality, worldly becoming, worldly views, and ignorance. This constructed island is the actual elevation of the mind beyond worldly gravity (kāma, dosa, moha). Having established this unshakable ground, one then fulfills Gautama the Supreme Arahant's final directive: **"Atta-dīpā viharatha, atta-saraṇā... dhamma-dīpā, dhamma-saraṇā..."**---dwelling in the Self as island and refuge, dwelling in Dhamma as island and refuge, with no other refuge. Thus, what is built through disciplined practice becomes the very refuge in which one abides: the island constructed is the island realized, and the citta, no longer submersible in saṃsāra, stands beyond the reach of all floods.

**(1). The Structural Core: "Dīpaṃ kayirātha" vs "Atta-dīpā viharatha"**

Both teachings revolve around the same operative principle:

- **dīpa** = island

- **kayirātha** = "one should make"

- **viharatha** = "dwell"

So the two work otgether:

- *Dhammapada 25* → **construct the island**

- *Final instruction* → **abide in the island**

This is a **temporal progression**: **First build the refuge, Then dwell in the refuge.**

**(2). The Means of Construction (Dhammapada 25)**

The verse specifies *how* the island is made:

- **uṭṭhāna** --- arising energy, effort (viriya)

- **appamāda** --- zeal

- **saṃyama** --- restraint

- **dama** --- taming, disciplining

This is the **engineering of the inner island**.

This is exactly the training triad is about:

- **Sīla → restrains the senses (saṃyama, dama)**

- **Samādhi → stabilizes and elevates the mind (appamāda sustained)**

- **Paññā → guides effort (uṭṭhāna rightly directed)**

So the "island" is not metaphorical, it is a **constructed condition of the citta**, purified of worldly gravity (kāma, dosa, moha).

**(3). The Nature of the Island**

**"yaṃ ogho nābhikīrati"** --- which no flood overwhelms

**ogha (flood)** refers to:

- sensuality

- worldly becoming

- worldly views

- ignorance

So the island is: **a stabilized, elevated citta that is no longer submersible in saṃsāra.** This aligns well with: when defilements lose their gravitational pull, the citta becomes **light, buoyant, unbound**.

**\**

**(4). The Completion: Atta-dīpa / Dhamma-dīpa**

The final instruction reveals the true nature of the island that has been constructed:

- **Atta-dīpa** --- the Self as island

- **Dhamma-dīpa** --- the Dhamma of **Gautama the Supreme Arahant** as island

This does not indicate two separate refuges, but a single reality expressed in two inseparable dimensions:

**Ontological**

- **Atta** --- the luminous citta, the seat of being, capable of purification and transcendence

**Transformative / Lawful**

- **Dhamma** --- the exact liberating teaching that purifies, stabilizes, and elevates the citta

Thus, the instruction is exact and functional: By applying the Dhamma of the Supreme Arahant, the Self is fully transformed and purified; established in that purified state, the Self itself becomes the island and refuge, through which one departs the world and enters the realm of Nibbāna.

What is constructed through discipline becomes the very refuge that enables transcendence beyond the world.

Finally, the two teachings unify into a single instruction:

Through aroused energy, zeal, and disciplined restraint, one constructs the island of a purified citta through the Dhamma of the Supreme Arahant, **Gautama the Supreme Arahant**; having fully transformed and purified the Self, one then dwells in that very Self as island and refuge---established in Dhamma---thereby departing the world and becoming forever beyond the reach of the floods.

**\**

**6(26). Pamādamanuyuñjanti, bālā dummedhino janā;\
            Appamādañca medhāvī, dhanaṃ seṭṭhaṃ'va rakkhati.**

The ignorant, foolish folk indulge in zealless; the wise guard zeal as the greatest treasure.

**7(27). Mā pamādamanuyuñjetha, mā kāmaratisanthavaṃ;\
            Appamatto hi jhāyanto, pappoti vipulaṃ sukhaṃ.**

Not indulge in zealless; not delight in sensual pleasures. The zealous meditator obtains abundant bliss.

**8(28).  Pamādaṃ appamādena, yadā nudati paṇḍito;\
             Paññāpāsādamāruyha, asoko sokiniṃ pajaṃ;\
             Pabbataṭṭho'va bhūmaṭṭhe, dhīro bāle avekkhati.**

When a wise man discards zealless by zeal, he, free from sorrow, ascends to the palace of wisdom and surveys the sorrowing ignorant folk as a mountaineer surveys those below.

**9(29). Appamatto pamattesu, suttesu bahujāgaro;\
            Abalassaṃ'va sīghasso, hitvā yāti sumedhaso.**

Zealous amongst the negligent, watchful amongst the sleeping, the wise one advances like a swift horse, having left behind a weak one.

**10(30). Appamādena Maghavā, devānaṃ seṭṭhataṃ gato;\
              Appamādaṃ pasaṃsanti, pamādo garahito sadā.**

With zeal Maghavā become the chief amongst the devas. Zeal is ever praised; zealless is ever despised. 

**11(31). Appamādarato bhikkhu, pamāde bhayadassi vā;\
              Saṃyojanaṃ aṇuṃ thūlaṃ, ḍahaṃ aggī'va gacchati.**

The zealous bhikkhu, realizing the danger of zealless, advances like **fire**, **burning** all fetters great and small. 

**12(32). Appamādarato bhikkhu, pamāde bhayadassi vā;\
              Abhabbo parihānāya, nibbānass'eva santike.**

The zealous bhikkhu, realizing the danger of zealless, unable to regress, he is just in the vicinity of the nibbāna.

#### Chapter 3:  Citta Vagga -- Citta

**1(33). Phandanaṃ capalaṃ cittaṃ, dūrakkhaṃ dunnivārayaṃ;\
            Ujuṃ karoti medhāvī, usukāro'va tejanaṃ.**

The wavering, unsteady citta (mind-heart), hard to guard, difficult to restrain --- the wise person straightens it like an arrow-maker straightens an arrow.

**2(34). Vārijo'va thale khitto, okamokata ubbhato;\
            Pariphandatimidaṃ cittaṃ, māradheyyaṃ pahātave.**

Like a fish that is drawn from its watery abode and thrown upon land, this citta trembles in order to leave the reach of Māra (deva of Death). 

**3(35). Dunniggahassa lahuno, yattha kāmanipātino;\
            Cittassa damatho sādhu, cittaṃ dantaṃ sukhāvahaṃ.**

The citta is difficult to restrain, quick, it falls into sex impulse (**kāma**). Good is the taming of the citta. A restrained citta brings happiness.

**4(36). Sududdasaṃ sunipuṇaṃ, yattha kāmanipātinaṃ;\
            Cittaṃ rakkhetha medhāvī, cittaṃ guttaṃ sukhāvahaṃ.**

The citta is very difficult to see, very subtle, it falls into sex impulse (**kāma**). The wise guard the citta; a guarded citta brings happiness. 

**5(37). Dūraṅgamaṃ ekacaraṃ, asarīraṃ guhāsayaṃ;\
            Ye cittaṃ saṃyamessanti, mokkhanti mārabandhanā.**

Wandering alone, dwelling in a cave, no more (rāga, dosa, and moha) emanating from the body; a well restrained citta, is liberated from the bond of Māra. 

**6(38). Anavaṭṭhitacittassa, saddhammaṃ avijānato;\
            Pariplavapasādassa, paññā na paripūrati.**

He whose citta is unsteady, he who does not know the true dhamma (Gautama's teachings), he whose faith wavers --- the wisdom of such one will not become perfect. 

**\**

**7(39). Anavassutacittassa, ananvāhatacetaso;\
            Puñña pāpapahīṇassa, natthi jāgarato bhayaṃ.**

He whose citta is free from sexual desires (Non-leaking), who is free from the impact of worldly strikes, who is virtuous (the world-transcending power of merit), who has abandoned wrong doing--- for such a vigilant one there is no fear. 

**(1) Anavassuta-cittassa**: Non-leaking Citta, \"leaking\" its internal energy into **kāma** (sex). When a **bhikkhu** practices **Brahmacariya**, they plug the leaks of the mind. A \"leaking\" mind is a mind that is **kāmanipātino** (falling into sex-impulses).

**(2) Ananvāhata-cetaso**: Un-impacted Mind, **cetasa** is the functioning of the mind, the mental phenomena. This describes a mind where the **Mental Phenomena** (*cetasa*) is no longer \"struck\" or \"dented\" by the impact of the external world.

When an average person experiences external stimulation (sights, sounds, sex-impulses), it triggers a reactive chain that produces defiled **cetasa**---mental manifestations of **rāga**, **dosa**, or **moha**. For the one who is *ananvāhata*, that chain is broken.

The term **Ananvāhata** (*an* + *anu* + *āhata*) literally means \"not struck along with\" or \"un-impacted.\"

- **The Worldly Impact:** External stimuli are manifestations of the physical world, which are themselves manifests of the Universal or Worldly **Mental Phenomena**. For the worldling, these stimuli \"strike\" the mind and create a vibration of craving or clinging.

- **The Unshakable Citta:** For the practitioner who has attained purification, the **Citta** is stable and non-leaking that external stimulation cannot penetrate or \"defile\" the functioning of the mind. The stimulus occurs, but it does not produce a corresponding defiled manifestation (*cetasa*).

**(3) Puñña pāpa-pahīṇassa**: To go beyond both the ascensional merit (**Puñña**), and world-binding force of wrongdoing (**Pāpa**).

To explain **puñña** (merit) and **Pāpa** (wrongdoing) from the perspective of the Veda, we must look at the root **√pū**, which carries the meaning of \"purifying,\" \"cleansing,\" or \"brightening.\" In the Vedic and Brahmic tradition, merit is not just a moral tally---it is a functional, energetic state of the **Citta**.

**Etymology: The Root *Pū* (Purification)**

In Sanskrit, *Puṇya* (Pali: *Puñña*) is derived from **√pū** (to cleanse).

- **Vedic Context:** In the *Rigveda*, the term is used in relation to the **Pavamāna** (the self-purifying flow). Just as water or fire cleanses physical dross, *Puñña* is the force that cleanses the **Mental Phenomena** of the \"dust\" (*raja*) of the **Kāma-dhātu**.

- **Functional Duality:** If *Pāpa* is that which \"thickens\" or \"pollutes\" the mind, making it heavy and **kāmanipātino** (falling), then *Puñña* is that which \"thins\" and \"clarifies\" the mind, making it buoyant and **Sugato** (directed to wholesomeness).

**The Vedic Concept of *Sukṛta* (Well-Done)**

Before *Puṇya* became the standard term, the Vedas used **Sukṛta**. This refers to actions aligned with **Ṛta** (Order, Higher Laws).

- **World-Transcending Energy:** By acting in alignment with the higher laws (the standard of Brahma), the practitioner accumulates a \"store\" of refined energy.

- **The \"Bright\" Path:** The Vedas describe two paths: the dark path (*Kṛṣṇa*) of binding and the bright path (*Śukla*) of transcendence. *Puñña* is the substance of the bright path.

**Puñña as \"Buoyancy\" for the Citta**

In the broader Sanskrit tradition, *Puṇya* is often linked to the ability to \"ascend.\"

- **The Weight of the World:** *Pāpa* is worldly because it is \"heavy\"; it binds the mind to the earth and the **Sex-Realm**.

- **The Propulsion:** *Puñña* is \"light.\" It allows the **Citta** to rise out of the reach of Māra. This is why it is world-transcending. Without this purified energy, the mind lacks the \"thrust\" required to pierce through the upper strata of the universe.

**Why it is Ultimately \"Relinquished\" (*Pahīṇa*)**

Gautama the Supreme Arahant's liberation path is not ascension to a higher realm, but to completely transcend the world, depart from this world to the realm of Nibbana. Similar to Gautama's liberation from the world (**exit from saṃsāra**), in the high-Vedic and Upanishadic layers, there is a concept:

- Once the practitioner reaches the **Parama Gati** (the Highest Goal/Nibbāna-Dhātu), they must leave behind the \"boat\" of *Puṇya*.

- In the *Mundaka Upanishad*, it is said that the wise \"shake off both good and evil\" (*puṇya-pāpe vidhūya*) to reach the supreme stainless unity.

- **Arahanist Correction:** While the Upanishads might lean toward non-duality, the Arahanist Path uses this same Sanskrit logic to show that the \"purifying fire\" of merit is used to burn away the \"dross\" of wrongdoing. Once the dross is gone and the fire has done its work, the fire itself is extinguished as the Citta exits the universe. This is why it requires abandoning both the **world-binding** force of wrongdoing (**Pāpa**) and also the ascensional merit (**Puñña**), because the goal of liberation is **transcendence of** the world.

**(4). Jāgarato:** The Vigilant. This refers to the one who is \"awake\" or \"vigilant.\" It is the opposite of the \"sleeping\" worldlings who are lost in samsara. The vigilant one sees the path of liberation from the world and remains ready to transcend.

**\**

**8(40). Kumbhūpamaṃ kāyamimaṃ viditvā, nagarūpamaṃ cittamidaṃ ṭhapetvā; Yodhetha māraṃ paññāvudhena, jitañca rakkhe anivesano siyā.**

Having understood this body to be like a jar; having accepted this citta like a fortress, attack Māra with the weapon of wisdom. You should protect the victory with no attachment. 

**9(41). Aciraṃ vat'ayaṃ kāyo, paṭhaviṃ adhisessati;\
          Chuddho apetaviññāṇo, niratthaṃ'va kaḷiṅgaraṃ.**

Not long, this body will lie upon the ground, discarded, without consciousness, worthless, like a piece of worthless wood.

**10(42). Diso disaṃ yaṃ taṃ kayirā, verī vā pana verinaṃ;\
              Micchā panihitaṃ cittaṃ, pāpiyo naṃ tato kare.**

Whatever (harm) a foe may do to a foe, or a hater to a hater, a wrongly directed citta can do itself far greater damage.

**11(43). Na taṃ mātā pitā kayirā, aññe vā pi ca ñātakā;\
             Sammā panihitaṃ cittaṃ, seyyaso naṃ tato kare.**

What (favor) neither mother, nor father, nor any other relative can do, a properly directed citta can do even far better than that.

Comment: **Direction of the Citta (panihita)**

- **micchā panihitaṃ cittaṃ** --- wrongly directed citta

- **sammā panihitaṃ cittaṃ** --- rightly directed citta

The term **panihita** is critical: it means *set, aimed, aligned, established toward a goal*.

**What is "Right Direction"?**

**The highest and only worthwhile goal of the citta is its own liberation.**

Verses 42--43 establish the decisive principle of the path: the destiny of a being is determined not by external forces, but by the direction of the citta itself. A wrongly directed citta, aligned with worldly sensuality, aversion, and ignorance, inflicts greater harm than any enemy, for it binds itself deeper into the gravitational field of the world. Conversely, a rightly directed citta---aligned with the Dhamma of Gautama the Supreme Arahant---accomplishes what no parent or relative can: the complete purification and liberation of the citta. Thus, the supreme and only meaningful function of the citta is its own transcendence; when properly directed, it becomes both the path and the means by which one departs the world and realizes Nibbāna-dhātu.

#### Chapter 7. Arahanta Vagga -- Arahant 

**1(90). Gataddhino visokassa, vippamuttassa sabbadhi;\
            Sabbaganthappahīṇassa, pariḷāho na vijjati.**

For him who has completed the journey, who is sorrowless and wholly liberated, who has broken all (worldly) bonds, there is no more torment.

**2(91). Uyyuñjanti satīmanto, na nikete ramanti te;\
            Haṃsā'va pallalaṃ hitvā, okam okaṃ jahanti te.**

Those who have mindfulness exert themselves; they do not delight in any (worldly) abode. Like swans that leave a lake, they abandon one dwelling place after another. 

**3(92). Yesaṃ sannicayo natthi, ye pariññātabhojanā;\
            Suññato animitto ca, vimokkho yassa gocaro;\
            Ākāse'va sakuntānaṃ, gati tesaṃ durannayā.**

Those who do not hoard, who have understood food, whose domain of understanding (for this world) is emptiness and signlessness---their path, like that of birds in the air, cannot be traced.

**4(93). Yassāsavā parikkhīṇā, āhāre ca anissito;\
            Suññato animitto ca, vimokkho yassa gocaro;\
            Ākāse'va sakuntānaṃ, padaṃ tassa durannayaṃ.**

He whose taints are destroyed, who is not attached to food, whose domain of understanding (for this world) is emptiness and signlessness---their path, like that of birds in the air, cannot be traced. 

**5(94). Yass'indriyāni samathaṅgatāni, assā yathā sārathinā sudantā;\
            Pahīnamānassa anāsavassa, devā'pi tassa pihayanti tādino.**

He whose senses are calmed, like horses well-tamed by a charioteer, who has abandoned pride and is free from defilements---even the devas admire such a one.

**\**

**6(95). Paṭhavi samo no virujjhati, indakhilupamo tādi subbato;\
           Rahado'va apetakaddamo, saṃsārā na bhavanti tādino.**

He is like the earth, he is not provoked; he is like Indra's pillar, steadfast, virtuous; he is like a lake free from mud---for such a one samsāra does not arise.

**7(96). Santaṃ tassa manaṃ hoti, santā vācā ca kamma ca;\
            Sammadaññā vimuttassa, upasantassa tādino.**

Calm is his mind, calm is his speech and action, who is liberated through perfect knowledge, perfectly peaceful and stable. 

**8(97). Assaddho akataññū ca, sandhicchedo ca yo naro;\
           Hatāvakāso vantāso, sa ve uttamaporiso.**

He who is without faith (**Assaddha**) and ungrateful (**Akataññū**), breaker of bonds (**sandhiccheda**), who has made an end of rebirth (**Hatāvakāsa**) and has discarded craving (**Vantāsa**) ---he, indeed, is a Supreme person (**uttamaporisa**).

Comment: **Invertion of Common Sanskrit and Pali Terms**

**(1). Assaddho (A + Saddhā)**

- **Root:** *śrad-* (heart) + *dhā-* (to place).

- **Literal Meaning:** \"One who does not place their heart \[in the hands of another\].\"

- **Linguistic Analysis:** In standard usage, *Saddhā* is the conviction or trust in the words of a teacher. By adding the privative prefix **A-**, it becomes \"faithless.\"

- **Etymological Shift:** For the *uttamaporiso* (noblest person), "assaddho" indicate the fulfillment of **Aparapaccayā**---complete non-reliance on others. Having moved from belief to direct vision, and having realized union with Nibbāna-dhātu, the need to "place the heart" in any system, teacher, or structure falls away. This marks the emergence of true sovereignty: a fully self-established citta that depends on no external worldly condition for its stability or knowing. Established as Atta-dīpa and Atta-saraṇa, the purified and liberated being stands beyond all worldly supports---as the complete transcendence of dependence itself.

  Even while still present in the world with the residual body, the liberated being may associate with humans, communities, or even Brahma beings; yet such association is purely functional and never constitutes dependence. The citta, having realized Aparapaccayā, does not lean on any being, realm, or structure for support, identity, or refuge. Thus, while interaction continues on the surface of existence, inwardly there is complete sovereignty: the liberated one is fully established as Atta-dīpa and Atta-saraṇa, untouched by the relational fabric of the world, even while moving within it.

**(2). Akataññū (A + Kata + Aññū)**

This is the most brilliant linguistic pun in the *Dhammapada*.

- **Roots:** **A-**: Negative/Privative prefix.

  - **Kata**: Past participle of *karoti* (to make, do, or construct).

  - **Aññū**: From *jānāti* (to know/realize).

- **Worldly Analysis:** *Akata* (what has not been done) + *aññū* (knowing). In common Pali, this means someone who \"does not acknowledge what has been done for them\"---an ingrate.

- **Dhamma Analysis:** Here, **Akata (Unmade)** is a technical synonym for **Nibbāna-Dhātu**, because Nibbāna is the only realm that is not \"made\" (*sankhata*) by worldly causes.

- **Conclusion:** The Arahant is \"The Knower of the Unmade.\" They have realized that which belongs outside the laws of the **loka** (world).

**(3). Sandhicchedo (Sandhi + Chedo)**

- **Roots:**

  - **Sandhi**: Connection, joint, or junction (often used for the \"joints\" of rebirth or the grammar of joining words).

  - **Chedo**: From *chid-* (to cut or sever).

- **Linguistic Analysis:** In a social context, a *sandhicchedo* was a \"hole-cutter\" or a burglar who broke into houses by cutting through the mud walls.

- **Etymological Shift:** Here it is repurposed to mean one who has severed the \"joint\" of *Paṭicca-samuppāda* (Dependent Origination). They have broken into the \"House of the Builder\" (as mentioned in verses 153-154) and cut the connections of **rāga, dosa, and moha** that bind the **Citta** to involuntary reincarnation.

**(4). Hatāvakāso (Hata + Avakāsa)**

- **Roots:**

  - **Hata**: Past participle of *hanati* (to kill, strike, or destroy).

  - **Avakāsa**: Space, opportunity, or room.

- **Linguistic Analysis:** Usually refers to a \"hopeless case\" or someone who has lost all opportunity for success.

- **Etymological Shift:** Here *Avakāsa* refers to the \"space\" required for karma to sprout. If there is no \"space\" (no clinging or craving), the seeds of past actions have nowhere to land. The Arahant has \"slain\" the very possibility of future **dukkha**.

**(5). Vantāso (Vanta + Āsa)**

- **Roots:**

  - **Vanta**: From *vam-* (to vomit/spit out).

  - **Āsa**: Desire, hope, or expectation.

- **Linguistic Analysis:** Someone who has \"spit out their hopes\"---often implying a person in despair.

- **Etymological Shift:** For one who has achieved **Brahmacariya**, this signifies the complete expulsion of the taints. They have \"vomited\" out the world.

**9(98). Gāme vā yadi vāraññe, ninne vā yadi vā thale;\
           Yatthārahanto viharanti, taṃ bhūmirāmaṇeyyakaṃ.**

Whether in village or in forest, in valley or on hill, wherever Arahants dwell, that place is delightful. 

**10(99). Ramaṇīyāni araññāni, yattha na ramatī jano;\
             Vītarāgā ramissanti, na te kāmagavesino.**

Delightful are the forests, where ordinary people find no joy; those without rāga (worldly craving and attachment) will delight there, for they seek no sensual pleasures.

#### Chapter 12. Atta Vagga - Self/Soul 

**1(157). Attānañce piyaṃ jaññā, rakkheyya naṃ surakkhitaṃ;\
              Tiṇṇam aññataraṃ yāmaṃ, paṭijaggeyya paṇḍito.**

If one holds one\'s self/soul dear, one should protect it well. At least in one of the three ages of life, a wise man should be vigilant.

**Comment: The Three Ages (Tiṇṇam Yāmaṃ)**

In ancient India, the human lifespan is traditionally divided into three distinct stages, each roughly thirty to thirty-five years. The text suggests that the **paṇḍito** (wise man) must be vigilant in these periods to protect the self/soul:

1)  **First Age (Youth):** In the first age is the age of formation, with the primary task of study, which provides orientation and right understanding. The highest and most essential subject to study is brahmacariya---the disciplined way of life that safeguards the citta from early entanglement in sensuality. Through brahmacariya, the foundations of Sīla are established, preventing the dissipation of energy and the formation of worldly binding habits. Thus, to "protect the self" in youth is to train and stabilize the citta at its most impressionable stage, ensuring that its trajectory is set not toward the world, but toward spirituality and liberation.

2)  **Second Age (Adulthood):** The period of family, professional responsibility, and "worldly" engagement. Vigilance is required here to ensure that the pursuit of wealth or status does not cause one to neglect **Brahmacariya**. In the second age of life, the task shifts from formation to stabilization. The citta, already oriented through study and brahmacariya, must now be firmly established in Sīla and deepened through Samādhi while fully exposed to the pressures of worldly existence. Adulthood presents not only gross distractions but subtle forms of attachment through responsibility, identity, and accomplishment. Thus, to "protect the self" in this stage is to maintain unwavering discipline and inward detachment, preventing the citta from leaning on any role or condition. Brahmacariya continues here as a force of containment and intensification, ensuring that the energy of the citta is not dissipated but directed toward irreversible progress. In this way, the island constructed in youth becomes fortified, and the path toward liberation is made stable and unshakeable.

3)  **Third Age (Old Age):** The final threshold before the dissolution of the body. Vigilance here is critical for the **Citta** to remain **Sugato** (directed to wholesomeness) as it prepares for the transition. In the third age of life, the task is the consummation of the path through the full transformation of identity. One rises from worldly identity to a purified Brahmic identity through brahmacariya and brahmavihāra, aligning with higher beings such as the ancestral Brahma gods. With the help of this refined and world-transcending identity, the citta is fully purified in the purified identity of Arahant, where all defilements are extinguished. Finally, a world-transcended, liberated identity is established in the Tathāgata---Nibbāna-dhātu--based identity. Anchored in unshakable values that govern the Manussa (race of man) order, the citta completes its ascent, transcends all worlds, and abides in the other shore of the deathless realm.

The term **aññataraṃ** (one of the three) implies a hierarchy of effort based on a person's past actions and current circumstances:

- **The Ideal:** Ideally, a person is vigilant throughout all three ages.

- **The Minimum Requirement:** If one has lived a life of rāga, dosa, and moha during youth and adulthood, it is not "too late." The text emphasizes that one must wake up and be vigilant **at least** in the final stage.

- **The Warning:** It serves as a pragmatic warning. If a person neglects the soul in all three stages, there is no way to liberation. They remain bound to the involuntary cycles of death and dukkha because they failed to guard the soul when they had the capacity.

The "protection" (rakkha) mentioned is the active guarding of the soul from falling into unwholesome states. This vigilance (paṭijaggeyya) ensures that the mind does not cling to what is worldly (loka) and instead prepares for transcendence.

Comment: **Attā Is Not Merely "Self": Recovering the Meaning of Soul**

For over a century, the translation of *attā* (Pāli) and *ātman* (Sanskrit) as "self" has shaped how the teaching is understood in English. This choice, though now conventional, is neither neutral nor sufficient. It narrows a term of **ontological depth** into a **psychological abstraction**, and in doing so, redirects the aim of the path itself.

A more accurate rendering---both linguistically and doctrinally---is:

**Attā / Ātman = Soul**

**(1). The Problem with "Self"**

In modern English usage, *self* refers to:

- personality (self-image, self-expression)

- psychological processes

- social identity

These are precisely the domains the teaching identifies as **conditioned and unstable**.

To translate *attā* as "self" therefore creates a conceptual distortion:

it suggests that the path concerns **refinement of personality**, rather than **liberation of the being from the world**.

This is why contemporary readers fall into the contradiction of pursuing a "self-less self"---a linguistic artifact of mistranslation.

**(2). Why "Soul" Fits the Term**

The English word *soul* carries features that align far more closely with *attā / ātman*:

- it refers to the **animating principle of life**

- it implies **continuity beyond a single lifetime**

- it carries **moral and existential weight**

- it is something that can be **endangered, purified, or saved**

This matches the functional role of the **citta** in Gautama the Supreme Arahant's teaching:

the citta is the carrier of the Soul---the locus of continuity, defilement, purification, and liberation.

**(3). Etymological Convergence (Indo-European Layer)**

When examined etymologically, the case becomes stronger.

**Sanskrit / Pāli**

- **ātman / attā** --- originally linked to **breath, vital force, inner essence**

**Greek**

- **ψυχή (*psychē*)** --- breath, life, soul

- also connected to animation and continuity of life

**Latin**

- **anima** --- breath, spirit, soul

- **animus** --- inner principle, mind, life-force

**Old English / Germanic**

- **sāwol / sāwl (soul)** --- though debated, consistently used to denote the **inner life-principle that survives death**

Across these traditions, a common semantic field appears:

**breath → life → animating essence → enduring principle**

This is exactly the semantic field of *ātman*.

By contrast:

- *self* derives from Proto-Germanic *selbaz* → meaning **same, identical**

- it emphasizes **identity**, not **essence**

So linguistically:

**"self" encodes sameness**\
**"soul" encodes life-principle**

Only the latter matches *attā*.

**(4). Doctrinal Necessity: The Weight of Anattā**

The consequences become decisive when we examine **anattā**.

If translated as:

- **"no-self"** → it becomes a psychological claim

- "there is no fixed identity"

But correctly understood as:

**"not-soul"** → it becomes an ontological clarification

Namely:

**the five aggregates are not the Soul**

- rūpa

- vedanā

- saññā

- saṅkhāra

- viññāṇa

These are:

- worldly conditioned

- impermanent

- part of the world-system

They are not:

- the carrier of continuity

- the entity that is liberated

Thus: **Anattā does not deny the Soul---it denies misidentifying the world as the Soul.**

**(5). Reading Dhammapada 160 Correctly**

**attā hi attano nātho**

**ko hi nātho paro siyā**

**attanā va sudantena**

**nāthaṁ labhati dullabhaṁ**

**Literal rendering:**

**The self is indeed the protector of the soul.\
Who else indeed could be the protector?\
Only when the soul is well-tamed,\
one obtains a protector that is hard to obtain.**

This verse makes a critical distinction:

- **attā** --- the acting, disciplining principle

- **attano** --- that which is protected, the belonging essence

This allows a precise functional reading:

**The self (as disciplining agent) protects the soul (as the deeper ontological principle).**

Through **taming (sudantena)**:

- the citta becomes a **nātha** (protector, master)

- the Soul becomes **secured from the world**

**(6). Liberation Requires Ontological Language**

The path taught by **Gautama the Supreme Arahant** is not:

- psychological optimization

- identity refinement

- emotional balance

It is: **exit from the world (loka)**

Such a goal cannot be carried by minimal language.

- "Self" suggests adjustment

- "Soul" implies **risk, gravity, and salvation**

And this is exact: the Soul can be defiled, bound, purified, and finally liberated into Nibbāna-dhātu.

(7). Reclaiming the Sacred Meaning of the Path

To restore **Soul** is to restore honesty. It affirms that beings are more than biological systems or psychological narratives. It affirms that the Soul can be endangered, purified, restored, and ultimately **saved**.

**Brahmacariya exists for one reason:** to free the Soul from the world.

Through ethical withdrawal (**Sīla**), concentrated lifting of the mind (**Samādhi**), and liberating realization (**Paññā**), the defiled citta is purified. When liberation (**Vimutti**) is complete, the Soul leaves the world entirely and abides in **Nibbāna-dhātu**, the **Deathless realm**.

Only there does the Soul become **permanent and eternal, deathless and blissful**. This is not a metaphor. It is the destination.

Restoring **Soul** restores the seriousness of liberation itself---and reveals again what the path was always meant to accomplish.

***"Atta-dīpā viharatha, atta-saraṇā, anañña-saraṇā;\
dhamma-dīpā, dhamma-saraṇā, anañña-saraṇā."***\
"Dwell with your Soul as your island, your Soul as your refuge, there is no other refuges; My Dhamma as your island, my Dhamma as your refuge, there is no other refuges."

- Gautama the Supreme Arahant\
  (DN 16, Mahāparinibbāna Sutta)\
  (DN 26, Cakkavatti-Sīhanāda Sutta)\
  (SN 22.43, Attadīpa Sutta)

**To translate attā as "self" is to reduce an ontological principle to a psychological construct. The term properly denotes the Soul---the animating, enduring principle carried by the citta, capable of defilement and purification, and destined either for continued entanglement in the world or for liberation beyond it. Anattā does not negate this Soul, but corrects the fundamental error of identifying it with the five worldly aggregates. Thus, the path is not the refinement or dissolution of personality, but the protection, purification, and ultimate release of the Soul into Nibbāna-dhātu, the Deathless realm.**

Subcomment: **Reflections on Atammayatā and the Path**

**Atammayatā** must be understood with exactness. It does not indicate the absence of identity, but **non-identification with the world (loka)**.

It is not *no identity* --- it is **no worldly identity**.

This distinction is decisive.

In MN 113 (Sappurisa Sutta), even at the summit of attainment in this world --- the dimension of neither-perception-nor-non-perception --- the practitioner is cautioned not to identify (**Atammayatā**). In MN 137 (Salayatana-vibhanga Sutta), the progression from diversified equanimity (*nanatta-upekkha*) to unified equanimity (*ekatta-upekkha*), even the most refined states are "formation" (*saṅkhāra*) and shall be Relinquished/Non-identified with. Thus, the path does not culminate in refined states, but in atammayatā --- complete non-identification with all that is worldly.

**1). The Function of Atammayatā**

Atammayatā operates as:

- a **force of disengagement**

- a **safeguard against subtle attachment**

- the **culmination of non-identification with the worldly** **conditioned phenomena**

It prevents the citta from:

- "making itself" out of experience

- identifying with form, perception, or refined states

- binding itself even to the highest attainments within saṃsāra

Thus: **Atammayatā is the completion of non-identification with all that belongs to the world.**

It is the final purification of orientation before transcendence.

**2). Not the End, but the Threshold**

Atammayatā is not the final destination.

It is: **the final disengagement from the world---\
the clearing of all identification with worldly conditioned existence**

Even the most refined states:

- formless attainments

- unified equanimity

- subtle dimensions of perception

are not to be identified with.

This confirms: **Atammayatā operates at the highest limit of the world, ensuring that no residue of worldly identification remains.**

**3). Identity Is Not Destroyed, but Transformed**

The path does not culminate in the absence of identity, but in its **complete transformation and transcendence**, as already established:

- **Worldly identity** → bound to aggregates and decay

- **Brahmic divine identity** → luminous, world-transcending, aligned with the ancestral **Brahma gods**

- **Purified identity (Arahant)** → free from defilements

- **Liberated identity (Tathāgata)** → established in Nibbāna-dhātu

Atammayatā governs this entire movement by ensuring:

**no identification with the world persists at any stage**

**4). Compatibility of the Three Identities**

These identities are not mutually exclusive or sequentially discarded.

They are **compatible and integrative**:

- The **Brahmic divine identity** provides the **world-transcending force**

- The **Arahant identity** establishes **complete purification (Brahmic divine identity becomes Arahant)**

- The **Tathāgata identity** fulfills **final liberation beyond the world** (**Arahant identity becomes Tathāgata**)

Thus: **they do not replace one another---they converge**

With the support of Brahmic alignment:

- the citta is elevated beyond kāma realm

- purification completes → Arahant

- liberation stabilizes → Tathāgata

So rather than "dropping identity," the process is:

**refinement → purification → transcendence → establishment**

**5). The Final Crossing**

This resolves with clarity:

- **Atammayatā** → complete non-identification with the world

- **Vimutti** → release from the world

- **Nibbāna-dhātu** → the real, world-transcending realm od existence

Thus: **the path is not toward nothingness, but toward complete freedom from the world**

The designation of the **Tathāgata** confirms this:

- *tathā* --- there (the Deathless realm)

- *gata* --- gone, arrived, or lead to

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**Atammayatā is the consummate discipline of non-identification---not the absence of identity, but the complete refusal to identify with anything within the world. It functions as the final safeguard at the highest levels of practice, ensuring that the citta does not bind itself even to the most refined states of existence. Through this non-identification, identity itself is not destroyed but transformed: from worldly identity to a purified Brahmic divine identity, from there to the purified identity of the Arahant, and finally to the liberated identity of the Tathāgata, established in Nibbāna-dhātu. These are not abandoned but integrated---each supporting the ascent of the citta. Anchored in the law of values that governs the Manussa (race of man) order and aligned with the ancestral Brahmic lineage, the citta completes its disengagement from the world and crosses fully to the Deathless realm.**

**2(158). Attānam eva paṭhamaṃ, patīrūpe nivesaye;\
              Ath'aññam anusāseyya, na kilisseyya paṇḍito.**

Let one first establish one's self/soul in a proper state, and then one may instruct others. Such a wise man will not be defiled. 

**3(159). Attānaṃ ce tathā kayirā, yathaññamanusāsati;**

**Sudanto vata dametha, attā hi kira duddamo.**

If one should act towards one's self/soul in the same way that one instructs others, then, being well-tamed, one would truly tame others; for the self/soul is indeed difficult to tame, it is said.

**4(160). Attā hi attano nātho, ko hi nātho paro siyā;\
             Attanā'va sudantena, nāthaṃ labhati dullabhaṃ.**

The self is indeed the protector** **of the soul. Who else indeed could be the protector? With a self well-tamed, one attains a protector** ** that is hard to find.

**5(161). Attanā hi kataṃ pāpaṃ, attajaṃ attasambhavaṃ;\
              Abhimatthati dummedhaṃ, vajiraṃ v'asmamayaṃ maṇiṃ.**

Indeed, the evil done by the self, born of the soul, originated from the soul, crushes the foolish one, just as a diamond crushes a stone gem.

**6(162). Yassa accantadussīlyaṃ, māluvā sālam iv'otataṃ;\
              Karoti so tathattānaṃ, yathā naṃ icchatī diso.**

Whose misconduct is extreme, like a creeper entwining a Sal tree, that person does to himself just what his enemy would wish. 

**7(163). Sukarāni asādhūni, attano ahitāni ca;\
              Yaṃ 've hitañca sādhuñca, taṃ ve paramadukkaraṃ.**

Easy to do are unvirtuous deeds, and things harmful to the soul; but truly, that which is beneficial and virtuous is extremely difficult to do. 

**\**

**8(164). Yo sāsanaṃ arahataṃ, ariyānaṃ dhammajīvinaṃ;\
              Paṭikkosati dummedho, diṭṭhiṃ nissāya pāpikaṃ;\
              Phalāni kaṭṭhakass'eva, attaghātāya phallati.**

Whoever rejects the teaching of the Arahants, of the noble ones who live by the Dhamma, relying on an evil view, that foolish one---just like the fruits of the poisonous tree---it results in self-harm and ruin.

**9(165). Attanā hi kataṃ pāpaṃ, attanā saṃkilissati;\
              Attanā akataṃ pāpaṃ, attanā'va visujjhati;\
              Suddhi asuddhi paccattaṃ, n'āñño aññaṃ visodhaye.**

By the soul indeed is evil done; by the soul is one defiled. By the soul is evil left undone; by the soul alone is one purified. Purity and impurity are strictly of the soul; no one can purify another. 

**10(166). Attadatthaṃ paratthena, bahunā'pi na hāpaye;\
                Attadattham abhiññāya, sadatthapasuto siyā.**

One should not neglect the self's purpose for the sake of others, however great that may be. Having clearly understood the self's purpose, one should be devoted to one's own true goal. 

Comment: **The True Purpose of the Soul**

1). The Work of Purification

The soul's primary objective is the eradication of **rāga** (worldly craving and attachment), **dosa** (survival instinct of fear and anger), and **moha** (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the liberation path). These are not merely "character flaws" but are the binding forces of **the citta** to the world that lead to to death and **dukkha**. Purity is not a gift; it is a mechanical result of the soul being **well-tamed** (*sudanto*). The soul must act as its own "savior" by using **Concentration (Samadhi)** and **Wisdom (Panna)** to strip away the worldly stains that prevent it from seeing its true direction.

The soul's true purpose is to be purified. This leads to the **ancient path to true freedom**, where the soul no longer seeks to improve its worldly condition but instead seeks to transcend it entirely. The world is the "stone gem" that is eventually crushed; the soul is the diamond that must remain unbroken and exit the cycle. The home and destination for the soul is the deathless realm of **Nibbāna**. This is only possible for a soul that has fulfilled its **Brahmacariya** and reached the state of an **Arahant**.

2). Absolute Self-Responsibility

This framework rejects all external agency. Because "purity and impurity are strictly of the soul," the practitioner accepts that:

- **No one purifies another:** No deity, teacher, or ritual can perform the final taming.

- **The priority is internal:** One must not neglect the soul's purpose for the sake of others, for a soul that is not yet established in the proper state cannot truly guide another.

- **The soul is the architect:** Every action done by the soul, born of the soul, and originated from the soul determines whether it remains crushed by the world or rises to transcend it.

**The True Purpose:** To transform the soul from an untamed agent of worldly manifestation into a purified essence (**Sugato**) capable of navigating to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

#### Chapter 13. Loka Vagga -- World

**1(167). Hīnaṃ dhammaṃ na seveyya, pamādena na saṃvase;\
              Micchādiṭṭhiṃ na seveyya, na siyā lokavaḍḍhano.**

One should not associate with inferior teachings; one should not connect with zealless. One should not embrace wrong views; one should not be one who increases (attachment to) the worldly.

**2(168). Uttiṭṭhe nappamajjeyya, dhammaṃ sucaritaṃ care;\
             Dhammacārī sukhaṃ seti, asmiṃ loke paramhi ca.**

One should strive and not be negligent; one should practice the Dhamma well. The one who lives according to the Dhamma lives happily in this world and in the next. 

**3(169). Dhammaṃ care sucaritaṃ, na naṃ duccaritaṃ care;\
             Dhammacārī sukhaṃ seti, asmiṃ loke paramhi ca.**

One should practice the Dhamma well, one should not practice it badly. The one who lives according to the Dhamma lives happily in this world and in the next. 

**4(170). Yathā pubbuḷakaṃ passe, yathā passe marīcikaṃ;\
             Evaṃ lokaṃ avekkhantaṃ, maccurājā na passati.**

Just as one should see a bubble, just as one should see a mirage, so one who observes the world thus---the king of death does not see him.

**5(171). Etha passath'imaṃ lokaṃ, cittaṃ rājarathūpamaṃ;\
             Yattha bālā visīdanti, natthi saṅgo vijānataṃ.**

Come, behold this world---variegated and constructed like a royal chariot; where fools become immersed, but for those who truly know, there is no attachment.

Comment: **Key Etymological Clarification**

**(1). cittaṃ (adjectival usage in this verse)**

- From √*cit* in the sense of:

  - to form (by citta)

  - to construct mentally (from citta)

  - to vary / diversify perception

It means: **the condition of being mentally constructed, variegated, and perceptually assembled**

Thus: cittaṃ lokaṃ = "the world as mentally constructed appearance"

it is not **citta:**

- From √*cit* = to know, perceive, be conscious

- **citta (noun)** = the mind-heart, the rider / observing principle

It is the **knowing position within experience**, not the content of experience itself.

**(2). rājarathūpamaṃ**

This is a compound of three elements:

- **rāja** → king, royal (signifying refinement, complexity)

- **ratha** → chariot (a constructed vehicle composed of interdependent parts)

- **upamā / upamaṃ** → from *upa + √mā*:

  - **upa** = near, alongside, in proximity

  - **√mā** = to measure, to delimit, to compare structurally

**Core literal sense: "a measured comparison placed alongside a royal chariot"**

**Functional meaning in this verse: a cognitive model used to measure the constructed nature of experience against a composite vehicle**

So *rājarathūpamaṃ* more precisely means:

"to be understood through the measuring-alignment of a royal chariot as a model of constructed, composite appearance"

In other words, it is not decorative simile---it is a **diagnostic instrument of perception**.

**\**

**(3). rājaratha (structural reference)**

- **ratha** = assembled vehicle (wheels, axle, frame, reins, horses)

- **rāja** = emphasizing complexity, ornamentation, apparent unity and sovereignty

Thus: **rājaratha = a complex, assembled system that appears unified but is functionally composite**

In doctrinal terms: the experiential world as a structured but non-essential aggregation of dhammas

**(4). saṅgo**

- from √*sañj* = to cling, attach, stick together

Thus: **saṅga = binding through misrecognition of constructed appearance as true existence**

**(5). vijānataṃ**

- vi- = distinct, clear separation

- √*jñā* / √*ñā* = to know, discern

Thus: **vijānataṃ = those who know through clear discernment of structure (not conceptual belief)**

"Come, behold this conditioned world---this field of experience as it is measured and understood through the model of a royal chariot: a structured, composite appearance that seems unified but is assembled from parts. Here the untrained sink into immersion and identification, but for those who clearly discern its constructed nature, there is no binding attachment."

Comment: **The Body as Micro-Universe, the Chariot of Experience, and the Unsurpassed Charioteer (Anuttaro-purisa-damma-sārathi)**

In ancient India, a sārathi (charioteer) is a master of horses (symbolizing the mind and senses). We designate Gautama the Supreme Arahant as the supreme sārathi --- the best to tame the inner qualities (dhammas) of his mind.

Gautama the Supreme Arahant\'s path to liberation is intrinsically linked to the practice of taming the mind. The mind is seen as the source of both defilements and liberation. An untrained mind is easily swayed by desires, aversions, and delusions, leading to actions that perpetuate the cyclic existence of suffering (samsara). Conversely, a tamed and disciplined mind is the key to attaining Nibbana, the ultimate liberation from the world of suffering. To tame the mind, as this title implies, is to train it --- like a powerful horse --- toward liberation, wisdom, and peace.

**(1). The Body as Micro-Universe: The Chariot of Formation**

The body is a **micro-universe of conditioned processes**---a self-contained system of elements, sensations, and biological motion.

Like a chariot, the body is:

- composed of interdependent parts

- maintained through continuous conditions

- capable of movement through supporting forces

It is the **visible vehicle of existence**, the structural base through which experience becomes localized.

Thus: The body is the chariot of manifestation---the micro-universe in which lived experience takes form. It is a **constructed platform upon which experience unfolds**.

**(2). The Horses: Dhammas as Driving Forces**

Within this embodied chariot-system, movement is driven by **dhammas**---mental and instinctive forces arising from the citta (mind-heart):

- rāga (worldly craving and attachment)

- dosa (survival instinct of fear and anger)

- moha (ignorance of not knowing the liberation and delusion of the true reality)

- and the full spectrum of reactive tendencies

These are the **horses of the defiled mind / system**.

They are **energetic forces that generate motion**. But without training, they pull in conflicting directions, producing instability, suffering, and fragmentation of experience.

Thus: The horses are not the problem---their lack of training is.

**(3). The Mind (Citta): The Rider of Experience**

The **citta** is the rider---the knowing principle that experiences the entire system. It is:

- the observer of motion

- the knower of states

- the witness of arising dhammas

But it is not automatically in control. It can either:

- become absorbed in the movement of the chariot, or

- the structure of the system it is experiencing

When untrained, the rider is carried away by the horses. When trained, the rider begins to understand and guide them.

Thus: The mind is the witnessing rider within the chariot-system of experience.

**(4). The Chariot as Lived World (Loka)**

The **loka**---the world---is not external scenery alone. It is:

the total experiential field generated through body, senses, and mental formations. In this sense, the world is the **chariot in motion**.

What appears as "reality" is the coordinated functioning of:

- body (structure)

- dhammas (forces)

- citta (knowing)

When seen without clarity, the rider (citta) is carried by the horses and mistaken as part of the system. When seen with insight, the rider is recognized as independent stable essence of knowing, trapped in the system, and the horses are understood as conditioned forces that can be trained, harmonized, and directed the rider toward liberation.

**(5). The Unsurpassed Charioteer: Anuttaro-purisa-damma-sārathi**

The title **Anuttaro-purisa-damma-sārathi** means:

- **Anuttaro** → unsurpassed

- **purisa** → man, human being

- **damma (dhamma)** → the mental qualities, dispositions, and forces arising within citta
