- **sārathi** → charioteer, trainer, guide

Together, the meaning is: "The unsurpassed trainer of human nature, the master charioteer of dhammas." Thus the charioteer function is: the capacity to understand, regulate, and refine the dhammas of the mind until they move in harmony toward liberation.

**(6). Taming the Horses: The Path of Training**

Taming is **alignment through understanding**. The training unfolds gradually:

- **Precepts (sila)** → stabilize the chariot system

- **Concentration (samādhi)** → stabilization of the mind

- **Wisdom (paññā)** → clear recognition of constructed nature of the chariot and the world, and the liberation path

Through this process:

- impulsive forces are no longer chaotic

- attention becomes steady

- experience becomes transparent rather than compulsive

The chariot still moves---but it no longer runs uncontrolled, but on the clear path toward liberation.

**(7). The Goal: Liberation from Misidentification**

The final transformation is **cognitive liberation**. The practitioner realizes:

- the body is a constructed micro-universe

- the horses are conditioned forces

- the chariot is a functional system, not self / soul

- the rider is the true self / soul, with pure awareness, not ownership of the chariot system

When this is seen clearly, attachment (saṅga) ceases, because this chariot system is not grasped as part of the true self, it is a vehicle. The system continues to function, but with a clear goal of liberation from the whole system.

The teaching of the royal chariot and the unsurpassed charioteer forms a single integrated vision:

- The **body** is the chariot: a micro-universe of conditioned formation

- The **dhammas** are the horses: driving forces of motion

- The **citta** is the rider: the knowing principle of experience, the true self and soul

- The **Buddha as Sārathi** is the principle of unsurpassed training: the perfected mastery of all mental forces

And the entire path is summed up in one movement: from uncontrolled motion → to trained alignment → to clear seeing → to liberation from the world

The "royal chariot" is a precise model of lived experience as a structured system. The "unsurpassed charioteer" is the highest expression of disciplined intelligence applied to the total field of human existence.

When the horses are trained, the chariot becomes stable and on the right path. When the rider is clear, the system becomes purposeful, not chaotic. When attachment ceases, motion continues for liberation from the world without bondage.

This is the meaning of **Anuttaro-purisa-dhamma-sārathi --- the unsurpassed trainer of human nature, guiding the chariot of existence toward liberation.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**6(172). Yo ca pubbe pamajjitvā, pacchā so nappamajjati;\
             So imaṃ lokaṃ pabhāseti, abbhā mutto'va candimā.**

Whoever, having previously been negligent, afterwards is not negligent, he illuminates this world, just as the moon is freed from the clouds.

**7(173). Yassa pāpaṃ kataṃ kammaṃ, kusalena pidhīyati;\
              So imaṃ lokaṃ pabhāseti, abbhā mutto'va candimā.**

Whose bad deed that has been done is covered by good deeds, he illuminates this world, just as the moon is freed from the clouds.

**8(174). Andhabhūto ayaṃ loko, tanuk'ettha vipassati;\
              Sakuṇo jālamutto'va, appo saggāya gacchati.**

This world is blinded (ignorance); few here see with discernment. Like a bird released from a net, few reach heaven. 

**9(175). Haṃsādiccapathe yanti, ākāse yanti iddhiyā;\
              Nīyanti dhīrā lokamhā, jetvā māraṃ savāhiniṃ.**

Swans go on the path of the sun; they go in the sky by means of psychic power. The wise are led away from the world, having conquered Māra (deva od death) with his forces.

**10(176). Ekaṃ dhammaṃ atītassa, musāvādissa jantuno;\
                Vitiṇṇaparalokassa, natthi pāpaṃ akāriyaṃ.**

For the person who has transgressed the one law (truth), who speaks lies, and who Rejects the next world (not believe in next life), there is no evil that cannot be done.

**11(177). Na ve kadariyā devalokaṃ vajanti, bālā have nappasaṃsanti dānaṃ; Dhīro ca dānaṃ anumodamāno, ten'eva so hoti sukhī parattha.**

Misers do not go to the realms of the devas; fools indeed do not praise generosity. But the wise one, rejoicing in giving, and thus becomes happy in the next life.

**12(178). Pathabyā ekarajjena, saggassa gamanena vā;\
               Sabbalokādhipaccena, sotāpattiphalaṃ varaṃ.**

By (ruling) the whole earth as sole king, or by going to heaven, or by sovereignty over the entire world, the fruit of sotāpanna (stream-entry) is better.

#### Chapter 20. Magga Vagga -- Path

**1(273). Maggān'aṭṭhaṅgiko seṭṭho, saccānaṃ caturo padā;\
              Virāgo seṭṭho dhammānaṃ, dvipadānañca cakkhumā.**

The Eightfold Path is the best of paths, the Four Noble Truths are the best of truths; Fading off worldly passion (**virāga**) is the best of dhamma, and The One Endowed with Vision (**cakkhumā**) is the best of bipeds. 

**2(274). Eso'va maggo natth'añño, dassanassa visuddhiyā;\
             Etañhi tumhe paṭipajjatha, mārass'etaṃ pamohanaṃ.**

This is the only path, there is no other for the purification of vision (dassana); Follow this (path), for it is the confounding of Māra (deva of death).

**3(275). Etañhi tumhe paṭipannā, dukkhassantaṃ karissatha;\
             Akkhāto vo mayā maggo, aññāya sallakantanaṃ.**

For having entered upon this \[path\], you will make an end of dukkha; The path was declared to you by me, having known the pulling out of the arrow (of defilements).

**4(276). Tumhehi kiccaṃ ātappaṃ, akkhātāro Tathāgatā;\
              Paṭipannā pamokkhanti, jhāyino mārabandhanā.**

The effort must be made by you, the Tathāgatas are \[only\] teachers; Those who have entered the path and practice jhana (Jhāyi) will be freed from the bond of Māra.

**5(277). "Sabbe saṅkhārā aniccā"ti, yadā paññāya passata;\
              Atha nibbindati dukkhe, esa maggo visuddhiyā.**

'All saṅkhārā (worldly formations) are impermanent,' when one sees this with Paññā; Then one disenchants with dukkha; this is the path to purification.

**6(278). "Sabbe saṅkhārā dukkhā"ti, yadā paññāya passata;\
             Atha nibbindati dukkhe, esa maggo visuddhiyā.**

'All saṅkhārā are dukkha,' when one sees this with Paññā; Then one disenchants with dukkha; this is the path to purification.

**7(279). "Sabbe dhammā anattā"ti, yadā paññāya passata;\
               Atha nibbindati dukkhe, esa maggo visuddhiyā.**

'All dhammā are anatta (not-self),' when one sees this with Paññā; Then one disenchants with dukkha; this is the path to purification.

**8(280). Uṭṭhānakālamhi anuṭṭhahāno, yuvā balī ālasiyaṃ upeto;\
    Saṃsannasaṅkappamano kusīto, paññāya maggaṃ alaso na vindati.**

One who does not strive when it is time to strive, who, though young and strong, is full of laziness; With a mind of sluggish intentions and indolence, the lazy person does not find the path to Paññā.

**9(281). Vācānurakkhī manasā susaṃvuto, kāyena ca nākusalaṃ kayirā;\
    Ete tayo kammapathe visodhaye, ārādhaye maggaṃ isippaveditaṃ.**

Guarding speech, well-restrained in will (*mano*), and doing no unwholesome act with the body; One should purify these three channels of kamma (**kammapathe**), and accomplish the path declared by the Seers (**Isi**).

**10(282). Yogā ve jāyatī bhūri, ayogā bhūrisaṅkhayo;\
               Etaṃ dvedhā pathaṃ ñatvā, bhavāya vibhavāya ca;\
               Tath'attānaṃ niveseyya, yathā bhūri pavaḍḍhati.**

From meditation (yoga), the broadened mind (**bhūri**) is born; from the lack of meditation (yoga), the broadening of the mind decays; Having known this twofold path of progress and decline, one should so establish oneself that the broadened mind may increase.

Comment: **Bhūri: Broad as the Earth**

**Yogā (Meditation)** is the daily harness of **Concentration (Samadhi)**.

- **The Earth Metaphor:** Just as the earth is vast, a **bhūri** mind has developed the capacity to see at the scale of the world and the universe.

- **The Results of Meditation (Yogā):** **Bhūri** is the scale. Through **Concentration (Samadhi)**, the **Citta** expands. It is no longer a small, cramped space trapped by sensory desires; it becomes a vast field of awareness that recognizes the multi-dimensional, existential nature of the mind and all beings---including the race of man---within this world, the universe, and beyond.

- **The Decay (Ayogā):** Without the **yoga** of meditation, the mind shrinks. It becomes narrow, fixated on small worldly gains, and easily \"pierced\" by the arrow of craving. This is **bhūrisaṅkhayo**---the shrinking or destruction of that expansive state.

**11(283). Vanaṃ chindatha mā rukkhaṃ, vanato jāyati bhayaṃ;\
                Chetvā vanañca vanathañca, nibbanā hotha bhikkhavo.**

Cut down the forest (metaphor for the thicket of craving), not \[just\] a tree; from the forest, fear is born. Having cut down both the forest and the undergrowth (clearing the brush), be without a forest, O bhikkhus.

**12(284). Yāva hi vanatho na chijjati, aṇumatto'pi narassa nārisu;\
                Paṭibaddhamano tāva so, vaccho khīrapako'va mātari.**

As long as even a small amount of passion for women is not cut off in a man, So long is his mind bound, like a calf to its mother for milk. 

For as long as the undergrowth (the brush of desire), even the smallest amount, is not cut down, of a man toward women; So long is the mind bound, like a suckling calf to its mother.

**13(285). Ucchinda sineham attano, kumudaṃ sāradikaṃ 'va \[pāṇinā\];\
                Santi-maggam eva brūhaya, nibbānaṃ sugatena desitaṃ.**

Cut off your own affection (oily attachment, \"glue\" of craving), as one would a lily of the autumn with the hand; Develop only the path to peace, to Nibbāna as declared by the Sugato.

**14(286). Idha vassaṃ vasissāmi, idha hemantagimhisu;\
                Iti bālo vicinteti, antarāyaṃ na bujjhati.**

'Here I will dwell during the rainy season, here in winter and summer'; So the fool thinks, not awakening to the danger.

**\**

**15(287). Taṃ puttapasusammattaṃ, byāsattamanasaṃ naraṃ;\
                Suttaṃ gāmaṃ mahogho'va, maccu ādāya gacchati.**

That man, intoxicated with children (sons) and cattle, with a mind deeply attached (to the worldly); Death carries him away, as a great flood sweeps away a sleeping village.

**16(288). Na santi puttā tāṇāya, na pitā na'pi bandhavā;\
                Antakenādhipannassa, natthi ñātisu tāṇatā.**

There are no children (sons) for protection, no father, nor even kinsmen (relatives); For one seized by the Terminator (**Antaka**, Death), there is no protection among kin (relatives).

**17(289). Etam atthavasaṃ ñatvā, paṇḍito sīlasaṃvuto;\
                Nibbānagamanaṃ maggaṃ, khippam eva visodhaye.** 

Having realized this fact of life, the wise one, well-restrained by Sīla; Should quickly walk the path of purification leading to Nibbāna.

#### Chapter 25. Bhikkhu Vagga -- Bhikkhu

**1(360). Cakkhunā saṃvaro sādhu, sādhu sotena saṃvaro;\
              Ghāṇena saṃvaro sādhu, sādhu jivhāya saṃvaro.**

Restraint in the eye is good; good is restraint in the ear; Restraint in the nose is good; good is restraint in the tongue.

**2(361). Kāyena saṃvaro sādhu, sādhu vācāya saṃvaro;\
              Manasā saṃvaro sādhu, sādhu sabbattha saṃvaro;\
              Sabbattha saṃvuto bhikkhu, sabbadukkhā pamuccati.**

Restraint in the body (Kāyena) is good; good is restraint in speech (vācāya); Restraint in the will (Manasā) is good; good is restraint in everything; The bhikkhu restrained in everything is set free from all dukkha.

**3(362). Hatthasaṃyato pādasaṃyato, vācāyasaṃyato saṃyatuttamo;\
              Ajjhatta-rato samāhito, eko santusito tam āhu bhikkhuṃ.**

Restrained in hand (hattha), restrained in foot (pāda), restrained in speech (vācāya), restrained in the highest \[the will\] (uttama, manasā); Delighting in the High-Self (**Ajjhatta-rata**), concentrated (samāhita), solitary (eka) and content (santusita), him they call a bhikkhu.

Comment: **The Bhikkhu Defined: Restraint, Sovereignty, and the Delight in the High-Self**

A bhikkhu is not defined by robe, institution, or social designation. This Dhammapada verse 362 establishes a **functional and ontological definition**:

**one who has achieved total restraint and has shifted delight from the world to the High-Self.**

**(1). Total Restraint: The Foundation of Sovereignty**

The verse begins with four layers of restraint:

- **Hattha-saṃyata** --- restraint in hands (action)

- **Pāda-saṃyata** --- restraint in feet (movement)

- **Vācā-saṃyata** --- restraint in speech

- **Saṃyata-uttama** --- restraint in the highest: the will (manasā)

This is **strategic containment of the citta**.

Each level prevents outward leakage:

- action → prevents gross entanglement

- movement → prevents habitual drift

- speech → prevents karmic proliferation

- will → prevents internal alignment with craving

When these are unified:

**the citta becomes governed from within, no longer driven by the world**

This is the beginning of **true sovereignty**.

**(2). Ajjhattarato: The Strategic Pivot**

The defining shift appears here: **Ajjhattarato --- delighting in the High-Self**

**Etymological Precision**

- **Ajjhatta (Adhi + Atta)**

  - *Adhi* --- high, above, superior, governing

  - *Atta* --- Self / Soul\
    → **the High-Self, the governing interior principle**

- **Rata** (√ram)\
  → to delight in, to abide in, to find fulfillment in

So: **Ajjhattarato = one who delights in and abides in the High-Self**

**(3). The Shift of the Center of Gravity**

This is the decisive operation of the path:

**the relocation of the citta's center of gravity**

**The Lower Self Identity (Worldly Layers)**

- physical

- energetic

- emotional

- mental

These are:

- worldly conditioned

- reactive

- governed by universal law of cause and effect, law of decay and death

When the citta delights here:

- it becomes **intoxicated (sammatta)**

- it contracts into **worldly identity**

- it is pierced by **craving (rāga)**

These layers are not the true self, or the Soul.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**The High-Self (the True Slef, the Soul)**

When the citta withdraws from the lower layers, it aligns with the **higher strata of being**:

**1). Divine Brahmic Identity**

- established through **brahmacariya** and **brahmavihāra**

- aligned with the law of values, the noble and civilized order of the race of man (manussa)

- free from sensual gravity from the world

This is the **operational platform of transcendence**.

**2). Purified Identity (Arahant)**

- complete cessation of worldly rāga, dosa, moha

- no internal disturbance from the external world

- the citta is fully stabilized from within

This is the **completion of purification**.

**3). Liberated Identity (Tathāgata)**

- fully world-transcended

- established in **Nibbāna-dhātu**

- beyond all worldly conditioned systems

This is the **final realization of the Highest-Self**.

**4). Ajjhattarato as Continuous Orientation**

To be Ajjhattarato is not a moment---it is a **continuous orientation**:

- no delight in sensory stimulation

- no investment in emotional fluctuation

- no identification with mental construction

Instead: **the citta rests in the High-Self as its stable reference point**

This produces:

- **Samāhita** --- deep, unshakable concentration (samadhi)

- **Eka** --- inner singularity, non-fragmentation

- **Santusita** --- complete contentment independent of the world

**5). Solitude and Contentment**

The verse concludes:

- **Eko** --- solitary

- **Santusito** --- content

This is **non-dependence from the world**.

The bhikkhu:

- does not rely on external validation

- does not seek fulfillment in relationships or conditions

- does not derive identity from the world

Thus: **solitude is internal sovereignty**

And **contentment is the natural expression of a self-established citta**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**A bhikkhu is one who has achieved total restraint in action, movement, speech, and will, thereby establishing sovereignty over the citta. Through this discipline, the center of gravity shifts from the worldly layers of existence to the High-Self (Ajjhatta). Delighting in this High-Self, the practitioner aligns first with the Brahmic order, then realizes the purified identity of the Arahant, and finally establishes the liberated identity of the Tathāgata in Nibbāna-dhātu. Established in this inner sovereignty, the citta becomes concentrated, unified, solitary, and fully content---no longer dependent on the world, and no longer subject to its laws. This is who is rightly called a bhikkhu.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**4(363). Yo mukhasaṃyato bhikkhu, mantabhāṇī anuddhato;\
              Atthaṃ dhammañca dīpeti, madhuraṃ tassa bhāsitaṃ.**

The bhikkhu who is restrained in his mouth, speaking with wisdom and not conceited; Who illuminates the Attha (the fact/meaning) and the Dhamma---sweet is his speech.

**5(364). Dhammārāmo dhammarato, dhammaṃ anuvicintayaṃ;\
             Dhammaṃ anussaraṃ bhikkhu, saddhammā na parihāyati.**

The bhikkhu who rests in the Dhamma (**dhammārāma**), delights in the Dhamma (**dhammarata**), high-contemplating (**anu-vi-cintaya**) the Dhamma; High-flow (**anu-sara**) the Dhamma, does not fall away from the True Dhamma (**saddhammā**).

**6(365). Salābhaṃ nātimaññeyya, n'āññesaṃ pihayaṃ care;\
             Aññesaṃ pihayaṃ bhikkhu, samādhiṃ n'ādhigacchati.**

One should not underestimate one\'s own gains, nor live envying others; The bhikkhu who envies others does not attain Concentration (Samadhi).

**7(366). Appalābho'pi ce bhikkhu, salābhaṃ n'ātimaññati;\
             Taṃ ve devā pasaṃsanti, suddhājīviṃ atanditaṃ.**

Even if a bhikkhu has little gain, he does not underestimate his own gains; The devas surely praise him, whose livelihood is pure and who is tireless.

**\**

**8(367). Sabbaso nāmarūpasmiṃ, yassa natthi mamāyitaṃ;\
              Asatā ca na socati, sa ve "bhikkhū"ti vuccati.**

He for whom there is completely no \'mine-making\' toward the psycho-Physical complex (Nāma-rūpa); And who does not suffer over what is not truly real (**asatā**), he is truly called a \'bhikkhu\'.

**9(368). Mettāvihārī yo bhikkhu, pasanno Buddhasāsane;\
             Adhigacche padaṃ santaṃ, saṅkhārūpasamaṃ sukhaṃ.**

The bhikkhu who dwells in Mettā, with confidence in the Buddha's Teaching; May he reach the peaceful state, the happiness of the stilling of worldly formations (Saṅkhāras).

**10(369). Siñca bhikkhu imaṃ nāvaṃ, sittā te lahum essati;\
                Chetvā rāgaṃ ca dosañca, tato nibbānam ehisi.**

Empty this boat, bhikkhu! Emptied, it will go swiftly; Having cut off rāga and dosa, then you will go to Nibbāna.

**11(370). Pañca chinde pañca jahe, pañca c'uttari bhāvaye;\
                Pañca saṅga-atigo bhikkhu, "ogha-tiṇṇo"ti vuccati.**

Cut off five, abandon five, and cultivate five further; The bhikkhu who has acomplished (**atigo**) the (three) groups (**saṅga**) of Fives is called \'one who has crossed (**tiṇṇo**) the flood (**ogha**)\' (meaning Arahant).

Comment: 

**"Five he should cut off":** These are the five lower fetters (*orambhāgiyāni saṃyojanāni*):

1)**. Sakkāyadiṭṭhi:** Body-view, belief in the body (as self).

- **Sakāya:** Of the body, belonging to the body.

- **Diṭṭhi:** View, belief, doctrine.

Attachment to the physical body as being the "self." the body is often the primary basis upon which people construct their sense of self. People identify with their physical appearance, their physical sensations, and their physical capabilities. This identification with the body can lead to attachment to the physical world, craving for physical sensational pleasures, fear of aging and death, and other forms of suffering.

2).** Vicikicchā:** Doubt (especially about Gautama the Supreme Arahant who is the originator of His Dhamma, and the Maha-Sangha who carries on His Dhamma).

3).** Sīlabbataparāmāsa:** Clinging to rites and rituals (mere rituals are only forms of spirituality, can not lead to liberation).

4).** Kāma-rāga:** Sexual and Sensual pleasures and attachment.

5).** Vyāpāda:** Ill-will, hatred, survival instinct of fear and anger.

**"Five he should abandon":** The five higher fetters (*uddhambhāgiyāni saṃyojanāni*):

1.  **Rūpa-rāga:** Attachment to the rūpa form (the fine-material existential states).

2.  **Arūpa-rāga:** Attachment to the arūpa formless (the immaterial existential states).

3.  **Māna:** Conceit, pride.

4.  **Uddhacca:** Restlessness, agitation.

5.  **Avijjā:** Ignorance.

**"Five more he should cultivate":** The five spiritual faculties (*pañcindriyāni*):

1.  **Saddhā:** Faith/trust/confidence.

2.  **Viriya:** Energy/vital force.

3.  **Sati:** Mindfulness/awareness.

4.  **Samādhi:** Concentration.

5.  **Paññā:** Wisdom.

**\**

**12(371). Jhāya bhikkhu mā pāmado, mā te kāmaguṇe ramessu cittaṃ;\
   Mā lohaguḷaṃ gilī pamatto, mā kandi "dukkham idan"ti ḍayhamāno.**

Practice **Jhāna** (Jhāya), bhikkhu! Do not be zealless. Do not let your Citta delight in the strands of sensory pleasure; Do not, being zealless, swallow the red-hot iron ball, lest you cry out \'This is dukkha!\' while being burned.

**13(372). Natthi jhānaṃ apaññassa, paññā natthi ajhāyato;\
                Yamhi jhānañca paññā ca, sa ve nibbānasantike.**

There is no jhāna for one without **paññā**, **paññā** is not for one who does not practice jhāna; In whom there are both jhāna and **paññā**, he is indeed near Nibbāna.

**14(373). Suññāgāraṃ paviṭṭhassa, santacittassa bhikkhuno;\
                Amānusī rati hoti, sammā dhammaṃ vipassato.**

For the **bhikkhu** who has entered an empty dwelling, whose **Citta** is peaceful; There is a more-than-human/divine delight, for one can High-see (**vipassata)** the (high-dimensional) **dhamma**.

**15(374). Yato yato sammasati, khandhānaṃ udayabbayaṃ;\
                Labhatī pīti pāmojjaṃ, amataṃ taṃ vijānataṃ.**

Whenever one understands the arising and passing away of the aggregates (**khandhas**); He obtains joy (**pīti**) and gladness (**pāmojja**), that is for the understanding of the deathless (the realm of Nibbana is out of the jurisdiction of the universal law of decay and death).

**16(375). Tatrāyam ādi bhavati, idha paññassa bhikkhuno;\
                 Indriyagutti santuṭṭhī, pātimokkhe ca saṃvaro.**

Here, that is the beginning for a **bhikkhu** of **paññā**; Guarding of the senses, contentment, and restraint according to the **Pātimokkha** (path to moksha, liberation).

**\**

**17(376). Mitte bhajassu kalyāṇe, suddh'ājīve atandite;\
               Paṭisanthāra-vuty'assa, ācāra-kusalo siyā;\
                Tato pāmojja-bahulo, dukkhass'antaṃ karissati.**

Associate with friends (**Mitte**) who are virtuous/wholesome (**kalyāṇe**), of pure livelihood (**suddh'ājīve**), and diligent (atandite); Let one's manner be hospitable (** Paṭisanthāra-vuty'assa**) and skilled in behavior (**ācāra-kusala**); Then, full of gladness (**pāmojja**), one will make an end of dukkha.

**18(377). Vassikā viya pupphāni, maddavāni pamuñcati;\
                Evaṃ rāgañca dosañca, vippamuñcetha bhikkhavo.**

Just as the jasmine plant sheds its withered flowers; Even so, **bhikkhus**, you should shed **rāga** and **dosa**.

**19(378). Santakāyo santavāco, santavā susamāhito;\
                Vantalokāmiso bhikkhu, "upasanto"ti vuccati.**

Peaceful in body, peaceful in speech, peaceful and well-concentrated; The **bhikkhu** who has vomited the world\'s bait is called \"approaching the peace (upasanto).\"

**20(379). Attanā coday'attānaṃ, paṭi-maṃsetha attanā;\
                So attagutto satimā, sukhaṃ bhikkhu vihāhisi.**

Through the soul (**Attanā**), instigate/arouse (**codaya**) the soul (**Attana**), re-examine (**paṭi-maṃsetha**) the soul (**attanā**); That **bhikkhu**, self-guarded (**attagutto**) and mindful (**satimā**), will live happily.

**21(380). Attā hi attano nātho, attā hi attano gati;\
                Tasmā saṃyamam-attānaṃ, assaṃ bhadraṃ 'va vāṇijo.**

For the self (**Attā**) is the protector (**nātho**) of the soul (**attano**), for the self (**attā**) lead the way of (**gati**) the soul (**attano**); Therefore, control (**saṃyama**) the soul (**attāna**), like a merchant (vāṇijo) do to a well-bred (**bhadra**) horse (**assa**).

**\**

**22(381). Pāmojja-bahulo bhikkhu, pasanno buddhasāsane;\
                Adhigacche padaṃ santaṃ, saṅkhāra-upasamaṃ sukhaṃ.**

The **bhikkhu** full of gladness, with confidence in the **Buddha\'s** teaching; would attain (Adhigacche) a peaceful (**santa**) ground/foundation (**pada**), happiness from thorough stilling of **saṅkhāras**.

**23(382). Yo have daharo bhikkhu, yuñjati Buddhasāsane;\
                So'maṃ lokaṃ pabhāseti, abbhā mutto'va candimā.**

Even a young **bhikkhu** who joins the **Buddha\'s** teaching; He illuminates this world (**loka**) like the moon set free from a cloud.

#### Chapter 26. Brāhmaṇa Vagga -- Brāhmana

**1(383). Chinda sotaṃ parakkamma, kāme panuda Brāhmaṇa;\
              Saṅkhārānaṃ khayaṃ ñatvā, akataññū'si Brāhmaṇa.**

Striving and cut the stream; discard sexual pleasures (**kāma**), O **Brāhmaṇa**; Knowing the end of the **saṅkhāras**, you are a knower of the Uncreated / Unmade (*akata, synonymous with **Asaṅkhata**, The Unconditioned, which is the foremost of the **34 characteristics of Nibbāna***), O **Brāhmaṇa**.

**2(384). Yadā dvayesu dhammesu, pāragū hoti brāhmaṇo;\
              Atha'ssa sabbe saṃyogā, atthaṃ gacchanti jānato.**

When the brāhmana becomes a master of the Other Shore (pāragū, similar to Vedagū, master of Veda, one of Gautama's titles) in regard to two dhammas (This Shore and Other Shore); Then, for him who knows, all fetters disappear.

**3(385). Yassa pāraṃ apāraṃ vā, pārāpāraṃ vā vijjati;\
Vītaddaraṃ visaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who knows (vijjati) the other shore (Pāraṃ), this shore (Apāraṃ), or both shores (Pārāpāraṃ), fearless (Vītaddaraṃ) and unbound (visaṃyuttaṃ) --- him I call a brāhmana.

**4(386). Jhāyiṃ virajam āsīnaṃ, katakiccaṃ anāsavaṃ;\
             Uttamatthaṃ anuppattaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

The one who practices Jhāna (**Jhāyi**), free from defilements (**viraja**), seated (**āsīna**), having done what was to be done (**katakicca**), without taints (**anāsava**); The one who has attained the highest goal (**Attha**)---him I call a **Brāhmaṇa**.

**5(387). Divā tapati ādicco, rattimābhāti candimā;\
              Sannaddho khattiyo tapati, jhāyī tapati brāhmaṇo;\
              Atha sabbam ahorattiṃ, buddho tapati tejasā.**

By day the sun shines, by night the moon shines; The warrior shines when armed, the brāhmana shines when practices Jhāna (**jhāyī**); But the Buddha shines with splendor all through the day and night. 

**6(388). Bāhitapāpo'ti brāhmaṇo, samacariyā samaṇo'ti vuccati;\
              Pabbājay'attano malaṃ, tasmā "pabbajito"ti vuccati.**

He is called a brāhmana because he has discarded evil, he is called a samana because of living (**cariya**) in evenness/equanimity (**sama**); Having renounced (pabbājaya) his defilements/impurities (mala) from his soul (attano), therefore he is called 'a renunciate (pabbajja).'  

Comment: Pabbajjā and Sama vs. Upekkha

1). Pabbajjā, in its original sense, is the renunciation of the world in both form and essence. Outwardly, it is the leaving of household life; inwardly, it is the expulsion of impurities (mala) from the attano, soul. The designation "pabbajita" is grounded in this purification: the renunciate is defined not merely by departure from society, but by the cleansing of the soul that the renunciation is meant to accomplish. In its highest sense, this purification converges with the state of the arahant, where the defilements are fully removed.

2). In the Vedas and early Upanishads, **Sama** is the root of \"Equanimity,\" but its mechanical meaning is **\"Symmetry,\" \"Levelness,\" or \"The Same.\"**

Sanskrit (Sama): the root is **√sam**, meaning \"even,\" \"same,\" \"level,\" or \"balanced.\" It describes a physical or energetic state of **non-deviation**.

**English (Equanimity):** This comes from the Latin ***aequanimitas***:

- **Aequus:** \"Even\" or \"Level\" (The exact equivalent of *Sama*).

- **Animus:** \"Mind\" or \"Spirit\" (The equivalent of *Citta* or *Manas*).

**The Connection:** \"Equanimity\" was literally invented to mean **\"Even-Mindedness.\"** Therefore, translators use it for **Sama** because both words describe the mechanical rejection of \"spikes\" (highs and lows).

**The Ancient Hindu/Vedic Logic of Sama**

In the Vedas and the *Bhagavad Gita*, **Sama** is not just a feeling; it is a **functional union**.

- **Samatvam (Evenness):** The Gita famously says, *\"Samatvaṃ yoga ucyate\"* (Evenness is called Yoga).

- It is the state where the **High-Self** remains \"the same\" (**Sama**) regardless of whether it is touched by heat or cold, praise or blame.

- **Unite/Union:** in the sense that **Sama** is the frequency where the individual stop clashing with the universal laws and instead \"level out\" into the background of the **Brahman**.

3). Sama vs. Upekkha

The distinction between **Sama** and **Upekkha**, is from two distinct mechanical stages of the **High-Self** engineering. One is a state of **internal calibration** (Sama), while the other is a state of **spatial-relational positioning** (Upekkha).

**(1). Sama: The Mechanical \"Leveling\" (Horizontal)**

**Sama** (Sanskrit/Pāli) is the frequency of **High-Neutrality**. Its root refers to \"evenness\" or \"equality.\" In the Vedic and Brahmic sense, it is the process of making the \"Mental Phenomena\" match the steady, unmoving background of the **High-Self**.

- **The Action:** It is the \"stilling\" of the spikes. Imagine your emotions and thoughts as a wave, **Sama** is the force that pulls the peaks down and the valleys up until the line is perfectly flat.

- **The Goal:** To remove **friction**. As long as there is a difference in \"height\" between your desire and reality, there is friction (**Dukkha**). By making everything \"the same\" (**Sama**), the friction disappears.

- **Layer focus:** It operates primarily on the **Emotion** and **Mental** layers. It is the practice of **Samacariyā** (living in evenness).

**(2). Upekkha: The Mechanical \"Transcendence\" (Vertical)**

**Upekkha** (Upa + Ikkha) is the frequency of **Super-Vision**. It is not a leveling of the field, but a **departure** from the field.

- **The Action:** **Upa-** (Upper) + **Ikkha** (to look). It is \"looking down from above.\" While **Sama** makes the waves flat, **Upekkha** is the act of the **Citta** stepping onto a mountain to watch the waves (flat or not) from a distance.

- **The Goal:** **Sovereign Independence**. It is the \"Kha\" (release). In this state, the **High-Self** realizes it is the observer on the cliff.

**7(389). Na Brāhmaṇassa pahareyya, n'āssa muñcetha brāhmaṇo;\
              Dhī Brāhmaṇassa hantāraṃ, tato dhī yassa muñcati.**

One should not strike a brāhmana, nor should a brāhmana vent (his wrath) on one who has struck him. Shame on him who strikes a brāhmana! More shame on him who gives vent (to his wrath)!

**8(390). Na Brāhmaṇass'etadakiñci seyyo, yadā nisedho manaso piyehi;\
    Yato yato hiṃsamano nivattatim, tato tato sammatimeva dukkhaṃ.**

Nothing is better for a brāhmana than restraint of mind from pleasant things; wherever the harmful mind (a mind filled with negative emotions like craving, aversion, and delusion) turns away, there suffering (dukkha) is calmed. 

**9(391). Yassa kāyena vācāya, manasā natthi dukkataṃ;\
              Saṃvutaṃ tīhi ṭhānehi, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who does no bad thing through body, speech or mind, who is restrained in these three ways --- him I call a brāhmana.

**10(392). Yamhā dhammaṃ vijāneyya, sammāsambuddha desitaṃ;\
                Sakkaccaṃ taṃ namasseyya, aggihuttaṃ 'va brāhmaṇo.**

From whomsoever one may learn the Dhamma as taught by the Rightly and Fully Enlightened One; One should pay homage to them with respect, as a Brāhmaṇa honors the sacrificial fire.

**11(393). Na jaṭāhi na gottena, na jaccā hoti brāhmaṇo;\
                Yamhi saccañca dhammo ca, so sucī so ca brāhmaṇo.**

Not by matted hair, nor by lineage, nor by birth does one become a Brāhmaṇa; In whom there is Truth (Sacca) and Dhamma, he is pure, he is a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **Beyond Hair, Birth, and Lineage: The True Brāhmaṇa Defined by Sacca and Dhamma**

This verse directly overturns identification based on **external markers and inherited status**. Matted hair (jaṭā), lineage (gotta), and birth (jaccā) are all rejected as defining criteria. What is affirmed instead is **sacca and dhamma**---truth and dhamma---as the basis of purity and the designation "brāhmaṇa."

In Gautama the Supreme Arahant's time, groups treated outward signs---especially matted hair---as indicators of renunciation and holiness. The symbolism was clear: rejection of social grooming and conventional identity. However, this remained at the level of **form**.

Gautama the Supreme Arahant's training reconfigures this entirely. The **shaved head (muṇḍa)** in the bhikkhu and bhikkhunī discipline as a **functional act of pabbajjā**. It removes visible ties to family, lineage, and bodily distinction. It also eliminates maintenance and vanity, reducing engagement with mental proliferation around appearance. The purpose is mechanical: to support **withdrawal from worldly identification**.

Thus, the verse establishes a critical distinction:

- **External renunciation (jaṭā, appearance, lineage)** does not define realization.

- **Internal alignment with sacca and dhamma** defines purity.

It's also important to note that both "brāhmaṇa" and "samaṇa" are ethnic groups.As Gautama the Supreme Arahant himself always identify himself as Brahmana. At the same time, he did not deny when people also identify him as Samana because his Sakya lineage is hybrid.

Both "brāhmaṇa" and "samaṇa" are remembered as descendents of divine origin. As stated here:

> In this **Devā**'s** **world (loka), there is the Death (**Māra**)**, **and the **Brahma**; there are **samānas** and **brāhmanas**, descendants **(pajā)** of **deva** and **manussa** (the race of man). "Sadevake loke, samārake sabrahmake, sassamaṇa-brāhmaṇiyā pajāya sadeva-manussāya."
>
> (SN 56:11 Dhamma Cakka Pavattana Sutta)

Gautama the Supreme Arahant's dhamma transcend both:

- **Brāhmaṇa** becomes a designation of **purity through truth and dhamma**, not just birth.

- **Samaṇa** becomes a designation of **evenness/equinimity (sama) in conduct**, not just physical marks.

- **Bhikkhu** is not an ethnic or lineage category, but a **functional role within the training of renunciation from the world**, defined by precepts (sīla).

So the verse is **ontological and structural**: It removes the basis of identity from body, lineage, and social classification, and relocates it entirely in the **state of the citta aligned with truth (sacca) and dhamma**.

**12(394). Kiṃ te jaṭāhi dummedha, kiṃ te ajinasāṭiyā;\
                Abbhantaraṃ te gahaṇaṃ, bāhiraṃ parimajjasi.**

What use is your matted hair, O fool? What use is your deer skin garment? Your inside is a tangled jungle, yet you polish the outside. 

**13(395). Paṃsukūladharaṃ jantuṃ, kisaṃ dhamanisanthataṃ;\
                Ekaṃ vanasmiṃ jhāyantaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who wears just rags from a dust heap, is lean, with veins showing, who practise Jhana (**jhāyati**) alone in the forest --- him I call a brāhmana.

**jhāyati** (Pāli) and its Sanskrit equivalent **dhyāyati** (root **√dhyai**)

In Sanskrit, **dhyāyati** is the verb \"to meditate,\" and **jhāyi** (or **dhyāyī**) is the agent noun, meaning \"the meditator\" or \"one who is in the state of dhyāna.\"

- **Vedic Origin (Dhi):** In the *Rigveda*, the word **Dhī** refers to \"visionary\" or \"inspired thought.\" It wasn\'t just thinking; it was the \"Mental Phenomena\" being struck by a flash of truth.

- **Classical Sanskrit (Dhyāna):** By the time of the *Upaniṣads* and the *Bhagavad Gītā*, **√dhyai** evolved into a technical term for the continuous flow of the mind toward a single object.

There is a fascinating linguistic overlap in the Pāli and Prakrit traditions where **jhāyati** (to meditate) and **jhāyati** (to burn, from Sanskrit **kṣāyati**) became homonyms.

- **Dhyāna as Fire:** In the Ancient Path, meditation is not just \"thinking\"; it is a \"burning.\"

- **The Agni Connection:** Just as the **Brāhmaṇa** tends the sacrificial fire (**aggihuttaṃ**), the **bhikkhu** tends the internal fire of **jhāyati**.

- **The Function:** This \"meditative burning\" consumes the **rāga, dosa, and moha**. It is the mechanical process of refining the \"oil\" of the mind into the \"light\" of the **Citta**.

**Dhyāna in the Bhagavad Gītā**

In the *Bhagavad Gītā* (6.19), the state of the **dhyāyī** (jhāyi) is described with mechanical precision:

*\"Yathā dīpo nivāta-stho neṅgate sopamā smṛtā\...\"* (**As a lamp in a windless place does not flicker, that is the simile for the yogi of subdued mind\...**)

- **Steady Frequency:** This is the state of **Sama** (Evenness). The **jhāyi** has stilled the \"wind\" of the world so that the **Citta** remains a solid, unmoving beam of light.

- **Sustained Observation:** Unlike regular thought (which jumps), **dhyāna** is compared to the \"unbroken flow of oil\" (**taila-dhārāvat**).

The relationship between **Jhāna** (Meditation), **Tapas** (Heat), and **Tejas** (Radiance) is a progression of **Internal Thermodynamcis**. In ancient Hindu scriptures (like the *Rigveda* and *Maitri Upaniṣad*), **Tapas** involves heat generated through practice.

**Jhāyati** (to meditate) and **Jhāyati** (to burn) are homonyms in the Prakrit dialects used by Gautama the Supreme Arahant. Tibetan **\"Light-Body\"** practice also involves hear and burning. **Tejas**---the spiritual \"glint\" or sharp energy of the **Arahant** in Buddhism or Saints in other traditions. They are often described as \"radiant like the sun.\"

**14(396). Na c'āhaṃ Brāhmaṇaṃ brūmi, yonijaṃ mattisambhavaṃ;\
               Bhovādi nāma so hoti, sa ce hoti sakiñcano;\
               Akiñcanaṃ anādānaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

I do not call him a brāhmana merely because he is born of such a womb, born from such a mother. If he has attachments, he is to be called with a title only. He who has no attachments (akiñcanaṃ), is free from clinging (anādānaṃ) --- him I call a brāhmana.

**15(397). Sabbasaṃyojanaṃ chetvā, yo ve na paritassati;\
               Saṅgātigaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Having cut every fetter/ bond, he who truly does not tremble; Who has overstepped (**atiga**) the group (Saṅga, meaning attachment or dependent) and is detached (visaṃyutta, unyoked, independent)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**16(398). Chetvā naddhiṃ varattañca, sandānaṃ sahanukkamaṃ;\
                Ukkhittapaḷighaṃ buddhaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Having cut the strap (naddhiṃ) and the rope (varattañca), the cord (sandānaṃ) together with its appendages; Having lifted the obstacles (ukkhittapaḷighaṃ), the buddha (awakened)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **Dismantling the Harness of Reincarnation**

This verse uses the imagery of a cart or a draught animal being released from its equipment.

**The Straps and Ropes** **(Naddhiṃ/Varatta):** These represent the flexible but strong bonds of **rāga** and **dosa**. They are the \"reins\" by which the world pulls the **Citta** left and right.

**The Cord and Appendages (Sandānaṃ sahanukkamaṃ):** This refers to the complex \"wiring\" of wrong views and the latent tendencies (**anusaya**) that remain hidden in the \"Lower-Self.\" To cut these is to ensure the machine cannot be \"re-started\" by external stimuli.

**Ukkhittapaḷighaṃ (The Lifted Cross-bar/** **obstacles):** A *paḷigha* is the heavy iron or wooden bar used to lock a city gate.

- **Mechanical Meaning:** This is the bar of **Mohas** (ignorance). As long as this bar is down, the **Citta** is imprisoned within the walls of the universe. Lifting it is the act of **Paññā** (Wisdom) opening the exit.

**The Awakened One as the Sovereign Mechanic:** Buddha here not as a title, but as a description of a state: The **Brāhmaṇa** is \"Awakened\" because he has realized the path of liberation from the world.

**17(399). Akkosaṃ vadhabandhañca, aduṭṭho yo titikkhati;\
                Khantibalaṃ balānīkaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who, without anger, endures abuse, violence and imprisonment; He whose internal army is power/strength (Balānīka), whose power/strength is patience/endurance (Khantibala) --- him I call a brāhmana. 

Comment: **on Khantī (Patience as Supreme Tapas)**

"Akkosaṃ vadhabandhañca... khantibalaṃ balānīkaṃ... tam ahaṃ brūmi brāhmaṇaṃ." (Dhp 399)\
"Khantī paramaṃ tapo titikkhā." (Dhp 184)

Khantī is a **stabilized power of the citta** that does not generate dosa under pressure. In Dhp 399, the true brāhmaṇa is defined by this capacity: even under abuse, violence, and constraint, the citta remains **aduṭṭha (unangered)**. This is not suppression, but **non-arising of hostility**.

For this reason, khantī is called the **highest tapas** (Dhp 184). Tapas is equivalent to samadhi training, the only training in ancient time to go beyond human limit, to become divine, and union with a higher reality. Tapas burns away defilements.

Thus, **khantibala**---the strength of endurance---is called an **internal army (balānīka)**. It protects the citta. It is a **defensive power** that prevents the formation of new kamma rooted in anger, and allows Paññā to operate without distortion.

Within the path structure:

- **Sīla** restrains outward reaction

- **Samādhi** stabilizes the citta under impact

- **Paññā** sees the worldly contact as conditioned nāma-rūpa

→ This convergence produces **khantī**: patience /endurance without aversion.

Therefore: Khantī is the point where worldly contact does not become conflict. It helps burning of dosa, and thus the highest tapas.

**18(400). Akkodhanaṃ vatavantaṃ, sīlavantaṃ anussadaṃ**;**\
                Dantaṃ antimasārīraṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who is free from anger (akkodhana), vow keeper (vatavanta, code or rules for *Pabbajjā/renunciant*  duty, vow/observance), sīla keeper (sīlavanta, code or rules for virtue/ethical conduct), and free from the swellings of Rāga, Dosa and Moha (anussada); He who is tamed (danta) and wears his final body (antimasārīra)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **ussada and anussada**

**ussada** (Sanskrit: *utsada*) literally means \"swelling,\" \"elevation,\" or \"protruberance.\"

**(1). The Three Swellings of the Mind**

Ancient commentaries and the internal logic of the Suttas identify three primary \"swellings\" that create an uneven frequency in the **Citta**:

- **Rāga-ussada (The Swelling of Lust/Greed):** The mind \"bulges\" toward an object it wants to consume.

- **Dosa-ussada (The Swelling of Hatred):** The mind \"inflates\" with heat and pressure to fight or flight.

- **Moha-ussada (The Swelling of Pride/Delusion):** This is the most common meaning of *ussada*. It is the \"puffing up\" of the ego.

**\**

**(2). Anussadaṃ: The State of \"Flatness\" or \"Evenness\"**

To be **anussadaṃ** means your internal landscape is perfectly level.

- **Mechanical Meaning:** There are no \"peaks\" of pride and no \"valleys\" of self-loathing.

- **The Result:** Because there are no swellings, there is no **friction**. The world can \"rub\" against the **Citta**, but because the surface is perfectly smooth (**Sama**), nothing catches.

- The **Brāhmaṇa** has \"leveled\" his mind. He no longer projects his identity onto the world. He is \"flat\" because he has discarded the \"Lower-Self\'s\" need to be \"somebody.\"

**(3). The Relationship to \"Final Body\"**

The verse connects being **anussadaṃ** (without swellings) directly to **antimasārīraṃ** (the final body).

- **The Logic:** Reincarnation is caused by the three \"swellings\". These swellings act like \"hooks\" that catch onto new wombs.

- If the **Citta** is perfectly smooth and has no \"projections,\" there is nothing for the laws of the universe to grab onto. The \"Final Body\" remains the final one because the \"Grasping-Swellings\" have been surgically removed.

**19(401). Vāripokkharapatt'eva, āragger'iva sāsapo;\
                Yo na limpati kāmesu, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Like water on a lotus leaf, like a mustard seed on the point of a needle; He who does not cling to sex pleasures---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**20(402). Yo dukkhassa pajānāti, idh'eva khayam attano;\
                Pannabhāraṃ visaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who, in this very life, understands with wisdom (**Paññā**) the destruction (**Khaya**) of dukkha for his soul (**attano**); Who has laid down the burden (pannabhāraṃ) and is unyoked/ detached (**visaṃyutta**) \[with the world\]---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **The Importance of Unloading**

This verse defines liberation in **real-time terms**: the destruction (khaya) of dukkha is realized **here and now** through Paññā, and its visible mark is **unloading**---the laying down of the burden (pannabhāra).

**Bhāra (burden)** is the operational weight of identity with the worldly. It includes:

- the five aggregates as a **carried system**, and

- the ongoing **responsibility of maintaining 'I' and 'mine'** in the loka.

The ordinary stance is: "I must protect my body, my name, my future."

This stance binds the citta as a **bearer**. It generates continuous load:

- guarding, managing, defending, projecting

- all rooted in **mamāyitaṃ** toward the aggregates

**Pannabhāra --- The Laid-Down Burden**

"Laying down of the worldly" is a **cognitive--ontological release** produced by Paññā. With that seeing, the citta **ceases to assume the role of bearer**. This is the off-switch of dukkha:

when the citta stops carrying, the load has nowhere to rest.

**Visaṃyutta --- The Unyoked State**

With the burden dropped, the worldly **yoke (yutta)** is broken.

- The citta is no longer **harnessed** to the machinery of saṃsāra

- Worldly contact still occurs, but there is **no binding** to them

- Function remains; **ownership is absent**

**Engineering the Weightless Citta**

Through **Sīla--Samādhi--Paññā**:

- **Sīla** stops gross carrying (reactive maintenance of worldly existence)

- **Samādhi** lifts the citta beyond sensory compulsion

- **Paññā** sees the path of purification of the citta toward liberation

→ leading to:

- **Nibbidā** (disenchantment from worldly carrying)

- **Virāga** (fading of worldly ownership and craving)

- **Vimutti** (release from the worldly role of bearer)

The result is a **weightless citta**:

- present in the world

- not carrying the world, weightless

Unloading (pannabhāra) is the decisive act where the citta relinquishes the role of bearer. With the yoke broken (visaṃyutta), dukkha ends at its root---not by changing the world, but by no longer carrying the worldly within.

**21(403). Gambhīra-paññaṃ medhāviṃ, maggāmaggassa kovidaṃ;\
                Uttamatthaṃ anuppattaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He of deep (**gambhīra**) wisdom (**pañña**), the sage (medhāvi), skilled (kovida) in the right path (magga) and wrong path (amagga); Who has attained (anuppatta) the highest goal (uttamattha)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**22(404). Asaṃsaṭṭhaṃ gahaṭṭhehi, anāgārehi c'ūbhayaṃ;\
                Anokasāriṃ appicchaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who does not mix with both householders (laypeople) and the homeless (the wanderers/monks); Who wanders without a fixed home (**anokasārin**) and has few wants (**appiccha**)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**23(405). Nidhāya daṇḍaṃ bhūtesu, tasesu thāvaresu ca;\
                Yo na hanti na ghāteti, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Having laid aside violence towards all beings, whether feeble or strong; He who neither kills nor causes others to kill---him I call a brāhmana. 

**24(406). Aviruddhaṃ viruddhesu, attadaṇḍesu nibbutaṃ;\
                Sādānesu anādānaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Friendly among the hostile, peaceful among the violent; Unattached among the attached---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**25(407). Yassa rāgo ca doso ca, māno makkho ca pātito;\
                Sāsapor'iva āraggā, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He from whom rāga and dosa, conceit/pride (māno) and hypocrisy (makkho) have fallen away; Like a mustard seed from the point of a needle---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**26(408). Akakkasaṃ viññāpaniṃ, giraṃ saccaṃ udīraye;\
                Yāya n'ābhisaje kañci, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He should speak words that are gentle (akakkasa), instructive (viññāpani), and truth (sacca); Words by which he offends no one---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**27(409). Yo'dha dīghaṃ va rassaṃ vā, aṇuṃ thūlaṃ subhāsubhaṃim;\
                Loke adinnaṃ nādiyati, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who in this world takes nothing that is not given, be it long or short, small or great, pleasant or unpleasant --- him I call a brāhmana.

**28(410). Āsā yassa na vijjanti, asmiṃ loke paramhi ca;\
                Nirāsāmsaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who has no hopes in this world or the next, free from longing and all fetters --- him I call a brāhmana.

He in whom there are no longings, either for this world (worldly life) or the next; Who is free from longing and unyoked/ detached (**visaṃyutta**) ---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**\**

**29(411). Yassālayā na vijjanti, aññāya akathaṃkathī;\
                Amatogadhaṃ anuppattaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He in whom there are no attachments (ālayā), who through direct knowledge (aññāya) is free from doubt; Who has reached the plunge into the Deathless (Amatogadha)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **Ālayā (No Attachments/Abodes)**

*Ālaya* literally means a house or a place of settling. The **Brāhmaṇa** has no \"mental roost.\" Most minds are always looking for a place to \"land\" (in a thought, a feeling, or an identity). To have no *ālayā* means the **Citta** is always \"in flight,\" never settling into a worldly manifestation.

**Aññāya Akathaṃkathī (Free from Doubt through Gnosis):** \"How-how-ing\" (*kathaṃ-kathā*) is the friction of uncertainty.

- Doubt is a \"wobble\" in the system. By knowing (**aññāya**) the mechanics of the **path of liberation from the world** directly, the wobble is eliminated. You no longer ask \"How do I escape?\" or \"Is this it?\" because you have seen the \"Code\" of the universe directly.

**Amatogadhaṃ (The Plunge into the Deathless):** *Amata* (Deathless) is the realm beyond the laws of cause and effect. *Ogadha* implies a \"plunge\" or \"stepping into.\"

- **The Result:** The **bhikkhu** has moved beyond the \"Law of Death\" that governs the world.

The **Brāhmaṇa** has reached the **complete cessation of worldly craving** by seeing the end of the line.

- As long as there is doubt, the mind keeps \"re-calculating\" and searching for security in the **Loka**.

- The **Citta** disenchants from the worldly and looks toward the only thing that doesn\'t \"rise and fall\": the **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

**\**

**30(412). Yo'dha puññañca pāpañca, ubho saṅgaṃ upaccagā;\
                Asokaṃ virajaṃ suddhaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who, in this world, has transcended the clingings of both merit (puñña) and demerit (pāpa); Who is sorrowless (asoka), stainless (viraja), and pure (suddha)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: To exit the **Loka**, the **Citta** must stop being \"weighted\" by either the \"dark\" energy of evil or the \"golden\" energy of worldly merit.

1.  **Upaccagā (Transcending Both):** Most beings try to trade **pāpa** (bad kamma) for **puñña** (merit).

    - While merit leads to better rebirths, it is still a \"bondage.\" To be a **Brāhmaṇa**, you must break the chain entirely---whether it is made of iron or gold. You move from \"Better Living\" to \"Complete Exit.\"

2.  **Asoka (Sorrowless):** Sorrow is the friction caused when the world changes and the mind tries to hold on. The **Brāhmaṇa** is sorrowless because he has no \"hooks\" in the world.

3.  **Viraja (Stainless):** *Raja* (dust/stain) refers to the subtle residues of **rāga, dosa, and moha**. To be **viraja** means the \"Surface Tension\" of the **Citta** is so high that the dust of the world cannot settle on it.

4.  **Suddha (Pure):** This is the state of the **Citta** when it is no longer \"mingled\" with any worldly manifestations.

The **Brāhmaṇa** has reached a state beyond \"Good and Evil\" as defined by the **Loka**.

- The practitioner recognizes that even \"merit\" is worldly; it belongs to the world. It produces results within the cycle of reincarnation. To gain true freedom, the **Citta** will disenchant even the highest merits of the world, leave them behind, and transcend.

By being **viraja**, you ensure that your **Mental Phenomena** are not \"clouded\" by the lingering dust of your past actions.

**31(413). Candaṃ 'va vimalaṃ suddhaṃ, vippasannam anāvilaṃ;\
                Nandībhavaparikkhīṇaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Like the moon, spotless (vimala), pure (suddha), Supreme Clarity of Sight (vippasanna), and free from turbidity (not muddy) (anāvila); He who has thoroughly exhausted (parikkhīṇa) the delight in worldly existence (nandī-bhava)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**32(414). Yomaṃ palipathaṃ duggaṃ, saṃsāraṃ moham accagā;\
                Tiṇṇo pāragato jhāyī, anejo akathaṃkathī;\
                Anupādāya nibbuto, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who has transcended this swampy path (palipatha), this difficult path (dugga), the cyclic existrence of birth and death (saṃsāra), and the darkness of moha; Who has crossed over (**Tiṇṇo**) to lead to the other shore (pāragata), practices Jhāna (jhāyī), Free from Trembling (anejo, Stillness), free from \"how and why\" (doubt) (a-kathaṃ-kathī); Who through non-clinging / Without Fuel(**Upādāna**) (anupādāya) has quenched (nibbuto, nibbāti, cooled, extinguished) \[worldly craving and attachment\] ---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**33(415). Yo'dha kāme pahamtvāna, anāgāro paribbaje;\
                Kāma-bhava-parikkhīṇaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who, in this world, having given up sex pleasure (kāme), total wandering (**paribbaje**) as a homeless / without a house (anāgāro); Who has destructed both sex-desire and worldly existence (kāma-bhava-parikkhīṇa)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **Kāma and Bhava**

**(1.1). The Vedic Origin of Kāma as the Procreative Impulse**

In the most ancient Vedic and early Sanskrit layers, **Kāma** was primarily the \"Primordial Seed\" of desire---specifically the drive that ensures the continuation of the species.

- **The First Stirring:** In the *Nasadiya Sukta* (the Rig Vedic Hymn of Creation), **Kāma** is described as the first thing to arise in the \"One\" to bring about the universe. It was the **desire to be**, manifesting through sexual polarity.

- Anciently, **Kāma** was the name of the deity of love (Kāma-deva), and its function was nearly synonymous with **Maithuna** (sexual union). The focus was on the \"Heat\" (**Tapas**) required to create life.

**(1.2). The 3-Realm Cosmology: The \"Gender Wall\"**

The world is partitioned by \"Sensory Density.\" The presence or absence of gender is the primary filter between the lower and higher worlds.

**The Kāma-Dhātu (The Sex Realm)**

This is the \"Dual-Polarity\" zone.

- **Key Feature:** The existence of **Male and Female**.

- **The Mechanics:** In this realm, consciousness is \"Heavy.\" Reproduction occurs through the union of opposites. The **Citta** is trapped in a loop of seeking union with another body. As long as the mind identifies as \"Man\" or \"Woman,\" it is anchored to the gravity of this realm.

**The Rūpa-Dhātu (The Fine-Form Realm)**

This is the \"Asexual\" zone.

- **Key Feature:** **No Sex. No Male. No Female.**

- **The Mechanics:** These beings are \"Luminous\" and gender-neutral. They are not born from a womb; they manifest spontaneously. The \"Heat\" of sexual desire has been converted into the Light.

**(1.3). Kāma Sūtra and Tantra: Harnessing sexual energy for Ascension**

In Hindu and Vajrayana traditions, **Kāma** is not just suppressed; it is repurposed. If sexual energy is the strongest force in the **Kāma-Dhātu**, can it be used as the \"Escape Velocity\" to leave it?

**The Kāma Sūtra: Refining the Impulse**

The *Kāma Sūtra* is the first stage of \"Sensory Management.\" It teaches the householder to move from animalistic release to refined, aesthetic enjoyment.

- **The Goal:** To smooth the \"Friction\" of desire so it becomes a tool for relational harmony and psychological stability.

**Tantra: The Internal Combustion**

Tantra (and its related yoga systems) is the refined form for this energy.

- **Harnessing the Energy:** Instead of allowing the energy to flow \"outward\" and \"downward\" (for procreation/pleasure), Tantra uses techniques to turn the energy \"inward\" and \"upward.\"

- **Internal Union:** The practitioner seeks to unite the \"Male\" and \"Female\" principles *within* their own **Citta**. This internal union \"short-circuits\" the need for an external partner.

- **Ascension:** When this internal polarity is resolved, the **Citta** achieves the frequency of the **Rūpa-Dhātu**. You use the \"Power of the Sex Realm\" to blow the roof off the Sex Realm.

**(2.1). The Vedic and Sanskrit Root of Bhava: √bhū (The Becoming)**

The root is **√bhū**, which in Sanskrit and Vedic means **\"to be,\" \"to become,\" \"to arise,\" or \"to manifest.\"**

- **The Process:** In the Vedas, **√bhū** is not a static state. It is the transition from potentiality to actuality. It is the act of a seed **becoming** a sprout.

- **Bhavati:** \"It becomes\" or \"It happens.\"

- **Bhāva:** The state of becoming.

**(2.2). Bhāvanā: The Development**

**Bhāvanā** (Development) comes from the causative form of **√bhū**.

- It literally means \"causing to become\" or \"bringing into being.\"

- **In Practice:** When you develop **Concentration (Samadhi)**, you are performing **Bhāvanā**---you are cultivating, you are taking a mental potential and \"manifesting\" it into a stable reality within the physical and mental world.

**(2.3). Bhava as \"Worldly Manifestation\"**

**Bhava** shall be translated as **\"Worldly Existence\"** is superior to the generic \"existence.\" Here is why:

- **Loka (The World):** The environment where things arise.

- **Bhava (The Arising):** The actual \"taking shape\" within that environment.

- If **Bhava** were just \"existence,\" it would be an abstract concept. But **Bhava** is the **Worldly Process**. It is the \"becoming\" that happens because of the laws of cause and effect.

- **The Logic:** If something arises (**bhava**), the laws of this universe are embedded into this new being, it belongs to the world (**loka**). If it belongs to the world, it is subject to death and decay (**dukkha**).

**(2.4). The Engineering of \"Exhausting Bhava\"**

In verse 415, the term **Kāma-bhava-parikkhīṇa** (Exhaustion/depletion/destruction of Sex and Bhava) describes the end of this \"Arising.\"

- Without knowledge of the liberation path, beings constantly seek to \"Become\" (**Bhava**) something in this world---a better person, a more successful author, a higher being in a heaven.

- With knowledge of the liberation path, beings recognize that all \"becoming\" is worldly. It is a bound to the universal laws of power, decay, and death.

- **The Result:** The **Citta** disenchants the process of arising. It stops \"developing\" (**bhāvanā**) worldly identities. When you stop the \"becoming,\" the fuel for **Bhava** is **parikkhīṇa** (exhausted).

**34(416). Yo'dha taṇhaṃ pahantvāna, anāgāro paribbaje;\
                Taṇhābhavaparikkhīṇaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who, in this world, having abandoned craving (taṇha), total wandering (**paribbaje**) as a homeless one (anāgāro); Who has exhausted both craving and worldly existence (taṇhā-bhava-parikkhīṇa)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **Taṇhā, Rāga, and Chanda**

The distinction between **Taṇhā** and **Kāma** is the difference between the **Internal Force** and the **External Objective**.

**(1.1). Etymology of Taṇhā: The \"Thirst\"**

**Taṇhā** (Pāli) or **Tṛṣṇā** (Sanskrit) comes from the root **√tṛṣ**, meaning \"to be thirsty\" or \"to be parched.\"

- **Origin:** Both roots for **Taṇhā** and **Kāma** exist in the earliest layers of the *Rig Veda*. However, in the Vedic context, *Tṛṣṇā* was often a literal, physical thirst or a poetic description of the earth \"thirsting\" for rain.

**(1.2). The Mechanical Difference**

If we look at the system of a \"desiring being,\" the roles are distinct:

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Feature**   **Kāma (Sensory Desire)**                                                                    **Taṇhā (Craving/Thirst)**
  ------------- -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Domain**    **Objective/Thematic.** It refers to the *content* (mainly Sex, expanded to food, beauty).   **Subjective/Dynamic.** It refers to the *drive* or the \"pulling\" action.

  **Scope**     Limited to the **Kāma-Dhātu** (Sense-Desire Realm).                                          Universal. Worldly. It exists in the **Rūpa** and **Arūpa** realms (as thirst for existence).

  **Analogy**   The \"Salt\" or the \"Flavor\" of the world.                                                 The \"Thirst\" that makes you keep eating the salt.
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

You can remove **Kāma** (the interest in sex/food) and still have **Taṇhā** (the thirst to *be* a \"holy person\" or to *exist* forever in a high heaven). **Taṇhā** is the deeper, more fundamental \"Suction\" of the **Citta**.

**Taṇhā** is a worldly \"suction\" that requires an object to consume. **Taṇhā** operates on the principle of \"Arising\" (**Bhava**). It seeks to pull something into the \"Self\" to satisfy a perceived lack.

**(1.3). The Structural Relationship**

**Taṇhā** is the fuel-pump; **Kāma** is one specific type of fuel.

- **Kāma-taṇhā:** Thirst for sensory pleasure (the lowest frequency).

- **Bhava-taṇhā:** Thirst for worldly arising/becoming (the \"high-frequency\" thirst for existence).

- **Vibhava-taṇhā:** Thirst for non-existence / annihilation (the \"negative\" thirst to be destroyed).

By understanding this, you see why the **Brāhmaṇa** must be **Taṇhā-bhava-parikkhīṇa** (Exhausted of Thirst and Arising). If you only exhaust **Kāma**, you might still \"arise\" in the **Rūpa-Dhātu** because the \"thirst for being\" (**Bhava-taṇhā**) is still active.

Comment: **Rāga**

The distinction between **Rāga** and **Taṇhā** is the difference between **Chemical Affinity** and **Suction Pressure**. While they often operate together, they represent different \"fault lines\" in the mind\'s engineering.

**(2.1). Etymology of Rāga: The \"Dye\"**

**Rāga** (Pāli and Sanskrit) comes from the root **√rañj**, meaning \"to color,\" \"to dye,\" or \"to redden.\"

- **Vedic/Ancient Context:** Anciently, it referred to the process of staining a cloth. If you dip a white cloth into a red dye, the cloth is \"colored\" (**rakta**).

- **Rāga** is the \"Stain\" or \"Tint\" of the **Citta**. It is the \"Lust\" or \"Passion\" that colors your perception. When **Rāga** is present, you don\'t see the world as it is; you see it through the \"red tint\" of your attraction.

**(2.2). The Mechanical Comparison: Rāga vs. Taṇhā**

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Feature**              **Rāga (Passion/Dye)**                                                       **Taṇhā (Craving/Thirst)**
  ------------------------ ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Mechanical Analogy**   **Chemical Affinity / Glue.** The way two surfaces stick together.           **Vacuum / Suction.** The force that pulls objects toward you.

  **Action**               It **\"colors\"** the object, making it appear \"attractive\" or \"mine.\"   It **\"pulls\"** the object, trying to consume it or absorb it.

  **The Result**           **Saṃyoga** (Bonding). The mind is \"stained\" by the object.                **Bhava** (Arising). The thirst creates the pressure for a new existence.
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

If **Taṇhā** is the vacuum pump that brings the \"fuel\" into the engine, **Rāga** is the \"stickiness\" of the fuel that makes it adhere to the cylinders. You can have a strong vacuum (**Taṇhā**) but if the surfaces are \"Teflon-coated\" (No **Rāga**), nothing sticks.

**(2.3). Structural Analysis: The \"Red-Tinted\" World**

**Rāga** is one of the three primary \"corruptions\" alongside **Dosa** (fear and anger) and **Moha** (ignorance and delusion).

- **Taṇhā is a Single Direction:** It is always a \"Pull.\"

- **Rāga is a Quality:** It is the specific *flavor* of the pull. You can have **Taṇhā** (thirst) for a drink, but **Rāga** is the \"passion\" for the specific vintage or the \"delight\" in the sensory experience.

It is through the complete eradication of **rāga**, **dosa**, and **moha** that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation and gain true freedom. While **Taṇhā** provides the momentum, **Rāga** provides the \"identification\" that makes the **Citta** believe it *is* the thing it desires.

**\**

**(2.4). Why Taṇhā isn\'t a \"Poison\" (Root)**

In Pāli physics, **Taṇhā** (Thirst/Craving) is categorized differently. It is not a \"Root\" but a **Drive**.

- **Taṇhā is the \"Fuel Pump\":** It is the active force that connects the roots to the world.

You don\'t call the \"suction\" of a vacuum a \"poison\"; you call it the **mechanical force**. The \"Poison\" is the **toxic residue** (Rāga/Dosa) that the suction pulls into the system.

**Rāga** is the \"adhesive\" that binds the **Citta** to the **Loka**.

- **The Physics:** **Rāga** is a \"stain\" or \"tint.\" It requires a \"surface\" (an aggregate or a concept) to stick to.

- To reach the **Deathless**, the mind must be **Vītarāgo** (stainless/colorless). **Rāga** is the very \"heat\" that **Nibbāna** (The Cooling) extinguishes. You cannot use the \"fire\" of passion to reach the \"quench\" of the cooling.

Comment: **Chanda**: Intent / Will

**Chanda** is the \"Mechanical Work\" or the \"Wholesome Volition\" required to move the system.

- **The Physics:** Unlike **Taṇhā** (which is a blind hunger), **Chanda** is an **Ethical Intent**. It is the \"interest\" or \"will to act\" that is ethically neutral until it is directed.

- You use **Dhamma-chanda** (Desire for the Dhamma) as the fuel for your **Brahmacariya**. It is the intention to practice **Sīla, Samadhi, and Paññā**.

- **The Purpose:** **Chanda** is the \"Rocket Engine\" that provides the thrust. Without the \"desire to attain the unattained,\" the **bhikkhu** would never sit down to meditate or analyze the Pāli canon. You use **Chanda** to cross the flood and reach the \"Other Shore\" (**Pāragato**).

**35(417). Hitvā mānusakaṃ yogaṃ, dibbaṃ yogaṃ upaccagā;\
               Sabbayogavisaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Having abandoned the human yoke /bond (mānusaka yoga), he has transcended (**upaccagā, Supreme Relinquishment**) the celestial yoke /bond (dibba yoga); Him, detached from all yokes /bonds (sabba-yoga-visaṃyutta)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**36(418). Hitvā ratiñca aratiñca, sītibhūtaṃ nirūpadhiṃ;\
                Sabbalokābhibhuṃ vīraṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

Having abandoned both household delight (rati) and aversion (arati), become cooled (sītibhūta) and without fuel (nirūpadhi); The hero (vīra) who has conquered all worlds (sabba-loka-abhibhu)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

Comment: **Rati and Sītibhūta**

**Rati** comes from the root **√ram**, meaning **\"to delight in,\" \"to enjoy,\" or \"to rest in.\"** In the *Vedas*, it refers to sexual pleasure or the delight found in a home and a partner. It is the name of the goddess of passion and the wife of **Kāmadeva** (the god of sensory desire). **Rati** is the feeling of \"settling in\" to a worldly object. It is the moment the **Citta** says, \"I like this; I want to stay here.\" It is the **delight of worldly dwelling**.

**Sītibhūta: The Physics of Cooling**

The term is a compound of **Sīti** and **Bhūta**. **Sīti (or Sīta):** From the root **√śyā** (Sanskrit: *śīta*), meaning **\"cold,\" \"cool,\" or \"frozen.\" In the Pāli Canon:** It is the direct opposite of **Aggī** (fire) and **Tapa** (heat/burning). While the world is \"burning\" with the fires of **rāga**, **dosa**, and **moha**, the state of liberation is always described as \"cool.\"

**Bhūta:** From the root **√bhū**, meaning **\"become,\" \"manifested,\" or \"state of being.\"** This indicates a completed state. It isn\'t just \"cooling down\"; it is a mind that has **become** cool. The transformation is finished.

**Sītibhūta:** \"One who has become entirely cooled.\"

**37(419). Cutiṃ yo vedi sattānaṃ, upapattiñca sabbaso;\
                Asattaṃ sugataṃ buddhaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who knows entirely (sabbaso) the passing away (cuti) and the arising / rebirth (upapattiñca) of beings (**sattā**); Unattached (asatta), Directed To Wholesomeness (sugata), and buddha (awakened)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**38(420). Yassa gatiṃ na jānanti, devā gandhabbamānusā;\
                Khīṇāsavaṃ arahantaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He whose destination (gati) cannot be perceived by devas, gandhabbas (spirits), and manussa; Who has exhausted (khīṇa) all taints (āsava), an Arahanta---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**39(421). Yassa pure ca pacchā ca, majjhe ca natthi kiñcanaṃ;\
               Akiñcanaṃ anādānaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He for whom there does not exist (natthi) anyting (kiñcana) in the past (pure), the future (pacchā), or present (majjhe, middle); Who possesses nothing (akiñcana) and clings to nothing (anādāna)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**40(422). Usabhaṃ pavaraṃ vīraṃ, mahesiṃ vijitāvinaṃ;\
                Anejaṃ nahātakaṃ buddhaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

The fearless (usabha), the supreme (pavara), the hero (vīra), the great seer (mahesi), the victorious (vijitāvina); The stillness (aneja), the purified (nahātaka), the buddha---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

**41(423). Pubbenivāsaṃ yo vedī, saggāpāyañca passata;\
                Atho jātikkhayaṃ patto, abhiñjāvosito muni;\
                Sabbavositavosānaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ.**

He who knows his past lives (pubbenivāsa), who sees heaven (sagga) and the abyss (apāya); Who has reached the exhaustion of birth (jāti-khaya), a sage (muni) perfected (vosito) by higher knowledge (abhi-ñjā); One who has perfected of all that was to be done. (Sabba-vosita-vosāna)---him I call a Brāhmaṇa.

### Nibbāna-Dhātu: the Realm of Nibbāna 

Gautama the Supreme Arahant reveals one of the most profound truth: there exists an unborn (Ajāta), unArise/unmanifest/unbecome (Abhūta), unmade/manufactured by actions (Akata), and unconditioned (Asaṅkhata) realm (Itv *Ajāta Sutta*). This teaching establishes the existence of Nibbāna-Dhātu---the Realm of Nibbāna---which transcends the entire conditioned world and universe.

*SN 43 Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta*, especially in the 43.44 *Parāyana Sutta*, where the Tathāgata gives thirty-three names for Nibbāna-Dhātu. These names reveal the characteristics of the realm of Nibbāna from many angles so that the citta can understand that Nibbāna is not merely a concept, but a real realm to be realized.

Both *Ajāta Sutta* and the *Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta* present Nibbāna-Dhātu as a real, attainable, and transcendent realm beyond the conditioned world. It is the Deathless Refuge beyond saṃsāra, the complete cessation of dukkha-dhammas, and the final liberation taught by Gautama the Supreme Arahant. Through the cultivation of the Noble Path, the citta can gradually detach from the conditioned world, transcend the bonds of saṃsāra, and finally realize this unborn, unconditioned, and eternal realm of Nibbāna.

In conclusion, Nibbāna is not an empty philosophical concept, but a truly attainable realm of existence. Through understanding the characteristics, we can gain a clearer understanding of the nature of the realm of Nibbāna and, with this as our goal, practice with zeal (Appamada) to finally go to the realm of Nibbāna and attain eternal life and supreme happiness and bliss.

#### Itivuttaka 43 Ajāta sutta (The Unborn)

This was indeed spoken by the Bhagavan (the Blessed One), spoken by the Arahant---so I have heard:

Vuttañhetaṁ bhagavatā vuttam-arahatāti me sutaṁ:

\"There is, bhikkhus, an unborn (Ajāta), an unArise/unmanifest /unbecome (Abhūta), an unmade/ manufactured by actions (Akata), an unconditioned (Asaṅkhata, It means \"not put together.\" Everything in the universe is a **Saṅkhāra**---a construction held together by the \"glue\" of craving. The **Asaṅkhataṁ** has no parts, no \"coordinates,\" and no dependency on causes and effects. It is the only realm that does not \"fall apart\" (*dukkha*), because it was never \"put together.\").

"Atthi, bhikkhave, ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ.

If, bhikkhus, there were not that unborn, unbecome, unmade, unconditioned, then no escape here from the born, become, made, and conditioned would be discerned.

No cetaṁ, bhikkhave, abhavissa ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ, nayidha jātassa bhūtassa katassa saṅkhatassa nissaraṇaṁ paññāyetha.

But because, bhikkhus, there is an unborn, an unbecome, an unmade, an unconditioned, therefore the escape from the born, become, made, and conditioned is discerned.\"

Yasmā ca kho, bhikkhave, atthi ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ, tasmā jātassa bhūtassa katassa saṅkhatassa nissaraṇaṁ paññāyatī"ti.

This matter the Bhagavan spoke. In this regard, it is said thus:

Etamatthaṁ bhagavā avoca. Tatthetaṁ iti vuccati:

\"Born, become, produced, made, conditioned, and unstable; A mass of aging and death, a nest of disease, brittle and perishable.

"Jātaṁ bhūtaṁ samuppannaṁ, kataṁ saṅkhatamaddhuvaṁ; Jarāmaraṇasaṅghāṭaṁ, roganīḷaṁ pabhaṅguraṁ. 

Produced by the \'leader\' of nutriment, it is not fit to be delighted in; The escape from it is Peace---beyond the sphere of speculation, and stable.

Āhāranettippabhavaṁ, nālaṁ tadabhinandituṁ; Tassa nissaraṇaṁ santaṁ, atakkāvacaraṁ dhuvaṁ.

The unborn, the unproduced, the sorrowless, the dustless realm; The cessation of all *dukkha*-phenomena (dhammā), the stilling of formations---this is Bliss.\"

Ajātaṁ asamuppannaṁ, asokaṁ virajaṁ padaṁ; Nirodho dukkha-dhammānaṁ, saṅkhāra-ūpasamo sukho"ti.

#### SN 43.44 Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta, Parāyana Sutta (The Final Destination)

**Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta** in the **Saṃyutta Nikāya, 43.12--43,** introduce every property one by one for the ream of Nibbana, and in the final sutta, titled \"The Final Destination\", acts as a comprehensive summary. Here, the **Tathāgata** provides the full list of synonyms to describe the realm of Nobbana. The sutta follows a repetitive and powerful structure:

\"Bhikkhus, I will teach you the Unconditioned and the path leading to the Unconditioned\... I will teach you the Truth and the path leading to the Truth\... I will teach you the Other Shore\... the Subtle\... the Very Difficult to See\... the Undecaying\... the Permanent\... the Deathless\... the Final Destination.\"

**The 33 Names for Nibbāna-dhatu**

The Pali Canon lists these specific properties to help the **citta** understand that Nibbāna-Dhātu is not merely a concept, but a realm to be realized. Here are the 33 names as summarized in the collection:

1.  

2.  **Asaṅkhata** (Unconditioned)

3.  **Anta** (The End)

4.  **Anāsava** (Taintless)

5.  **Sacca** (Truth)

6.  **Pāra** (The Other Shore)

7.  **Nipuṇa** (Subtle/Perfect)

8.  **Sududdasa** (Difficult to See)

9.  **Ajajjara** (Unaging/Undecaying)

10. **Dhuva** (Permanent)

11. **Apalokita** (Undisintegrating)

12. **Anidassana** (Non-manifestive)

13. **Nippapañca** (Unproliferated)

14. **Santa** (Calmed)

15. **Amata** (Deathless)

16. **Paṇīta** (Sublime)

17. **Siva** (Auspicious)

18. **Khema** (Peaceful/Safe)

19. **Taṇhākkhaya** (Destruction of craving)

20. **Acchariya** (Wonderful)

21. **Abbhuta** (Marvellous)

22. **Anītika** (Free from harm)

23. **Anītikadhamma** (State free from harm)

24. **Nibbāna** (Unbinding)

25. **Abyābajjha** (Unafflicted)

26. **Virāga** (Dispassion)

27. **Suddhi** (Purity)

28. **Mutti** (Liberated)

29. **Anālaya** (Free from attachment)

30. **Dīpa** (Island)

31. **Leṇa** (Shelter)

32. **Tāṇa** (Protection)

33. **Saraṇa** (Refuge)

34. **Parāyana** (Final Destination)

Attaining liberation from the bonds of Saṃsāra (the cycle of rebirth in the world) and reaching the realm of Nibbāna is the core teaching of Gautama the Supreme Arahant. This is true freedom, overcoming the dukkha of old age and death, and attaining eternal life and bliss. It is not nihilism or annihilation, but a real and ultimate existence for us by transcending the world. These 33 characteristics can be categorized as follows:

**I. Transcending Worldly Existence**
